\displaystyle\Theta_{L_{-},{\bm{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau,{\bm{\rho}}_{-})=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{k}}_{-}\in L_{-}+{\bm{\mu}}_{-}}(-1)^{B({\bm{k}}_{-},K_{-})}q^{-{\bm{k}}_{-}^{2}/2}e^{-2\pi iB({\bm{\rho}}_{-},{\bm{k}}_{-})}.

If the elliptic variable
\rho
is zero,
\Psi_{\bm{\mu}}
vanishes unless
{\bm{\mu}}=(\tfrac{1}{2},{\bm{0}})\mod\mathbb{Z}^{b_{2}}

Korpas:2019cwg
. In that case, it evaluates to

\Psi_{(\frac{1}{2},{\bm{0}})}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\frac{i^{K_{1}+1}}{2\sqrt{2y}}\overline{\eta(\tau)^{3}}\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{b_{2}^{-}}.

(78)

We will also need the dual theta series

\displaystyle\Theta_{D,L_{-},{\bm{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau,{\bm{\rho}}_{D,-})=\sum_{{\bm{k}}_{-}\in L_{-}+K_{-}/2}(-1)^{B({\bm{k}}_{-},{\bm{\mu}}_{-})}q^{-{\bm{k}}_{-}^{2}/2}e^{-2\pi iB({\bm{\rho}}_{D,-},{\bm{k}}_{-})}.

(79)

In order to write the integrand as a total anti-holomorphic derivative one can use the theory of mock modular forms and Appell–Lerch sums
Moore:1997pc
;
Malmendier:2012zz
;
Korpas:2017qdo
;
Korpas:2019cwg
;
Manschot:2021qqe
.
An important constraint is that the anti-derivative must be a well-defined function on the fundamental domain for
\tau
, and thus transform appropriately under duality transformations of the theory.

As discussed in Section
9.1
, in the large mass limit the duality group is
\Gamma^{0}(4)
, such that we can use results for the
N_{f}=0
theory
Korpas:2019cwg
. We write
f_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})
as

f_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})=\frac{\partial\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})}{\partial\bar{\tau}},

(80)

with
\widehat{F}_{\mu}
a specialisation of the Appell–Lerch sum
M
and its completion, which we define in Appendix
D.3
.
The holomorphic parts of
\widehat{F}_{\mu}
are given by
(
Korpas:2019cwg
, Eqs.(5.51) and (5.53))

\displaystyle F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,\rho)=

\displaystyle-\frac{w^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}q^{n^{2}/2-\frac{1}{8}}}{1-wq^{n-\frac{1}{2}}},

(81)

\displaystyle F_{0}(\tau,\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}-\frac{i}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}q^{n^{2}/2}}{1-wq^{n}},

where
w=e^{2\pi i\rho}
.

To evaluate the contributions from the strong coupling cusps, we introduce furthermore the “dual” functions
F_{D,\mu}

(
Korpas:2019cwg
, Equations (5.63) and (5.64))
,

F_{D,\mu}(\tau,\rho)=-\frac{w^{1/2}}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{q^{n(n+1)/2}}{1-(-1)^{2\mu}wq^{n}}.

(82)

We note that
F_{\frac{1}{2}}
has a finite limit for
\rho\to 0
,

F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)=\lim_{\rho\to 0}F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,\rho).

(83)

If the subscript
\mu
is clear from the context, we will occasionally drop it and denote
F=F_{\frac{1}{2}}
.
The first terms of the
q
-series are

F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)=2q^{\frac{3}{8}}\left(1+3q^{\frac{1}{2}}+7q+14q^{\frac{3}{2}}+\mathcal{O}(q^{4})\right).

(84)

This
q
-series is proportional to the McKay-Thompson series
H^{(2)}_{1A,2}

Cheng:2012tq
. See also the OEIS sequence A256209.

The duality groups are different for small masses, or other special points in the mass space. For such cases, other anti-derivatives are required. The most widely applicable anti-derivative will transform under
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. As we review in detail in Appendix
D.3
, anti-derivatives are not unique one can add an integration constant, i.e. a weakly holomorphic function of
\tau
. There are in fact three well-known mock modular forms with precisely the same shadow
\sim\eta^{3}
, namely
F
,
\frac{1}{24}H
and
\frac{1}{2}Q^{+}
.
8
8

8

We may view the relation between
H
,
F
and
Q^{+}
as follows. Following
(
zagier2009
, Section 5)
, there is a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow M_{k}^{!}\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}_{k}\overset{\mathscr{S}}{\longrightarrow}M_{2-k},

(85)

where
M_{k}
is the space of all classical modular forms of weight
k
,
M_{k}^{!}
is the larger space of weakly holomorphic modular forms of weight
k
, and
\mathbb{M}_{k}
is the space of mock modular forms of weight
k
. The ‘shadow map’
\mathscr{S}
associates the shadow
\mathscr{S}[h]
to each mock modular form
h\in\mathbb{M}_{k}
. This shadow map is surjective, but clearly not injective, since
\mathscr{S}[F_{\frac{1}{2}}]=\mathscr{S}[\frac{1}{24}H]=\mathscr{S}[\frac{1}{2}Q^{+}]\propto\eta^{3}
. We are thus considering the preimage
\mathscr{S}^{-1}[\eta^{3}]
, which contains
F_{\frac{1}{2}}
,
H
and
Q^{+}
. As we will discuss in more detail in Section
13.3
, their differences can be understood as integration ‘constants’. Roughly speaking, we can understand
\mathscr{S}^{-1}[\Psi]
as the space of anti-derivatives of
\Psi
.

Their completions are non-holomorphic modular functions for
\Gamma^{0}(2)
,
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
and
\Gamma^{0}(2)
, respectively. In
Korpas:2019cwg
it was shown that for
N_{f}=0
either of these three functions can be used for the evaluation of
u
-plane integrals, and they give the same result. This is possible because all three functions transform well under the monodromies on the
u
-plane. For
N_{f}=1
and
N_{f}=3
on the other hand,
F
and
Q^{+}
do not have the right monodromy properties, since they do not transform under
T^{3}
or
T
. This singles out the function
H
, which transforms under all possible monodromies for all
N_{f}
.

The function
H
is related to
F_{\frac{1}{2}}
as

H(\tau)=24\,F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)-2\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}},

(86)

with
F_{\frac{1}{2}}
as above.
This function is well known as the generating function of dimensions of representations of the Mathieu group
Eguchi:2010ej
;
Dabholkar:2012nd
,

H(\tau)=2\,q^{-1/8}\left(-1+45\,q+231\,q^{2}+770\,q^{3}+\mathcal{O}(q^{4})\right),

(87)

and transforms under
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.

Including either surface observables or the coupling to the background fluxes requires an elliptic generalisation, which has to transform under
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
in order to be applicable to
u
-plane integrals with small masses. In Appendix
D.4
, we construct such an
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
mock Jacobi form
H(\tau,\rho)
, and discuss the relation to
F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,\rho)
.
Deriving a similar expression related to
F_{0}
which transforms under
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
is more involved, since
F_{0}(\tau,\rho)
has a pole at
\rho=0
(due to the term
n=0
in the sum (
81
)). We leave it for future work to find such an elliptic generalisation of
F_{0}
. In Appendix
D.3
, we study further properties of the above mock modular forms in great detail, while their elliptic generalisations including zeros and poles are discussed in Appendix
D.4
.

10.3
Constraints for contributions from the cusps

In this subsection, we consider the
u
-plane integrals with vanishing external fluxes,
{\bm{k}}_{j}=0
. We consider the leading behaviour of the integrand near cusps, and determine selection rules for the cusps to have potential nonzero contributions.

Point observables

Let us first assume that the intersection form of
X
is odd. If we turn off the surface observable
{\bm{x}}
, we can evaluate the
u
-plane integral (
) for generic masses. As found above, if
J=(1,\bm{0}_{b_{2}^{-}})
then
\Psi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J}(\tau,0)
vanishes whenever
b_{2}^{-}>0
. This gives the result

\Phi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J,N_{f}}[e^{2pu}]=0,\qquad b_{2}^{-}>0.

(88)

Let us therefore proceed with
b_{2}^{-}=0
, such that
\sigma=1
and
\chi=3
. We calculate
u
-plane integrals for such manifolds in great detail in Section
11
.

In this case, the Siegel-Narain theta function (
78
) becomes

\Psi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=-i^{K_{1}}\frac{-i}{2\sqrt{2y}}\overline{\eta(\tau)^{3}}.

(89)

We can use the fact that
\frac{-i}{2\sqrt{2y}}\overline{\eta(\tau)}^{3}=\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{F}_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
is the shadow of the mock modular form
F\coloneqq F_{\frac{1}{2}}
, defined in (
81
).

As discussed in Section
, the
u
-plane integral can then be expressed as a sum over
q^{0}
-coefficients of the integrand evaluated near the cusps, labelled by
j
,

\Phi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J,N_{f}}[e^{2pu}]=-\frac{i^{K_{1}}}{24}\sum_{j}w_{j}{\rm Coeff}_{q_{j}^{0}}\left[\nu(\alpha_{j}\tau)H(\alpha_{j}\tau)\,e^{2pu(\alpha_{j}\tau)}\right].

(90)

Here,
\alpha_{j}\in\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
give the cosets in the fundamental domain (
), and
w_{j}
is the width of the cusp
j
.

Let us study which cusps contribute to the sum (
90
). We have that
H(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(q^{-\frac{1}{8}})
for
\tau\to i\infty
. One furthermore finds

\displaystyle u(\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(q^{-\frac{1}{4-N_{f}}}),\quad\frac{du}{da}(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(q^{-\frac{1}{2(4-N_{f})}}),

(91)

\displaystyle\frac{da}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(q^{-\frac{1}{2(4-N_{f})}}),\quad\Delta(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(q^{-\frac{2+N_{f}}{4-N_{f}}}).

in the weak coupling frame.
Then the measure factor goes as

\nu(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(q^{-\frac{12+\sigma N_{f}}{8(4-N_{f})}}),

(92)

which holds for generic masses and
b_{2}^{+}=1
. For
\sigma=1
the exponent is
\frac{1}{8}-\frac{2}{4-N_{f}}\leq-\frac{3}{8}
, where equality holds for
N_{f}=0
. Combining with the exponent
-\frac{1}{8}
of
H
, the exponent of the leading term in the
q
-expansion of
\nu Hu^{\ell}
(
90
) is

\nu(\tau)\,H(\tau)\,u(\tau)^{\ell}=\mathcal{O}(q^{-\frac{2+\ell}{4-N_{f}}}).

(93)

Since both
4-N_{f}>0
and
2+\ell>0
, this exponent is strictly negative. We confirm through explicit calculations in Section
11
that indeed for generic masses also the
q^{0}
term is present.
This shows that the cusp
i\infty
generally contributes to
\Phi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J,N_{f}}[e^{2pu}]
to all orders in
p
, for all
N_{f}\leq 3
.

This is not true for the strong coupling cusps,
j\in\mathbb{Q}
. These cusps are in fact simpler to analyse, since the measure
\nu
at strong coupling becomes a constant. In order to see this, recall that
u_{D}(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{D}=\mathcal{O}(1)
(see Section
9.2
for more details). We also have
\Delta_{D}(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(q)
, such that we are left with studying
\frac{da}{d\tau}=\frac{da}{du}\frac{du}{d\tau}
. Near a singularity
u_{j}^{*}
, the local coordinate reads
u_{D}(\tau)-u_{j}^{*}=\mathcal{O}(q^{\frac{1}{n}})
, where
n
is the width of the cusp
j
(corresponding to an
I_{n}
singularity). For asymptotically free SQCD, the possibilities are
n=1,2,3,4
.
Therefore, we have that
\left(\frac{du}{d\tau}\right)_{D}=\mathcal{O}(q^{\frac{1}{n}})
and thus

\nu_{D}(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(q^{\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\sigma}{8}}).

(94)

Since
H
is mock modular for
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, also
H_{D}(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(q^{-\frac{1}{8}})
. Thus we find that the lowest
q
-exponent of the contribution to an
I_{n}
cusp is

\nu_{D}(\tau)\,H_{D}(\tau)\,u_{D}(\tau)^{\ell}=\mathcal{O}(q^{\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\sigma}{8}-\frac{1}{8}}).

(95)

For our choice of period point
J=(1,\bm{0}_{b_{2}^{-}})
, we can set
\sigma=1
. Then the leading exponent is
\frac{1}{n}>0
, such that the
q^{0}
coefficient vanishes. Thus the for manifolds with
\sigma=1
the strong coupling cusps never contribute to correlation functions
\Phi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J,N_{f}}[e^{2pu}]
of the point observable.

The correlation functions for the point observable on manifolds with odd intersection form then receives contributions only from weak coupling. Since the width of the cusp at infinity is
w_{i\infty}=4-N_{f}
, we can simplify (
90
) substantially,

\Phi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J,N_{f}}[e^{2pu}]=-\frac{i^{K_{1}}(4-N_{f})}{24}\,{\rm Coeff}_{q^{0}}\left[\nu(\tau)\,H(\tau)\,e^{2pu(\tau)}\right].

(96)

In
Korpas:2019cwg
it is observed that for
N_{f}=0
, correlation functions of point observables are (up to an overall dependence on the canonical class) universal for any four-manifold with odd intersection form and given period point
J
. The reason for this is that the topological dependence of the measure factor
\nu\sim\vartheta_{4}^{-b_{2}}
cancels precisely with the holomorphic part of the Siegel-Narain theta function
\Psi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J}\supset\vartheta_{4}^{b_{2}}
. This is not true for
N_{f}>0
, which one may also see by comparing (
88
) with (
96
).

Surface observables

We can also consider correlation functions of surface observables
{\bm{x}}\in H_{2}(M)
supported on the compact four-manifold
X
. Following Section
10.2
,
\Psi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J}
for the choice
J=(1,\bm{0}_{b_{2}^{-}})
factorises as

\Psi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J}(\tau,{\bm{\rho}})=-i^{K_{1}}f_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,\rho_{1})\Theta_{L_{-},{\bm{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau,{\bm{\rho}}_{-}),

(97)

with
{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{+},{\bm{\mu}}_{-})\equiv(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\dots,\frac{1}{2})\mod\mathbb{Z}^{b_{2}}
. Due to (
77
), we have that

\Theta_{L_{-},{\bm{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau,{\bm{\rho}}_{-})=\prod_{k=2}^{b_{2}}-i^{K_{k}+1}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,\rho_{k}),

(98)

where
{\bm{\rho}}_{-}=(\rho_{2},\cdots,\rho_{b_{2}})
. The function
f_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,\rho)
is the shadow of the mock modular form
\tfrac{1}{24}H(\tau,\rho)
, as in (
286
). This allows to evaluate (
), where we also include the point observable,

\begin{split}&\Phi_{\frac{K}{2}}^{J,N_{f}}[e^{I_{-}({\bm{x}})}]=\frac{-i^{K_{1}}}{24}\!\!\sum_{j}w_{j}\\
&\qquad\times{\rm Coeff}_{q_{j}^{0}}\left[\nu(\tau_{j})e^{2pu(\tau_{j})/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}+{\bm{x}}^{2}G(\tau_{j})}H(\tau,\rho_{1,j})\prod_{k=2}^{b_{2}}(-i^{K_{k}+1})\vartheta_{1}(\tau_{j},\rho_{k,j})\right],\end{split}

(99)

where we calculate the local
q_{j}
series around the cusps
j
and extract the constant term.
Let us check that the case
{\bm{x}}=0
is consistent with the previous result. Consider thus that
{\bm{\rho}}=0
in above formula. If
b_{2}^{-}>0
and consequently
b_{2}\geq 2
, then all factors in the product vanish, since
\vartheta_{1}(\tau,0)\equiv 0
. This reproduces (
88
). If
b_{2}^{-}=0
on the other hand, then the product is over an empty set and therefore equal to
1
. By construction
H(\tau,0)=H(\tau)
, and the limit to (
90
) is obvious.

When do strong coupling cusps contribute?

In (
95
) it was found that the contribution of strong coupling cusps to the
u
-plane integral depends on an intricate way on the four-manifold
X
and on the type of cusp. For instance, let
u_{j}^{*}
be an
I_{n}
singularity, such that the local expansion reads
u_{D}(\tau)=u_{j}^{*}+\mathcal{O}(q^{\frac{1}{n}})
. Then the smallest exponent in the
q
-series of the measure factor
\nu_{D}
whose coefficient is strictly non-zero is
\tfrac{1}{n}+\tfrac{\sigma}{8}
, independent of the mass configuration
{\bm{m}}
giving rise to that
I_{n}
singularity.
Consider now an SQCD mass configuration containing singularities of type
I_{n}
and
I_{m}
which can be merged by colliding some masses. If the signature
\sigma
is such that the smallest exponent of the
q
-series of the integrand is positive, then their individual contributions vanish. However, if the
I_{n}
and
I_{m}
singularities merge to an
I_{n+m}
singularity, the lowest exponent can become non-positive and there can be a contribution to the
u
-plane integral. The simplest example would be two
I_{1}
singularities colliding to an
I_{2}
singularity.

For the complex projective plane
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
this does not occur, since for any
I_{n}
singularity and any
N_{f}
the smallest exponent is strictly positive,
\frac{1}{n}
. This is in agreement with the theorem that for
N_{f}=0
and for four-manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}(X)>0
that admit a Riemannian metric of positive scalar curvature the Seiberg-Witten invariants vanish
Witten:1994cg
;
Taubes1994
.
9
9

9

This is a consequence of the Bochner-Lichnerowicz-Weitzenböck formula, which relates the Dirac operator to the scalar curvature via the connection Laplacian. If a given metric has positive curvature, the kernel of the Dirac operator is empty. See
yanez2023
for an overview.
The theorem has been shown to generalise also to
N_{f}>0

bryan1996
;
Dedushenko:2017tdw
. See
MANTIONE2021566
for a survey on four-manifolds with positive scalar curvature.

To test whether this vanishing theorem also holds for the multi-monopole SW equations, we can calculate
u
-plane integrals for manifolds
X
of small signature that admit metrics with positive scalar curvature.
Such a class of four-manifolds are the del Pezzo surfaces
dP_{n}
. They are blow-ups of the complex projective plane at
n
points, where
n=1,\dots,8
. For
n=9
, it is known as
\frac{1}{2}\text{K3}
. These surfaces have
b_{2}^{+}(dP_{n})=1
and signature
\sigma(dP_{n})=1-n
. The canonical class of
dP_{n}
is
K=-3H+E_{1}+\dots+E_{n}
, with
E_{i}
the exceptional divisors of the blow-up, and
H
the pullback of the hyperplane class from
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. The intersection form can be brought to the form

Q_{dP_{n}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-\mathbbm{1}_{n}\end{pmatrix},

(100)

with
\mathbbm{1}_{n}
the
n\times n
identity matrix. From this it follows that
K_{dP_{n}}^{2}=9-n
, which is the degree of
dP_{n}
.
As explained in Section
10.2
, for manifolds with odd intersection form, the components
K_{j}
of the characteristic vector
K
are odd for all
j=1,\dots,b_{2}
. Without external fluxes
{\bm{k}}_{j}=0
, the
u
-plane integrals are well-defined if
{\bm{\mu}}=\frac{1}{2}K\mod L
. On the other hand, the Siegel-Narain theta function for
J=(1,0,\dots,0)
vanishes identically unless
{\bm{\mu}}=(\tfrac{1}{2},0,\dots,0)\mod\mathbb{Z}^{b_{2}}
. This shows that without surface observables and without external fluxes, the
u
-plane integrals necessarily vanish for the del Pezzo surfaces
dP_{n}
with
n\geq 1
.

If we include surface observables, the
\theta_{1}(\tau,\rho_{k})
in Eq. (
99
) transform to
\theta_{1}(\tau_{j},\rho_{k,j})
with the leading term in the
q_{j}
expansion of
\rho_{k,j}
a non-vanishing constant (where we use
\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{D}=\mathcal{O}(1)
and the
S
-transformation (
248
)). The leading term of
\vartheta_{1}
is
\theta_{1}(\tau_{j},\rho_{k,j})\sim q_{j}^{1/8}
, such that the product over
b_{2}-1
of these gives
q_{j}^{-(\sigma-1)/8}
. As a result, the
\sigma
dependence of the measure is cancelled by
\Psi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J}
, and the local asymptotics is
\mathcal{O}(q^{\frac{1}{n}})
for any
\sigma
. We conclude that the strong coupling cusps do not contribute after inclusion of surface observables. It would be interesting to explore if non-vanishing background fluxes affect this conclusion.

10.4
Wall-crossing

An intrinsic feature of
u
-plane integrals for
b_{2}^{+}=1
is the metric dependence and the wall-crossing associated with it.
The metric dependence of the Lagrangian is encoded in the period point
J\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})
, which generates the space
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})^{+}
of self-dual two-cohomology classes and is normalised as
Q(J)=1
. It depends on the metric through the self-duality condition
*J=J
. Using a period point
J
, we can project some vector
{\bm{k}}\in L
to the positive and negative subspaces
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})^{\pm}
using
{\bm{k}}_{+}=B({\bm{k}},J)J
and
{\bm{k}}_{-}={\bm{k}}-{\bm{k}}_{+}
.

Even when including the background fluxes, the dependence of the
u
-plane integrand on the metric is only through the Siegel-Narain theta function
\Psi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J}
. The metric dependence is then captured through the difference
\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J}-\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J^{\prime}}
for two period points
J
and
J^{\prime}
, which we aim to evaluate. To this end, we note that the difference

\Psi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,{\bm{z}})-\Psi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J^{\prime}}(\tau,{\bm{z}})=\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{\Theta}_{\bm{\mu}}^{J,J^{\prime}}(\tau,{\bm{z}})

(101)

is a total derivative to
\bar{\tau}
, with

\begin{split}\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{{\bm{\mu}}}\!(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\bm{z}},\bar{\bm{z}})&=\sum_{{\bm{k}}\in L+{\bm{\mu}}}\tfrac{1}{2}\left(E(\sqrt{2y}\,B({\bm{k}}+{\bm{b}},J))-\mathrm{sgn}(B({\bm{k}},J^{\prime}))\right)\\
&\quad\times e^{\pi iB({\bm{k}},K)}q^{-{\bm{k}}^{2}/2}e^{-2\pi iB({\bm{k}},{\bm{z}})}\end{split}

(102)

and

E(u)=2\int_{0}^{u}e^{-\pi t^{2}}dt=\text{Erf}(\sqrt{\pi}u)

(103)

a rescaled error function
E:\mathbb{R}\to(-1,1)
. We have under the
S
- and
T
-transformations,

\begin{split}&\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{{\bm{\mu}}}(-1/\tau,-1/\bar{\tau},{\bm{z}}/\tau,\bar{\bm{z}}/\bar{\tau})=i(-i\tau)^{b_{2}/2}\exp(-\pi i{\bm{z}}^{2}/\tau)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times e^{\pi iB({\bm{\mu}},K)}\,\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{K/2}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\bm{z}}-{\bm{\mu}}+K/2,\bar{\bm{z}}-{\bm{\mu}}+K/2),\\
&\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{{\bm{\mu}}}(\tau+1,\bar{\tau}+1,{\bm{z}},\bar{\bm{z}})=e^{\pi i({\bm{\mu}}^{2}-B(K,{\bm{\mu}}))}\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{{\bm{\mu}}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\bm{z}}+{\bm{\mu}}-K/2,\bar{\bm{z}}+{\bm{\mu}}-K/2).\end{split}

(104)

Since the couplings to the background fluxes are holomorphic, the inclusion of the latter is not affected by a total
\bar{\tau}
derivative. This allows to express
\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J}-\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J^{\prime}}
as an integral of the form
\mathcal{I}_{f}
(defined in (
)) for some function
f
satisfying
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{h}=y^{-s}f
, where we can read off

\widehat{h}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\nu(\tau;\{{\bm{k}}_{j}\})\,\widehat{\Theta}_{\bm{\mu}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\bm{z}},\bar{\bm{z}})\,e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}+{\bm{x}}^{2}G_{N_{f}}}.

(105)

Then, according to (
), we can write

\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J}-\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J^{\prime}}=-\int_{\partial\mathcal{F}({\bm{m}})}d\tau\,\widehat{h}(\tau,\bar{\tau}),

(106)

which may be evaluated using the methods in Section
. In particular, it can be evaluated using the indefinite theta function
\Theta_{\bm{\mu}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
, which is the holomorphic part of
\widehat{\Theta}_{\bm{\mu}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
. The contribution from the singularity at infinity is

\begin{split}\left[\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J}-\Phi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J^{\prime}}\right]_{\infty}&=\mathcal{K}_{N_{f}}\int_{-1/2+iY}^{1/2+iY}d\tau\,\frac{da}{d\tau}A^{\chi}B^{\sigma}\prod_{j,k=1}^{N_{f}}C_{ij}^{B({\bm{k}}_{i},{\bm{k}}_{j})}\\
&\quad\times\widehat{\Theta}_{\bm{\mu}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\sum_{j}{\bm{k}}_{j}v_{j},\sum_{j}{\bm{k}}_{j}\bar{v}_{j}),\end{split}

(107)

where we left out the observables. The contributions from the strong coupling singularities follow from modular transformations and will be discussed in Section
12
.

11
Example: four-manifolds with
b_{2}=1

Let us study in detail the
u
-plane integrals (
96
) for the point observable on four-manifolds with
b_{2}=1
.

The complex projective plane
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is the most well-known example. The complex projective plane has
\sigma=1
,
\chi=3
and thus
b_{2}=1
, furthermore
K=K_{1}=3
. With the exact results from
Aspman:2022sfj
;
Korpas:2019cwg
, condensed in Section
10
, it is straightforward to evaluate (
96
) for arbitrary masses.

In this Section, we compute the vev
\Phi_{\frac{1}{2}}^{N_{f}}[u^{\ell}]
for the point observable, which in the notation of
Korpas:2019cwg
is related to the exponentiated observable as

\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}},\{{\bm{k}}_{j}\}}^{N_{f}}[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}]\coloneqq\left\langle e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\mu}},\{{\bm{k}}_{j}\}}^{N_{f}}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\ell!}\left(\frac{2p}{\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\right)^{\ell}\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}},\{{\bm{k}}_{j}\}}^{N_{f}}[u^{\ell}].

(108)

If the background fluxes
{\bm{k}}_{j}=0
on
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
are vanishing, in part I we argued that the theory is consistent only if we restrict to
\bar{w}_{2}(X)\equiv\bar{w}_{2}(E)\mod 2L
. For
N_{f}=1
for instance, we can choose
\mu=1/2
. For this flux, we can also turn on any integral background flux
k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
. If we turn off the ’t Hooft flux
\mu=0
rather, the consistent formulation on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
requires half-integer background fluxes
k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2
.

In the following two subsections, we consider the large mass and small mass calculations for
\Phi_{1/2}^{N_{f}}
with
k_{j}=0
, while in subsection
11.3
we turn on non-vanishing background fluxes
k_{j}
for both
\mu=0
and
\mu=1/2
.

11.1
Large mass expansion with vanishing background fluxes

We first consider the large mass expansion for equal masses
m_{i}\eqqcolon m
for
N_{f}=1,2,3
, in the absence of background fluxes.
This allows to normalise the integral, by requiring that the decoupling limit
m\to\infty
for
\Phi_{1/2}[1]
reproduces the
N_{f}=0
result. We will demonstrate that with this normalisation, the decoupling limit of other observables also matches with
N_{f}=0
as expected. As shown in Section
10.3
, there are no contributions from the strong coupling cusps for
N_{f}\leq 3
and
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
Since the holomorphic part of the integrand is a function of
u
, large
m
expansion of the latter can be determined as described in Section
9.1
.

From (
), we find for the decoupling formula for equal masses

\Lambda_{0}^{4}=m^{N_{f}}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{4-N_{f}}.

(109)

In the large
m
limit, the domain is a truncated
\mathbb{H}/\Gamma^{0}(4)
domain for all
N_{f}
, as discussed in Section
10.1
. Combining the measure factor (
16
) applied to
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
with (
96
) and (
109
), we find in the notation of (
73
),

\begin{split}&\Phi_{1/2}\big[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\big]=(-1)^{N_{f}}\frac{2}{4-N_{f}}\frac{m^{3N_{f}}}{\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{N_{f}}}\frac{1}{\Lambda_{0}^{12}}\\
&\qquad\times{\rm Coeff}_{q^{0}}{\rm Ser}_{m^{-1}}\left[\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{12}\frac{\eta(\tau)^{27}}{P_{N_{f}}^{\text{M}}}\,F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)\,e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\right].\end{split}

(110)

Here, we have used the holomorphic part
F_{\frac{1}{2}}
of the anti-derivative
\widehat{F}_{\frac{1}{2}}
. It is straightforward to check that other choices of anti-derivative, such as
\frac{1}{24}\widehat{H}
give the same result.

We first present the series in a form which makes the decoupling limit manifest. To this end, we list the coefficients of
(p/\Lambda_{1}^{2})^{\ell}
as function of
\Lambda_{0}
and
m
, up to the overall prefactor
(m/\Lambda_{N_{f}})^{N_{f}}
. For
N_{f}=0
, we have

\begin{split}\Phi_{1/2}\big[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\big]&=1+\frac{19}{32}\Lambda_{0}^{4}\frac{p^{2}}{\Lambda_{0}^{4}}+\frac{85}{768}\Lambda_{0}^{8}\frac{p^{4}}{\Lambda_{0}^{8}}+\mathcal{O}(p^{5}).\end{split}

(111)

For
N_{f}=1
, we then find

\begin{split}\Phi_{1/2}\big[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{1}^{2}}\big]&=\frac{m}{\Lambda_{1}}\left(1-\frac{7}{32}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{4}}{m^{2}}\,\frac{p}{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}+\frac{19}{32}\Lambda_{0}^{4}\frac{p^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}^{4}}\right.\\
&\left.-\frac{7}{64}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{8}}{m^{2}}\frac{p^{3}}{\Lambda_{1}^{6}}+\left(\frac{85}{768}\Lambda_{0}^{8}+\frac{1093}{393216}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{12}}{m^{4}}\right)\frac{p^{4}}{\Lambda_{1}^{8}}+\mathcal{O}(p^{5},m^{-8})\right).\end{split}

(112)

For
N_{f}=2
, we find

\begin{split}\Phi_{1/2}\big[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{2}^{2}}\big]&=\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}\left(1+\frac{3}{2^{6}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{4}}{m^{4}}+\left(-\frac{7}{2^{4}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{4}}{m^{2}}\right)\frac{p}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}\right.\\
&+\left(\frac{19}{32}\Lambda_{0}^{4}+\frac{23}{256}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{8}}{m^{4}}+\frac{53}{2^{17}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{12}}{m^{8}}\right)\frac{p^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{4}}\\
&-\left(\frac{7}{32}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{8}}{m^{2}}+\frac{421}{49152}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{12}}{m^{6}}\right)\frac{p^{3}}{\Lambda_{2}^{6}}\\
&+\left(\frac{85}{768}\Lambda_{0}^{8}+\frac{2421}{65536}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{12}}{m^{4}}+\frac{2161}{3145728}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{16}}{m^{8}}\right)\frac{p^{4}}{\Lambda_{2}^{8}}\\
&\left.+\mathcal{O}(p^{5},m^{-8})\right).\end{split}

(113)

Finally, for
N_{f}=3
we find

\begin{split}\Phi_{1/2}\big[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{3}^{2}}\big]&=\frac{m^{3}}{\Lambda_{3}^{3}}\left(1+\frac{9}{2^{6}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{4}}{m^{4}}+\frac{5}{2^{18}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{12}}{m^{12}}\right.\\
&-\left(\frac{21}{2^{5}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{4}}{m^{2}}+\frac{3}{2^{6}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{8}}{m^{6}}\right)\frac{p}{\Lambda_{3}^{2}}\\
&+\left(\frac{19}{2^{5}}\Lambda_{0}^{4}+\frac{69}{2^{8}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{8}}{m^{4}}+\frac{1659}{2^{17}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{12}}{m^{8}}\right)\frac{p^{2}}{\Lambda_{3}^{4}}\\
&-\left(\frac{21}{2^{6}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{8}}{m^{2}}+\frac{3305}{3\times 2^{14}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{12}}{m^{6}}\right)\frac{p^{3}}{\Lambda_{3}^{6}}\\
&+\left(\frac{85}{768}\Lambda_{0}^{8}+\frac{13433}{2^{17}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{12}}{m^{4}}+\frac{13397}{2^{20}}\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{16}}{m^{8}}\right)\frac{p^{4}}{\Lambda_{3}^{8}}\\
&\left.+\mathcal{O}(p^{5},m^{-{9}})\right).\end{split}

(114)

For the
m\to\infty
decoupling limit (
24
), we multiply by the factor
(\Lambda_{0}/\Lambda_{N_{f}})^{\alpha}
with
\alpha=-4
for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Eliminating
\Lambda_{0}
by (
21
), this removes precisely the prefactors in the expressions (
112
), (
113
) and (
114
). We thus find a consistent decoupling limit (
24
) to the
N_{f}=0
result (
111
) for all three cases.

To facilitate comparison of these results with the UV expression (
), we have presented the data in an alternative form in the Tables

\Phi_{1/2}[u^{\ell}]
are listed as functions of

and
\Lambda_{N_{f}}
. The monomial in
m
and
\Lambda_{N_{f}}
is expressed as

\frac{\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{{\rm vdim}(\mathcal{M}^{Q,N_{f}}_{k})}m^{-\sum_{j}{\rm rk}(W^{j}_{k})}}{m^{r}}.

(115)

The exponent of the mass
m
is the virtual rank (
) of the matter bundle, that is, the rank of the obstruction bundle
W_{k}^{j}
. The exponent of the scale
\Lambda_{N_{f}}
can be identified with the (complex) virtual dimension (
) of the moduli space (see also (
)). The exponent
r
is the degree of the Chern class of the matter bundle. For
N_{f}=1
, we have

\begin{split}&{\rm dim}(\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k})=3k-4,\\
&{\rm dim}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k})=4k-3,\\
&{\rm rk}(W_{k})=-k-1.\\
\end{split}

(116)

With
s
the exponent of
p
, the data in the Tables
4
,
5
and
6
satisfy the selection rule (
). Moreover, since the integration is over the instanton moduli space, we have the selection rule
4p+2r={\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\rm i})
.
If the obstruction bundle is a proper bundle rather than a sheaf, we have
r\leq-\sum_{j}{\rm rk}(W^{j}_{k})
. We find that this is the case for all data in these tables. Thus even though the evaluation of the
u
-plane integral was performed in terms of a
1/m
expansion, the results in the tables have a good
m\to 0
limit. We will discuss this in more detail in the following Section
11.2
on the small mass expansion. It is furthermore noteworthy that for fixed
\ell
the coefficients have the same sign. The results of the next few sections show only a few exceptions to this.

From the large powers of 2 in the denominators, we deduce that the normalisation is not precisely consistent with integral Chern classes in the large
m
expansion. We discuss this in more detail in the next subsection, where we give results for generic masses
m_{j}
. Mathematically, these invariants are known as (virtual) Segre numbers of
X

Gottsche:2020ass
. We comment more on this connection in the section
12
on four-manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}>1
.

11.2
Small mass expansion with vanishing background fluxes

For small masses, the integration domains are now naturally the small mass perturbations of the domains for the massless theories. See e.g. Fig.
and
for the massless
N_{f}=1
and
N_{f}=2

10.1
, and the weak coupling cusps have width
4-N_{f}
. For the anti-derivative of
\Psi_{1/2}

\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
mock modular form
H(\tau)
(
86
), which transforms consistently on any of these domains.

With the normalisation determined above, we have for the small mass result

\Phi_{1/2}\big[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\big]=\frac{(-1)^{N_{f}}}{2\,\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{12-2N_{f}}}{\rm Coeff}_{q^{0}}{\rm Ser}_{m}\left[\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{12}\frac{\eta(\tau)^{27}}{P_{N_{f}}^{\text{M}}}\frac{H(\tau)}{24}e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\right].

The negative powers of 2 can also be understood as follows. An insertion of
u^{\ell}
, gives rise to a factor
2^{3\ell}
in the denominator since
2u
corresponds to a 2nd Chern character. Then, the factors of 2 in the tables suggest that the class
c_{l,j}
in (
) is not an integral class, but that
2^{3l/2}c_{l,j}
is. Thus each power of
u
gives rise to a factor of
2^{-3}
, while that of the matter bundle is
2^{-{3\,\rm rk}(W_{k}^{N_{f}})}
,

As a result, we find that in the massless case

2^{d_{N_{f}}(\ell)}\Phi_{\frac{1}{2}}^{N_{f}}\big[(\tfrac{u}{\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}})^{\ell}\big]\in\mathbb{Z},

(121)

where
d_{N_{f}}
is given by
11
11

11

It turns out that
d_{N_{f}}
is not linear or quadratic in
N_{f}
. One formula that fits all four values of
N_{f}
is

d_{N_{f}}(\ell)=3\ell+\frac{N_{f}}{2}(2+\ell)(N_{f}^{2}-2N_{f}+3).

(122)

d_{N_{f}}(\ell)=\begin{cases}3\ell,\quad&N_{f}=0\\
4\ell+2,\quad&N_{f}=1,\\
6\ell+6,\quad&N_{f}=2,\\
12\ell+18,\quad&N_{f}=3.\end{cases}

(123)

In the massive cases, the vevs
\Phi_{\frac{1}{2}}^{N_{f}}[(\tfrac{u}{\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}})^{\ell}]
are dimensionless, and so can only depend on the dimensionless ratios
\mu_{j}=m_{j}/\Lambda_{N_{f}}
. By the above argument, the negative powers of 2 are maximal for the top Chern class. Therefore,

2^{d_{N_{f}}(\ell)}\Phi_{\frac{1}{2}}^{N_{f}}\big[(\tfrac{u}{\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}})^{\ell}\big]\in\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{N_{f}}]

(124)

are valued in the polynomial ring in the masses
\mu_{j}
over the integers, with the same denominators
d_{N_{f}}(\ell)
as in the massless case.

(125)

As discussed in the previous section, a small or generic mass calculation requires an anti-derivative that transforms under
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
rather than under a subgroup. In Appendix
F
, we perform the calculation for
N_{f}=1
and
N_{f}=2
with generic masses, using the mock Jacobi form
H(\tau,\rho)
(
286
) for
\mu=\frac{1}{2}
rather than
F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,\rho)
. For specific choices of the background fluxes, the naive evaluation using this function gives different results depending on the evaluation point: For large masses, the point correlators have a well-defined
m\to\infty
limit, for small masses they have a good massless limit
m\to 0
, while for generic masses, i.e. without expanding in the masses at all, the correlators do not have either of these limits. Possible obstructions for this involve a pole
\tau_{0}
of the anti-derivative
H(\tau,\rho(\tau))
, which is not related to the branch point but rather due to solutions of
v_{j}(\tau_{0})=\frac{1}{2}
inside the fundamental domain. In Appendix
F
, we analyse this issue in some detail and discuss possible resolutions.

12
Contributions from strong coupling singularities

In this section, we analyse the mass dependence of the contributions from strong coupling singularities, or SW contributions, by analysing the wall-crossing of the
u
-plane integral. The general form of these contributions was determined in
Moore:1997pc
, and studied in various cases, for instance for the massless theories on specific manifolds in
Kanno:1998qj
;
Labastida:1995gp
and for generic masses in
Marino:1998uy
;
moore_talk2018
;
Dedushenko:2017tdw
.

The type of a strong coupling singularity is determined by the Kodaira classification of singular fibres (see Table
25
). The monopole and dyon singularities of the pure
N_{f}=0
theory are examples of
I_{1}
singularities.
12
12

12

This viewpoint depends on the global form of the theory, in this case that of pure
\mathcal{N}=2
SYM with gauge algebra
\mathfrak{su}(2)

Argyres:2022kon
. In particular, the SW curves for the various global forms are related by compositions of isogenies, which do not leave the type of
I_{n}
singular fibres invariant
Argyres:2015ffa
;
Closset:2023pmc
. We will comment on this further in the conclusions (Section
14
).
The collision of
n
mutually local
I_{1}
singularities gives rise to an
I_{n}
singularity. If rather
n=2,3,4
mutually non-local singularities collide, we get type
II
,
III
or
IV
Argyres-Douglas points. Both types of collisions can have nontrivial consequences for the partition functions in the limit.

Most of this section will deal with the
I_{1}
SW contributions for generic four-manifolds.
Under various assumptions, we also generalise the arguments to
I_{2}
contributions. For
K3
, we calculate the SW contributions in various examples in
N_{f}=1,2,3
, and study the limit to the AD mass locus in some detail. In Section
12.4
, we relate the contributions from the instanton component in some examples to Segre numbers
Gottsche:2020ass
;
Gottsche:2021ihz
. In Section
12.5
, we discuss the general structure of SW partition functions for arbitrary configurations, extending the notion of
generalised simple type
conditions familiar from the pure
N_{f}=0
case.

12.1
SW contributions of
I_{1}
singularities

Let us first study
I_{1}
singularities. This is the case for generic masses with arbitrary number
N_{f}
of flavours.
The Seiberg-Witten contribution from the strong coupling cusp
j=1,\dots,N_{f}+2
reads
Moore:1997pc
,

Z^{J}_{\text{SW},j,{\bm{\mu}}}=\sum_{c}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{n(c)}\,\text{SW}(c;J)\,\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{a_{j}=0}\,\left[\frac{e^{-S_{SW,j}}}{a_{j}^{1+n(c)}}\right]

(126)

where
n(c)
is the complex dimension of the monopole moduli space

n(c)=\frac{1}{8}(c^{2}-2\chi-3\sigma),

(127)

and the exponentiated action takes the form

e^{-S_{SW,j}}=\kappa_{j}\,\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\chi}\,\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\sigma}\,\mathcal{C}_{j}^{{\bm{k}}^{2}_{1}}\,\mathcal{D}_{j}^{B({\bm{k}}_{1},c)}\,\mathcal{E}_{j}^{c^{2}}\,\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},j},

(128)

where we specialise to
N_{f}=1
for simplicity. The general form for the contribution of the
j
-th singularity with general
N_{f}
contains a product
\prod_{i,k=1}^{N_{f}}\mathcal{C}_{ik,j}^{B({\bm{k}}_{i},{\bm{k}}_{k})}
and similar for
\mathcal{D}_{j}
, as explained in part I.
Here,
\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},j}
is short hand for the four couplings,

\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},j}=f_{1,j}^{{\bm{\mu}}^{2}}f_{2,j}^{B(K,{\bm{\mu}})}f_{3,j}^{B(c,{\bm{\mu}})}f_{4,j}^{B({\bm{k}}_{1},{\bm{\mu}})}.

(129)

For four-manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}=1
, SW-invariants are metric dependent
Witten:1994cg
;
Kronheimer1994
. If
B(c,J^{+})>0
and
B(c,J^{-})<0
, then
li_liu_1995
;
Morgan1995
;
PARK2004

\text{SW}(c;J^{+})-\text{SW}(c;J^{-})=-(-1)^{n(c)},

(130)

The SW invariant furthermore satisfies
Witten:1994cg
;
Morgan1995

\text{SW}(-c)=(-1)^{\chi_{\rm h}}\text{SW}(c),

(131)

with
\chi_{\rm h}=\frac{\chi+\sigma}{4}
the holomorphic Euler characteristic.

Changing the known result
Witten:1994cg
to our convention using (
28
), the result for
N_{f}=0
is

\begin{split}Z_{\bm{\mu}}[e^{2pu}]&=-2^{1-\chi_{\rm h}+\lambda}e^{\pi i\lambda/2}\sum_{c}\text{SW}(c)\,(-1)^{B(c-K,{\bm{\mu}})}\\
&\quad\times\left(e^{\pi i\chi_{\rm h}/2}e^{2pu_{1}^{*}}+e^{2\pi i{\bm{\mu}}^{2}}e^{2pu_{2}^{*}}\right)\end{split}

(132)

with
\lambda=2\chi+3\sigma
.

For instance for
X=K3
, we have
\sigma=-16
,
\chi=24
,
\chi_{h}=2
,
K=0
and thus
\lambda=2\chi+3\sigma=0
. Furthermore,
{\rm SW}(c)=1
for
c=0
, and vanishes otherwise.
The result for
K3
and
N_{f}=0
is then
OGrady1992DonaldsonsPF
;
Witten:1994ev

Z_{{\bm{\mu}}}[e^{2pu}]=\frac{1}{2}\left(e^{2pu_{1}^{*}}-e^{2\pi i{\bm{\mu}}^{2}}e^{2pu_{2}^{*}}\right).

(133)

From the relation in Section
10.4
between the wall-crossing of the
u
-plane integral and SW contributions, we have for the
j
-th singularity,

\begin{split}\left[\Phi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J^{+}}-\Phi^{J^{-}}_{\bm{\mu}}\right]_{j}&=\kappa_{j}\sum_{c}(-1)^{n(c)}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sgn}(B(c,J^{+}))-\mathrm{sgn}(B(c,J^{-})))\\
&\times\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{n(c)}\,\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{a_{j}=0}\left[a_{j}^{-1-n(c)}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{\chi}\,\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\sigma}\,\mathcal{C}_{j}^{{\bm{k}}^{2}_{1}}\,\mathcal{D}_{j}^{B({\bm{k}}_{1},c)}\,\mathcal{E}_{j}^{c^{2}}\,\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},j}\right].\end{split}

(134)

We substitute
\chi=4-\sigma
in the right hand side, such that

\begin{split}&\left[\Phi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J^{+}}-\Phi^{J^{-}}_{\bm{\mu}}\right]_{j}=-\kappa_{j}\sum_{c}(-1)^{(c^{2}-\sigma)/8}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sgn}(B(c,J^{+}))-\mathrm{sgn}(B(c,J^{-})))\\
&\times\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{(c^{2}-\sigma)/8-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{a_{j}=0}\left[a_{j}^{-(c^{2}-\sigma)/8}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{4}\,(\mathcal{B}_{j}/\mathcal{A}_{j})^{\sigma}\,\mathcal{C}_{j}^{{\bm{k}}^{2}_{1}}\,\mathcal{D}_{j}^{B({\bm{k}}_{1},c)}\,\mathcal{E}_{j}^{c^{2}}\,\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},j}\right].\end{split}

(135)

Contribution from monopole cusp
u_{1}^{*}

The left hand side is the contribution from the
u
-plane integral for
j=1
, which is obtained using the substitution
\tau=-1/\tau_{1}
. We introduce

\begin{split}&A_{1}(\tau_{1})=\tau_{1}^{1/2}\,A(-1/\tau_{1}),\\
&\quad\quad=2^{3/4}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-1/2}s_{1}^{-1/4}\,(27g_{3}(u_{1}^{*}))^{1/12}+\dots,\\
&B_{1}(\tau_{1})=B(-1/\tau_{1}),\\
&C_{1}(\tau_{1})=e^{-\pi iv_{1}^{2}/\tau_{1}}\,C(-1/\tau_{1}),\\
&\left(\frac{da}{d\tau}\right)_{1}(\tau_{1})=\tau_{1}^{-3}\left(\frac{da}{d\tau}\right)(-1/\tau_{1}),\\
&v_{1}(\tau_{1})=\tau_{1}\,v(-1/\tau_{1}).\end{split}

(136)

The substitution brings the analogue of the integral (
107
) near singularity
j=1
to the form

\begin{split}\left[\Phi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J^{+}}-\Phi^{J^{-}}_{\bm{\mu}}\right]_{1}&=-e^{\pi i\sigma/4}\mathcal{K}_{N_{f}}\lim_{Y\to\infty}\int_{-1/2+iY}^{1/2+iY}d\tau_{j}\left(\frac{da}{d\tau}\right)_{1}A_{1}^{\chi}B_{1}^{\sigma}C_{1}^{{\bm{k}}_{1}^{2}}\,e^{\pi iB({\bm{\mu}},K)}\\
&\times\sum_{{\bm{k}}\in L+K/2}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sgn}(B(c+{\bm{b}}_{1},J^{+}))-\mathrm{sgn}(B(c+2{\bm{b}}_{1},J^{-})))\\
&\times e^{\pi iB({\bm{k}},K)}\,q_{1}^{-{\bm{k}}^{2}/2}e^{-2\pi iB({\bm{k}},{\bm{k}}_{1}v_{1})}e^{-2\pi iB({\bm{k}},K/2-{\bm{\mu}})}\\
&=-2\pi i\,e^{\pi i\sigma/4}\,\mathcal{K}_{N_{f}}\\
&\times\sum_{c}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sgn}(B(c+2{\bm{b}}_{1},J^{+}))-\mathrm{sgn}(B(c+2{\bm{b}}_{1},J^{-})))\\
&\times\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{a_{1}=0}\left[A_{1}^{4}(B_{1}/A_{1})^{\sigma}C_{1}^{{\bm{k}}_{1}^{2}}\,q_{1}^{-c^{2}/8}e^{-\pi iB(c,{\bm{k}}_{1}v_{1})}e^{\pi iB(c+K,{\bm{\mu}})}\right]\end{split}

(137)

where we substituted
c=2{\bm{k}}
and
{\bm{b}}_{1}={\bm{k}}_{1}\,{\rm Im}(v_{1})
. We assume that
v_{1}=\mathcal{O}(q_{1})
for
\tau_{1}\to 0
. We hope to check this in the future.

Comparing (
135
) and (
137
), we can read off the couplings for singularity
j=1
,

\begin{split}&\kappa_{1}\mathcal{A}_{1}^{4}/\Lambda_{N_{f}}=2\pi i\,\mathcal{K}_{N_{f}}A_{1}^{4},\\
&e^{-\pi i/8}(a_{1}/\Lambda_{N_{f}})^{1/8}\mathcal{B}_{1}/\mathcal{A}_{1}=e^{\pi i/4}B_{1}/A_{1},\\
&\mathcal{C}_{1}=C_{1},\\
&\mathcal{D}_{1}=e^{-\pi iv_{1}},\\
&(-a_{1}/\Lambda_{N_{f}})^{-1/8}\mathcal{E}_{1}=q_{1}^{-1/8},\\
&\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},1}=e^{\pi iB(c+K,{\bm{\mu}})}.\end{split}

(138)

In the large mass expansion, the leading behavior for
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
for
N_{f}=1
can be determined from (
33
) and (
35
) by letting
\tau\to 0
. For the other couplings, we substitute
(
14
),

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{1}=2^{1/2}e^{\pi i/8}\kappa_{1}^{-1/4}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-1/2}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{1}^{1/2}\\
&\quad=2\,e^{\pi i/8}\kappa_{1}^{-1/4}s_{1}^{-1/4}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-1/2}(27g_{3}(u_{1}^{*}))^{1/12},\\
&\mathcal{B}_{1}=2^{1/4}e^{\pi i/2}\kappa_{1}^{-1/4}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{1/8}\frac{B_{1}}{a_{1}^{1/8}}\\
&\quad=2^{5/4}e^{\pi i(N_{f}+5)/8}\kappa_{1}^{-1/4}s_{1}^{-3/16}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-(2N_{f}+3)/8}\\
&\qquad\times\left((4-N_{f})P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{1}^{*})\,(27g_{3}(u_{1}^{*}))^{1/2}\right)^{1/8},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{1}=\left((-1)^{N_{f}}s_{1}^{-3/2}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2N_{f}-9}\frac{64}{4-N_{f}}\frac{\left(27g_{3}(u_{1}^{*})\right)^{3/2}}{P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{1}^{*})}\right)^{1/8}.\end{split}

(139)

Combining all the factors for
{\bm{k}}_{1}=0
, we find assuming the simple type condition,

\begin{split}Z_{\text{SW},1,{\bm{\mu}}}&=\kappa_{1}^{1-\chi_{\rm h}}2^{2\chi_{h}+\lambda}e^{\frac{\pi i}{2}(\chi_{\rm h}+\lambda)}(-1)^{N_{f}\chi_{\rm h}}s_{1}^{-3\chi_{\rm h}/2-\lambda/8}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{(2N_{f}-3)\chi_{\rm h}-\lambda}\\
&\quad\times\left((4-N_{f})P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{1}^{*})\right)^{-\chi_{\rm h}}(27g_{3}(u_{1}^{*}))^{\chi_{\rm h}/2+\lambda/6}\\
&\quad\times\sum_{c}\text{SW}(c)\,e^{\pi iB(c+K,{\bm{\mu}})}.\end{split}

(140)

In order to fix
\kappa_{1}
, we apply this to
X=K3
with
{\bm{k}}_{1}=0
,

Z_{\text{SW},1,{\bm{\mu}}}=-\frac{2^{4}}{\kappa_{1}}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-6+4N_{f}}(4-N_{f})^{-2}\,(P^{\rm M}_{N_{f}}(u_{1}^{*}))^{-2}\,27g_{3}(u_{1}^{*}),

(141)

where we used that
s_{1}
is a third root of unity. Comparing with (
133
) for
N_{f}=0
, we then find
\kappa_{1}=-2
. This holds for generic
N_{f}
since this number is independent of the masses.
Substitution in (
140
) and using (
131
) and
s_{1}=1
gives

\boxed{\begin{aligned} Z_{\text{SW},1,{\bm{\mu}}}&=-2^{1+\chi_{h}+\lambda}e^{\frac{\pi i}{2}(\chi_{\rm h}+\lambda)}(-1)^{N_{f}\chi_{\rm h}}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{(2N_{f}-3)\chi_{\rm h}-\lambda}C_{1}^{{\bm{k}}_{1}^{2}}\\
&\quad\times\left((4-N_{f})P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{1}^{*})\right)^{-\chi_{\rm h}}(27g_{3}(u_{1}^{*}))^{\chi_{\rm h}/2+\lambda/6}\\
&\quad\times\sum_{c}\text{SW}(c)\,e^{\pi iB(c-K,{\bm{\mu}})}e^{-\pi iv_{1}B({\bm{k}}_{1},c)}.\end{aligned}}

(142)

This agrees for
N_{f}=0
, with the well-known SW contribution at the monopole cusp
(
Witten:1994cg
, eq. (2.17))
. Using (
57
) for
n=1
, this matches for any
N_{f}
with
Moore:1997pc
;
Marino:1998uy
.

Contribution from dyon cusp
u_{2}^{*}

Let us also consider the contributions from the other cusps.
The local coupling
\tau_{2}
reads in terms of the effective coupling
\tau=2-1/\tau_{2}
, such that
\tau\to 2
for
\tau_{2}\to i\infty
. We introduce the following couplings

\begin{split}&A_{2}(\tau_{2})=\tau_{2}^{1/2}\,A(2-1/\tau_{2}),\\
&\quad=2^{3/4}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-1/2}\,s_{2}^{-1/4}\,(27g_{3}(u_{2}^{*}))^{1/12}+\dots,\\
&B_{2}(\tau_{2})=B(2-1/\tau_{2}),\\
&C_{2}(\tau_{2})=e^{-\pi iv_{2}^{2}/\tau_{2}}\,C(2-1/\tau_{2}),\\
&\left(\frac{da}{d\tau}\right)_{2}(\tau_{2})=\tau_{2}^{-3}\left(\frac{da}{d\tau}\right)(2-1/\tau_{2}),\\
&v_{2}(\tau_{2})=\tau_{2}\,v(-1/\tau_{2}).\end{split}

(143)

Then we find from the
u
-plane for wall-crossing

\begin{split}\left[\Phi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J^{+}}-\Phi^{J^{-}}_{\bm{\mu}}\right]_{2}&=-2\pi i\,e^{-2\pi i{\bm{\mu}}^{2}}\,e^{\pi i\sigma/4}\,\mathcal{K}_{N_{f}}\\
&\times\sum_{c}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sgn}(B(c+2{\bm{b}}_{1},J^{+}))-\mathrm{sgn}(B(c+2{\bm{b}}_{1},J^{-})))\\
&\times\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{a_{2}=0}\left[A_{2}^{4}(B_{2}/A_{2})^{\sigma}C_{2}^{{\bm{k}}_{1}^{2}}\,q_{2}^{-c^{2}/8}e^{-\pi iB(c,{\bm{k}}_{1}v_{2})}e^{\pi iB(c+K,{\bm{\mu}})}\right].\end{split}

(144)

Following the same steps as for the monopole cusp, we find for the various couplings

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{2}=2\,e^{\pi i/8}\kappa_{2}^{-1/4}s_{2}^{-1/4}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-1/2}(27g_{3}(u_{2}^{*}))^{1/12},\\
&\mathcal{B}_{2}=2^{5/4}e^{\pi i(N_{f}+5)/8}\kappa_{2}^{-1/4}s_{2}^{-3/16}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-(2N_{f}+3)/8}\\
&\qquad\times\left((4-N_{f})P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{2}^{*})\,(27g_{3}(u_{2}^{*}))^{1/2}\right)^{1/8},\\
&\mathcal{C}_{2}=C_{2},\\
&\mathcal{D}_{2}=e^{-\pi iv_{2}}\\
&\mathcal{E}_{2}=\left((-1)^{N_{f}}s_{2}^{-3/2}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2N_{f}-9}\frac{64}{4-N_{f}}\frac{\left(27g_{3}(u_{2}^{*})\right)^{3/2}}{P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{2}^{*})}\right)^{1/8},\\
&\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},2}=e^{\pi iB(c+K,{\bm{\mu}})-2\pi i{\bm{\mu}}^{2}}.\end{split}

(145)

This then gives for
{\bm{k}}_{1}=0
(which requires
{\bm{\mu}}=K/2
for
N_{f}=1
),

\begin{split}Z_{\text{SW},2,{\bm{\mu}}}&=\kappa_{2}^{1-\chi_{\rm h}}2^{2\chi_{h}+\lambda}e^{\frac{\pi i}{2}(\chi_{\rm h}+\lambda)}(-1)^{N_{f}\chi_{\rm h}}s_{2}^{-3\chi_{\rm h}/2-\lambda/8}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{(2N_{f}-3)\chi_{\rm h}-\lambda}\\
&\quad\times\left((4-N_{f})P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{2}^{*})\right)^{-\chi_{\rm h}}(27g_{3}(u_{2}^{*}))^{\chi_{\rm h}/2+\lambda/6}\\
&\quad\times\sum_{c}\text{SW}(c)\,e^{\pi iB(c+K,{\bm{\mu}})-2\pi i{\bm{\mu}}^{2}}.\end{split}

(146)

Applying this to
X=K3
, we find

Z_{\text{SW},2,{\bm{\mu}}}=-\frac{2^{4}}{\kappa_{2}}e^{-2\pi i{\bm{\mu}}^{2}}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-6+4N_{f}}(4-N_{f})^{-2}\,(P^{\rm M}_{N_{f}}(u_{2}^{*}))^{-2}\,27g_{3}(u_{2}^{*}).

(147)

For
N_{f}=0
, we have
27g_{3}(u_{2}^{*})=-1=e^{\pi i}
. With
s_{2}=e^{4\pi i/3}
(see Tables
3
and
3
),
we thus arrive at
\kappa_{2}=-2
. The general contribution from this cusp is

\boxed{\begin{aligned} Z_{\text{SW},2,{\bm{\mu}}}&=-2^{1+\chi_{h}+\lambda}e^{\frac{\pi i}{2}(\chi_{\rm h}+\lambda)}(-1)^{N_{f}\chi_{\rm h}}s_{2}^{-3\chi_{\rm h}/2-\lambda/8}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{(2N_{f}-3)\chi_{\rm h}-\lambda}C_{2}^{{\bm{k}}_{1}^{2}}\\
&\quad\times\left((4-N_{f})P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{2}^{*})\right)^{-\chi_{\rm h}}(27g_{3}(u_{2}^{*}))^{\chi_{\rm h}/2+\lambda/6}\\
&\quad\times\sum_{c}\text{SW}(c)\,e^{\pi iB(c-K,{\bm{\mu}})-2\pi i{\bm{\mu}}^{2}}e^{-\pi iv_{2}B({\bm{k}}_{1},c)}.\end{aligned}}

(148)

Contribution from hypermultiplet cusp
u_{3}^{*}

We will continue with the contribution from a singularity associated to one of the fundamental hypermultiplets. We label this singularity as
u_{3}^{*}
, and we assume that this singularity is approached for
\tau\to 1\in\mathbb{Q}
. This is case for the hypermultiplet singularity in the
N_{f}=1
theory. The contribution from hypermultiplet singularities in other theories, possibly at other points in
\mathbb{Q}
, can be determined similarly.

The coupling
\tau
then reads
\tau=1-1/\tau_{3}
in terms of the local coupling
\tau_{3}
near the singularity
j=3
. For simplicity, we fix
{\bm{\mu}}=K/2
such that we can choose vanishing background fluxes for the flavour symmetry. Then the wall-crossing of the
u
-plane integral at
u_{3}
reads

\begin{split}\left[\Phi_{K/2}^{J^{+}}-\Phi^{J^{-}}_{K/2}\right]_{3}&=-2\pi i\,e^{-\pi i\sigma/2}\,\mathcal{K}_{N_{f}}\\
&\times\sum_{c}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sgn}(B(c+2{\bm{b}}_{1},J^{+}))-\mathrm{sgn}(B(c+2{\bm{b}}_{1},J^{-})))\\
&\times\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{a_{3}=0}\left[A_{3}^{4}(B_{3}/A_{3})^{\sigma}C_{3}^{{\bm{k}}_{1}^{2}}\,q_{3}^{-c^{2}/8}e^{-\pi iB(c,{\bm{k}}_{1}v_{3})}e^{\pi iB(c+K,K/2)}\right],\end{split}

(149)

where we used that
K^{2}=\sigma\mod 8
.

We then find for the various couplings

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{3}=2\,e^{\pi i/8}\kappa_{3}^{-1/4}s_{3}^{-1/4}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-1/2}(27g_{3}(u_{3}^{*}))^{1/12},\\
&\mathcal{B}_{3}=2^{5/4}e^{\pi i(N_{f}+3)/8}\kappa_{3}^{-1/4}s_{3}^{-3/16}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-(2N_{f}+3)/8}\\
&\qquad\times\left((4-N_{f})P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{3}^{*})\,(27g_{3}(u_{3}^{*}))^{1/2}\right)^{1/8},\\
&\mathcal{C}_{3}=C_{3},\\
&\mathcal{D}_{3}=e^{-\pi iv_{3}},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{3}=\left((-1)^{N_{f}}s_{3}^{-3/2}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2N_{f}-9}\frac{64}{4-N_{f}}\frac{\left(27g_{3}(u_{3}^{*})\right)^{3/2}}{P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{3}^{*})}\right)^{1/8},\\
&\mathcal{F}_{K/2,3}=e^{\pi iB(c+K,K/2)}.\end{split}

(150)

We assume that the normalization
\kappa_{3}
equals
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=-2
. We then arrive for the general expression at

\boxed{\begin{aligned} Z_{\text{SW},3,K/2}&=-2^{1+\chi_{h}+\lambda}e^{\frac{\pi i}{2}(\chi_{\rm h}-\lambda)}(-1)^{N_{f}\chi_{\rm h}}s_{3}^{-3\chi_{\rm h}/2-\lambda/8}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{(2N_{f}-3)\chi_{\rm h}-\lambda}C_{3}^{{\bm{k}}_{1}^{2}}\\
&\quad\times\left((4-N_{f})P_{N_{f}}^{\rm M}(u_{3}^{*})\right)^{-\chi_{\rm h}}(27g_{3}(u_{3}^{*}))^{\chi_{\rm h}/2+\lambda/6}\\
&\quad\times\sum_{c}\text{SW}(c)\,e^{\pi iB(c,K/2)}e^{-\pi iv_{3}B({\bm{k}}_{1},c)}.\end{aligned}}

(151)

We have thus derived the SW contributions (
142
), (
148
), (
151
) for singularities at the cusps
\tau=0,2,1
, as is the case for instance in
N_{f}=1
with a generic mass.

12.2
SW contributions of
I_{2}
singularities

In the SW curves for
N_{f}\geq 2
,
I_{r}
singularities with
1<r\leq 2N_{f}-2
can occur for special values of the masses. Indeed, as shown in (
), such higher
I_{r}
singularities occur precisely if some of the
N_{f}
masses (anti-)align. In such cases, there is a singular point on the Coulomb branch where
r
mutually local dyons become massless. The ‘maximal’
I_{2N_{f}-2}
singularity occurs precisely in the massless case.
13
13

13

For
N_{f}=4
, in the massless limit the six singularities collide in an
I_{0}^{*}
singularity, rather than an
I_{6}
.

Let us consider such a configuration with an
I_{r>1}
singularity. The (complex) virtual dimension of the
r
-monopole equation reads
bryan1996

n_{r}(c)=\frac{r(c^{2}-\sigma)-2(\chi+\sigma)}{8}.

(152)

For
r>1
, the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{r}
are non-compact. There is a global symmetry
SU(r+1)
which acts on the moduli space of
r
-monopole equations. The non-compactness can be mitigated by studying the equivariant cohomology of
\mathcal{M}_{r}
with respect to the
SU(r+1)
action
LoNeSha
. This corresponds to deforming the
I_{r}
singularities to
I_{1}
singularities by making the masses generic, for which the spaces
\mathcal{M}_{1}
are compact.

We will proceed quite heuristically in the following by
assuming
the existence of well-defined numerical Seiberg-Witten invariants
\text{SW}_{r}
for
I_{r}
singularities, and a wall-crossing formula for these invariants similar to (
130
). We will then show for K3 that smoothness of the partition function as function of the mass implies sum rules for
\text{SW}_{2}
invariants in terms of ordinary
\text{SW}_{1}
invariants. The existence of a smooth limit is non-trivial, even with the freedom to fix
\text{SW}_{2}(c)
with
n_{2}(c)\leq 0
. We therefore consider it worthwhile to include it here.

We will consider
r=2
in what follows. We let the singularity
u_{3}^{*}
be of type
I_{2}
. This makes the analysis suited for the case of
N_{f}=2
with
m_{1}=m_{2}
, as can be seen from Fig.
.
Analogously to (
126
), the contribution from this singularity then reads

Z^{J}_{\text{SW},3,{\bm{\mu}}}=\sum_{c}\text{SW}_{2}(c;J)\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{n_{2}(c)}\,\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{a_{3}=0}\,\left[a_{3}^{-1-n_{2}(c)}e^{-S_{SW,3}}\right],

(153)

where
\text{SW}_{2}(c;J)
is a SW invariant for the
r
-monopole equations. These invariants are not expected to depend on the same four-manifold data as the
I_{1}
SW-invariants. An alternative way to express this contribution is as the
q_{3}^{0}
term,

Z^{J}_{\text{SW},3,{\bm{\mu}}}=\sum_{c}\text{SW}_{2}(c;J)\,\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{n_{2}(c)}\,{\rm Coeff}_{q_{3}^{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\pi i}\frac{da_{3}}{d\tau_{3}}a_{3}^{-1-n_{2}(c)}e^{-S_{SW,3}}\right].

(154)

Moreover, in the absence of background fluxes, the effective action takes the form

e^{-S_{SW,3}}=\kappa_{3}\,\mathcal{A}_{3}^{\chi}\,\mathcal{B}_{3}^{\sigma}\,\mathcal{E}_{3}^{c^{2}}\,\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},3}.

(155)

In analogy with (
130
), we
assume
that these invariants change under wall-crossing as

\text{SW}_{r}(c;J^{+})-\text{SW}_{r}(c;J^{-})=-(-1)^{n_{r}(c)}.

(156)

The relation between the
u
-plane wall-crossing and the SW-contributions then gives

\begin{split}\left[\Phi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J^{+}}-\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J^{-}}\right]_{3}&=\kappa_{3}\sum_{c}(-1)^{n_{r}(c)}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sgn}(B(c,J^{+}))-\mathrm{sgn}(B(c,J^{-})))\\
&\quad\times\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{n_{2}(c)}\,\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{a_{3}=0}\left[a_{3}^{-1-n_{2}(c)}\mathcal{A}_{3}^{\chi}\mathcal{B}_{3}^{\sigma}\mathcal{E}_{3}^{c^{2}}\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},3}\right].\end{split}

(157)

By substitution of
\chi+\sigma=4
, this becomes

\begin{split}\left[\Phi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J^{+}}-\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J^{-}}\right]_{3}&=-\kappa_{3}\sum_{c}(-1)^{(c^{2}-\sigma)/4}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sgn}(B(c,J^{+}))-\mathrm{sgn}(B(c,J^{-})))\\
&\quad\times\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{(c^{2}-\sigma)/4-1}\,\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{a_{3}=0}\left[a_{3}^{-(c^{2}-\sigma)/4}\mathcal{A}_{3}^{4}(\mathcal{B}_{3}/\mathcal{A}_{3})^{\sigma}\mathcal{E}_{3}^{c^{2}}\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},3}\right].\end{split}

(158)

To proceed, we assume that the
I_{2}
singularity is at
\tau\to 1
in the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}({\bm{m}})
, and choose vanishing background fluxes for simplicity. As a result, the ’t Hooft flux is fixed
{\bm{\mu}}=K/2
.
We can determine the couplings
\mathcal{A}_{3},\dots,\mathcal{F}_{{\bm{\mu}},3}
by comparing (
149
) and (
158
).
This gives

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{3}&=2^{1/2}e^{\pi i/8}\kappa_{3}^{-1/4}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-1/2}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{3}^{1/2},\\
\mathcal{B}_{3}&=2^{1/4}\kappa_{3}^{-1/4}e^{-\pi i/8}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{1/4}\frac{B_{3}}{a_{3}^{1/4}},\\
\mathcal{E}_{3}&=e^{-\pi i/4}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{-1/4}\,a_{3}^{1/4}q_{3}^{-1/8},\\
\mathcal{F}_{K/2,3}&=e^{\pi iB(c+K,K/2)},\end{split}

(159)

where

\left(\frac{da}{du}\right)_{3}(\tau_{3})=\tau_{3}^{-1}\left(\frac{da}{du}\right)(1-1/\tau_{3}).

(160)

\bm{I_{2}}
singularity in
\bm{N_{f}=2}

We continue by sketching the determination of these couplings for the
I_{2}
singularity in the
N_{f}=2
theory with equal masses. The functions
u
and
da/du
are known exactly for this theory
(
Aspman:2021vhs
, Section 5.1)
, we recall them in (
319
).
14
14

14

We have corrected the sign of
da/du
in (
319
) compared to
(
Aspman:2021vhs
, Eq. (5.4))
, such that
da/du\to i2^{3/2}\,q^{1/4}
per our convention (
) for the weak coupling limit.
It is then straightforward to determine couplings near the
I_{2}
singularity
\tau=1-1/\tau_{3}\to 1
. For example,
(da/du)_{3}
is given by (
324
), with leading terms in (
325
).

One similarly determines the first terms in the
q_{3}-
expansions of
u_{3}
(
326
), and
a_{3}
(
327
) at this singularity. Substitution in (
159
) then allows to the determine the contribution from the
I_{2}
singularity for the equal mass theory.

12.3
Results for
K3

Using the above formulas, it is straightforward to explicitly calculate SW contributions for specific four-manifolds, for generic as well as specific masses. We focus in this subsection on evaluations for
X=K3
, and discuss
N_{f}=1,2,3
separately.

\bm{N_{f}=1}

For simplicity, we consider a vanishing background flux,
{\bm{k}}_{1}=0
, such that
{\bm{\mu}}=0
necessarily. The full correlation function (
5
) then reads,

Z_{0}(m)[u^{\ell}]=\sum_{j=1}^{3}Z_{SW,j,0}(m)[u^{\ell}].

(161)

We can evaluate this for generic mass
m
using the topological data mentioned above Equation (
133
). This gives the results listed in Table
12
.

Similarly to the case for the
u
-plane integral for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
without background fluxes, large mass and small mass expansions of the singularities
u_{j}^{*}
and their contributions give identical results, which demonstrates that these correlation functions are smooth functions of
m
. Given the intricate cubic roots (see discussion around (
41
)), it is remarkable that the mass expansions terminate at finite order.
15
15

15

This is possibly a consequence of Vieta’s formula (
42
): Both small and large mass expansions of the singularities
u_{j}^{*}
are infinite series, while their sum is
\sum_{j=1}^{3}u_{j}^{*}=m^{2}
. By the fundamental theorem of symmetric polynomials, a similar statement can be made about sums
\sum_{j=1}^{2+N_{f}}P(u_{j}^{*})
with
P
a polynomial. However, as is clear from the explicit expressions (
142
), (
148
), (
151
), the correlators
Z_{0}[u^{\ell}]
are sums of such kind with
P
a
rational
function.

\ell

\displaystyle Z_{0}(m)[u^{\ell}]
for
N_{f}=1

0

\displaystyle 64\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}

1

\displaystyle 64\frac{m^{4}}{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}+8\Lambda_{1}m

\displaystyle 64\frac{m^{6}}{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}+16\Lambda_{1}m^{3}+\frac{9}{4}\Lambda_{1}^{4}

:
Table with values of
Z_{0}[u^{\ell}]
for
N_{f}=1
.

The dimension of the moduli space and the rank of the matter bundle are

\begin{split}&{\dim}(\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k})=3k-2,\\
&{\dim}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k})=4k-6,\\
&{\rm rk}(W_{k})=-k+4.\end{split}

(162)

This is positive for small
k
, and as a result the dimension of the moduli space of non-Abelian monopoles is larger than the moduli space of instantons for these values. The point observable
u
is a 4-form on the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k}
of non-Abelian SW equations.

Large mass limit and expansion

The decoupling limit
m\to\infty
(
24
) applied to
Z_{0}[u^{\ell}]
does not exist, since the contribution of
u_{3}^{*}
diverges. There is thus no smooth decoupling limit to the
N_{f}=0
result as before for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. However motivated by the distinction between the monopole and instanton component, see Figure
9
in Section
7
, we can consider the partition function for the instanton component as the sum of the contribution of
u_{1}^{*}
and
u_{2}^{*}
,

Z^{\rm i}_{0}(m)[u^{\ell}]=Z_{SW,1,0}(m)[u^{\ell}]+Z_{SW,2,0}(m)[u^{\ell}].

(163)

Then the decoupling limit applied to
Z^{\rm i}_{0}(m)[u^{\ell}]
is consistent with the
N_{f}=0
result, that is to say, it vanishes in the limit. This is due to the dimension of the moduli space not being a multiple of 4 in this case, such that insertions of point observables give a vanishing answer.

We can consider the
large
mass expansion of
Z_{0}^{\rm i}
. In contrast to
Z_{0}(m)[u^{\ell}]
, this is an infinite series. The first few terms are,

\begin{split}&Z_{0}^{\rm i}(m)[u^{0}]=-\frac{3}{4}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{4}}{m^{4}}-\frac{5}{16}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{7}}{m^{7}}-\frac{63}{512}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{10}}{m^{10}}-\frac{99}{2048}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{13}}{m^{13}}+\dots,\\
&Z_{0}^{\rm i}(m)[u^{1}]=-\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{4}}{m^{2}}-\frac{7}{16}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{7}}{m^{5}}-\frac{175}{1024}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{10}}{m^{8}}-\frac{273}{4096}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{13}}{m^{11}}+\dots,\\
&Z_{0}^{\rm i}(m)[u^{3}]=-\frac{5}{8}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{7}}{m^{3}}-\frac{245}{1024}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{10}}{m^{6}}-\frac{189}{2048}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{13}}{m^{9}}-\frac{4719}{131072}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{16}}{m^{12}}+\dots,\end{split}

(164)

The infinite series demonstrate that infinitely many instanton sectors contribute. For example, we deduce from
Z^{\rm i}_{0}[u^{0}]
that the top Chern class has maximal degree
2\dim(\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k})
and thus exceeds the rank of the matter bundle. In other words, it is actually a matter sheaf rather than a matter bundle.

Small mass expansion

Instead of a large mass expansion, one can also make a small mass expansion of the contributions of
u_{1}^{*}
and
u_{2}^{*}
. The coefficients turn out to be complex numbers, obscuring their interpretation as intersection numbers. Moreover the expansion involves negative powers of
\Lambda_{1}
, which suggests that the terms arise from sectors with negative instanton numbers
k
. Altogether, the large
m
expansion thus seems more physical.

Limit to AD mass

In section
8.1
, we argued that in an AD limit
m\to m_{\text{AD}}
, we can split the singularities into two sets
S
and
S^{\prime}
, where the singularities in
S^{\prime}
collide in the AD point, while the ones in
S
are not involved in the limit. The contribution from
S^{\prime}
we denote in (
4
) by
Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}
. In our notation, the singularities
u_{2}^{*}
and
u_{3}^{*}
then give the contribution
Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}
.

In the limit
m\to m_{\text{AD}}=\tfrac{3}{4}\Lambda_{1}
for
N_{f}=1
, the contributions from
u_{2}^{*}
and
u_{3}^{*}
diverge individually as
(m-m_{\text{AD}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, where the coefficients for
u_{2}^{*}
and
u_{3}^{*}
differ by a minus sign. Their sum is correspondingly regular, and we find a finite limit
Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}
. The conditions for a smooth limit were studied in
Marino:1998uy
, and led to sum rules for SW invariants and the notion of superconformal simple type. For the type
II
AD point on
K3
there is no such sum rule, which is indeed not necessary because the partition function itself is regular without any constraints.

More specifically, we calculate
Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}[1]=\frac{320}{9}
, while the contribution from
u_{1}^{*}
is
\frac{4}{9}
. All together it reproduces the result in the Table
12
at
m=m_{\text{AD}}
. For
\ell=1
, the first cusp contributes
-\frac{5}{12}\Lambda_{1}^{2}
, and the sum of
u_{2}^{*}
and
u_{3}^{*}
is
Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}[u^{1}]=\frac{80}{3}\Lambda_{1}^{2}
. Their sum again agrees with the table.
We can in fact calculate the AD contribution for any
\ell
,
16
16

16

This implies that
Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}[e^{2pu}]=Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}(1)e^{2pu_{\text{AD}}}
. This generating function
Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}^{1}(p)\coloneqq Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}[e^{2pu}]
then satisfies
\Delta(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{p})Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}^{1}(p)
with
\Delta(u)=u-u_{\text{AD}}
, similar to the structure result in section
12.5
.

Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}[u^{\ell}]=\frac{320}{9}\left(\frac{3\Lambda_{1}^{2}}{4}\right)^{\ell}=Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}[1]\,u_{\text{AD}}^{\ell},

(165)

where
u_{\text{AD}}=\frac{3}{4}\Lambda_{1}^{2}
.
Clearly, all point correlators are nonzero. This is an indication that the selection rule associated with the SCFT is not valid in this case
Shapere:2008zf
;
Moore:2017cmm
;
Gukov:2017
.
This selection rule reads
3\ell=1
for
K3
in the absence of surface observables, and so it does not have any solutions for point observables only. We conclude that this selection rule is not valid for the AD point of the SQCD curve. Rather, it holds for the AD curve obtained by promoting the deformation parameters to operators and assigning scaling dimensions
Argyres:1995xn
;
Moore:2017cmm
;
Marino:1998uy
. This explains our notation
Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}
, where we keep
Z_{\text{AD}}
for the partition function calculated from the AD curve.

Notably, from (
165
) it follows that

Z_{\widetilde{\text{AD}}}[\mathtt{u}^{\ell}]=0,\qquad\ell\geq 1,

(166)

where
\mathtt{u}=u-u_{\text{AD}}
is the Coulomb branch parameter with the AD point at its origin. That is, the 0-observable
\mathtt{u}
is a null vector of the
\widetilde{\text{AD}}
theory, which is in agreement with the selection rule (see also
Moore:2017cmm
). We will discuss the relation to SW contributions of AD curves in a future work
AFMM:future
.

\bm{N_{f}=2}

We continue with the
N_{f}=2
theory. For distinct masses,
{\bm{m}}=(m_{1},m_{2})
with
m_{1}\neq m_{2}
, this theory has 4 strong coupling singularities of type
I_{1}
. We find for the partition function,

\begin{split}Z_{0}({\bm{m}})[u^{0}]&=32\Lambda_{2}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{(m_{1}-m_{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{32\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{(m_{1}-m_{2})^{2}}+\frac{8\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{m_{2}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(m_{1}-m_{2}).\end{split}

(167)

The divergence is due to the monopole contributions of the singularities
u_{3}^{*}
and
u_{4}^{*}
.

The singular behaviour near the equal mass limit,
{\bm{m}}=(m,m)
, has an interesting interpretation in terms of vertex algebras
Dedushenko:2017tdw
. The singularity is expected to be cancelled by a contribution from the Higgs branch (we will comment on this further below).

The equal mass limit is non-singular for the contributions from
u_{1}^{*}
and
u_{2}^{*}
. For the partition function of the instanton component (
163
) with equal masses, we find

\begin{split}&Z^{\rm i}_{0}({\bm{m}})[u^{0}]=\frac{4\Lambda_{2}^{5}}{m^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{(\Lambda_{2}+2m)^{3}}+\frac{1}{(\Lambda_{2}-2m)^{3}}\right)\\
&\quad\qquad\quad\,\,\,=-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{6}}{m^{6}}-\frac{5}{4}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{8}}{m^{8}}-\frac{21}{32}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{10}}{m^{10}}-\frac{55}{512}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{14}}{m^{14}}+\dots,\\
&Z^{\rm i}_{0}({\bm{m}})[u^{1}]=-\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{6}}{m^{4}}-\frac{21}{16}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{8}}{m^{6}}-\frac{25}{32}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{10}}{m^{8}}-\frac{91}{256}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{12}}{m^{10}}-\frac{9}{64}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{14}}{m^{12}}+\dots,\\
&Z^{\rm i}_{0}({\bm{m}})[u^{3}]=-\frac{5}{4}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{8}}{m^{4}}-\frac{115}{128}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{10}}{m^{6}}-\frac{113}{256}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{12}}{m^{8}}-\frac{373}{2048}\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{14}}{m^{10}}+\dots.\end{split}

(168)

The equal mass case and multi-monopole SW-invariants

Instead of taking the equal mass limit of
Z_{0}({\bm{m}})
, one can evaluate the partition function directly at
m_{1}=m_{2}=m
. While this gives a finite answer, the theory now involves a strong coupling cusp of type
I_{2}
. The
Q
-fixed equation is a multi-monopole equation, and will thus involve a generalization of the SW-invariant for
I_{1}
singularities as discussed in Section
12.2
. Even though the limit
m_{1}\to m_{2}
is singular for the sum of the contributions from
u_{3}^{*}
and
u_{4}^{*}
, one obtains a finite answer if one works directly in the equal mass theory
m_{1}=m_{2}=m
. This was demonstrated for
m=0
in
Kanno:1998qj
.

To this end, let us recall that for a non-vanishing contribution, the virtual dimension of the monopole moduli space (
152
) should be non-negative, while at the same time
c_{+}^{2}=0
. These two conditions have no solutions for a generic Kähler point
J
except for
c=0
, but can have a finite number of solutions for special choices of
J
.

For
K3
, the requirement that (
152
) is non-negative is
c^{2}\geq 16(\frac{1}{r}-1)
. Together with the condition that
c_{+}=0
and that
c
is a
\text{Spin}^{c}
structure, this gives the following possibilities

\displaystyle r=1

\displaystyle:\quad c_{-}^{2}=0\qquad

\displaystyle n_{1}(c)=0

(169)

\displaystyle r=2

\displaystyle:\quad c_{-}^{2}=0,-8,\qquad

\displaystyle n_{2}(c)=2,0

\displaystyle r=3

\displaystyle:\quad c_{-}^{2}=0,-8,\qquad

\displaystyle n_{3}(c)=4,1,

\displaystyle r=4

\displaystyle:\quad c_{-}^{2}=0,-8,\qquad

\displaystyle n_{4}(c)=6,2.

The contributions from different
c
can have different signs, such that the total contribution can be metric independent, even if individual contributions are metric dependent.

Returning to the case
r=2
, using the exact expressions in Appendix
E.3
, for the contribution from
c=0
we obtain

-\text{SW}_{2}(0)\frac{16\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{\kappa_{3}}\frac{(57\Lambda_{2}^{4}-48\Lambda_{2}^{2}m^{2}+64m^{4})}{(\Lambda_{2}^{2}-4m^{2})^{3}},

(170)

and for the contribution from
c^{2}=-8

-\sum_{c,c^{2}=-8}\text{SW}_{2}(c)\frac{2\Lambda_{2}^{6}}{\kappa_{3}(\Lambda_{2}^{2}-4m^{2})^{3}}.

(171)

In the massless limit, these contributions (
170
) and (
171
) match with
(
Kanno:1998qj
, Eq. (4.6))
(with
c=2\lambda_{\text{KY}}
). The total result then becomes

\begin{split}Z_{0}(m)[u^{0}]&=\frac{2\Lambda_{2}^{6}}{(\Lambda_{2}^{2}-4m^{2})^{3}}\left(\frac{4\Lambda_{2}^{2}+48m^{2}}{m^{2}}\,\text{SW}_{1}(0)-\sum_{c,c^{2}=-8}\frac{\text{SW}_{2}(c)}{\kappa_{3}}\right.\\
&\quad-\left.8(57\Lambda_{2}^{4}-48\Lambda_{2}^{2}m^{2}+64m^{4})\,\frac{\text{SW}_{2}(0)}{\kappa_{3}}\right),\end{split}

(172)

where we substituted the contribution from
u_{1}^{*}
plus
u_{2}^{*}
given in Eq. (
168
), and made the dependence on
\text{SW}_{1}(0)
explicit.

While this is finite for generic
m
, we notice a divergence for the individual contributions as the mass approaches the AD mass,
m\to m_{\text{AD}}=\Lambda_{2}/2
. On the other hand, the previous discussion and results for
N_{f}=1
give an indication that the combined contribution of the three cusps may become a (Laurent) polynomial in
m
. In addition to (
172
), there is a contribution from the non-compact Higgs branch. It is unclear however, how the Higgs branch dynamics could cancel the divergence for
m\to m_{\text{AD}}=\Lambda_{2}/2
. Although heuristically, we are then led to the idea that the 2-monopole invariants
{\rm SW}_{2}
satisfy special relations to ensure smoothness at
m_{\text{AD}}=\Lambda_{2}/2
. This is similar to the constraints on the SW invariants from the AD mass locus in other theories
Marino:1998uy
.

To deduce the relations for
{\rm SW}_{2}
, note that both contributions from
c=0
(
170
) and
c^{2}=-8
(
171
) have a cubic singularity at
m=m_{\text{AD}}
. Correspondingly, their sum is regular in the AD limit if three linear combinations of the invariants
\text{SW}_{2}(c)
vanish. Remarkably, there is a linear dependence among these three combinations, and the AD limit is regular if
17
17

17

If we eliminate
\kappa_{3}
, we find
\sum_{c:c^{2}=-8}\text{SW}_{2}(c)+264\text{SW}_{2}(0)=0
. This condition alone is not sufficient for the regularity of the limit.

\displaystyle\sum_{c:c^{2}=-8}\text{SW}_{2}(c)+392\,\text{SW}_{2}(0)-64\kappa_{3}\,\text{SW}_{1}(0)

\displaystyle=0,

(173)

\displaystyle\sum_{c:c^{2}=-8}\text{SW}_{2}(c)+520\,\text{SW}_{2}(0)-128\kappa_{3}\,\text{SW}_{1}(0)

\displaystyle=0.

These vanishing combinations are reminiscent of the sum rules for the type
II
AD theory
Marino:1998uy
.
The unique solution to these equations, setting
\text{SW}_{1}(0)=1
, is

\frac{\text{SW}_{2}(0)}{\kappa_{3}}=\frac{1}{2},\qquad\frac{\sum_{c,\,c^{2}=-8}\text{SW}_{2}(c)}{\kappa_{3}}=-132.

(174)

We list correlation functions
\left<u^{\ell}\right>
for small
\ell
in Table
13
for this solution.
Since the correlation functions are polynomials in
m
, the AD limit
m\to m_{\text{AD}}
is smooth. More generally, we expect that the multi-monopole invariants
\text{SW}_{r>1}
can be deduced in this way from the
I_{1}
SW invariants.

We do stress though that the analysis of the multi-monopole suggests that
{\rm SW}_{2}(c)=0
,
c\neq 0
, for generic metric since there are no solutions to
c_{+}=0
. It is thus an interesting question whether (
174
) can be derived starting from the multi-monopole equations.

Comparing different limits

In
Marino:1998uy
;
Marino:1998uy_short
, the collision of two
I_{1}
singularities to a type
II
AD point has been considered, which is the only existing limit in
N_{f}=1
. For
N_{f}\geq 2
on the other hand, higher type AD points appear, which allow for a larger variety of possible collisions. If we have two masses for instance, we can form a type
III
AD point in three possible ways (see Fig.
12
). Carefully calculating the three possible limits of partition functions should allow us to study the precise form of the contribution from the Higgs branch (see also
Moore:1997dj
;
losev1998
;
Dedushenko:2017tdw
;
Nakatsu:1997jw
;
Martens:2006hu
;
LoNeSha
;
Antoniadis:1996ra
).

Figure 12
:
Plot of the mass space
(m_{1},m_{2})
in
N_{f}=2
. The AD locus (blue) consists of two contours giving rise to a Coulomb branch (CB) with each one
II
AD point. When the loci meet, the CB contains a type
III
point (green). This point can be approached from three distinct configurations: Away from
m_{1}=m_{2}
, the limit is
I_{1}+I_{1}+I_{1}\to III
(brown). On the line
m_{1}=m_{2}
(orange), there is a
I_{2}
singularity, with a limit
I_{2}+I_{1}\to III
(purple). Finally, on the generic AD locus there is a
II
singular point with a limit
II+I_{1}\to III
(cyan). On any point on the
I_{2}
line
m_{1}=m_{2}
, a Higgs branch with geometry
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
meets the Coulomb branch.

We can now compare the calculation of the SW contribution from the type
III
Argyres-Douglas point, as illustrated in Fig.
12
. As required, the limit
m\to m_{\text{AD}}
for the
I_{2}+I_{1}
contribution is smooth, and from Table
13
we find that it equals 32.
Calculating the limit of
I_{1}+I_{1}+I_{1}
from any direction away from
m_{1}=m_{2}
, we can use the expansion (
167
)
for
N_{f}=2
, where we expand all singularities around
m_{1}=m_{2}
. This expansion agrees with setting say
m_{1}=m_{\text{AD}}=\frac{1}{2}\Lambda_{2}
and considering the limit
m_{2}\to m_{\text{AD}}
, as in Fig.
12
. In this limit, the constant part in (
167
) evaluates to 32 as well, such that the two limits
I_{1}+I_{1}+I_{1}\to III
and
I_{2}+I_{1}\to III
agree precisely, up to the divergent term
32\Lambda_{2}^{2}/(m_{1}-m_{2})^{2}
. Thus the
\text{SW}_{2}
invariant for the
I_{2}
contribution naturally regularises the singular limit of colliding two
I_{1}
singularities. This is another nontrivial constraint on the relations (
174
).

\bm{N_{f}=3}

Another configuration suitable for studying
I_{2}
singularities is
{\bm{m}}=(m,0,0)
in
N_{f}=3
, which for generic mass
m
is
(I_{1}^{*},2I_{2},I_{1})
. See for example
Aspman:2021vhs
for more details. The two
I_{2}
singularities
u_{\pm}=\frac{1}{8}m\Lambda_{3}
become the
I_{4}
singularity in the massless limit
m\to 0
, while the
I_{1}
singularity
m_{*}=m^{2}+\frac{1}{2^{8}}\Lambda_{3}^{2}
collides with either
u_{+}
or
u_{-}
for
m\to\pm m_{\text{AD}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{16}\Lambda_{3}
.
We can calculate the sum over the two
I_{2}
SW contributions and the
I_{1}
SW contribution. For this, we solve the sextic equation associated with the SW curve
Aspman:2021vhs
and calculate the local expansions
a
,
\frac{du}{da}
,
\frac{da}{d\tau}
and
\Delta_{3}
near the cusps
u_{\pm}
using either of the two respective solutions near the cusps. We find again that the AD limit
m\to m_{\text{AD}}
is regular if we impose sum rules on the SW invariants. With the expressions (
159
), we find that the AD limit is regular if and only if (
174
) holds.
This may be viewed as a consequence of the fact that the SW curves for
{\bm{m}}=(m,m)
and
{\bm{m}}=(m,0,0)
are isogenous
ACFMM23
.

We list some values of
Z_{0}[u^{\ell}]
in Table
14
. The order 6 pole comes from the mass singularity, since for
N_{f}=3
we have
\left((m_{1}-m_{2})(m_{2}-m_{3})(m_{3}-m_{1})\right)^{\chi_{h}}\sim m^{6}
for all masses vanishing. One qualitative difference to
N_{f}=2
is that the mass divergence for
m\to 0
disappears for larger
\ell
, i.e.
Z_{0}[u^{\ell}]
is regular as
m\to 0
for
\ell\geq 5
. For the
I_{2}
singularity in
N_{f}=2
, the leading term is always
m^{-2}+
regular, for any
\ell
.

for
