12.4
Relation to results for algebraic surfaces and Segre numbers

The coefficients in the mass expansions such as (
164
) and (
168
) are known in the mathematical literature as Segre numbers
Gottsche:2021ihz
. Yet, in the UV formulation (see Section
), correlation functions are intersection numbers obtained by integrating
Chern
classes on the moduli space.
In this Section, we aim to explain the connection between Chern classes and Segre classes, and provide a relation between SW partition functions and these geometric invariants.

Brief review of Segre classes of moduli spaces

The (total) Segre class
s(E)
of a vector bundle is a characteristic class, which is suitable for the analysis of intersection theory in singular settings.
18
18

18

We refer the reader to
fulton1984
for definitions, in particular the Sections 3.1 and 3.2.
In this context, the more familiar Chern classes are defined in terms of the Segre classes. To this end, one defines for a vector bundle
E
the following formal power series,

\begin{split}s_{t}(E)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}s_{j}(E)\,t^{j},\\
\end{split}

(175)

in terms of the Segre classes
s_{j}(E)
. We refer for their definition to
(
fulton1984
, Section 3.1)
. The Chern classes
c_{j}(E)
are then defined by the inverse of
s_{t}(E)
,

c_{t}(E)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}(E)\,t^{j}=s_{t}(E)^{-1}.

(176)

It can be shown that
c_{t}(E)
is a polynomial whose degree is bounded by
{\rm rk}(E)
.

Segre classes first appeared in the context of moduli of vector bundles in an article by Tyurin
Tyurin1994
.
Lehn
Lehn:1999
put forward an algorithmic description for the evaluation of top Segre classes for Hilbert schemes of points, which corresponds physically to
U(1)
gauge theory. His conjecture was recently proven in
Marian:2015
. Reference
gottsche2022
gives proofs for various conjectures relating Segre and Verlinde numbers of Hilbert schemes. Segre classes were introduced in
Gottsche:2020ass
for higher rank bundles over projective surfaces. Reference
Oberdieck_2022
gives proofs for these higher rank conjectures for
K3
surfaces as well as for the Segre-Verlinde correspondence. Segre classes have also appeared in a series of work by Feehan and Leness on SW invariants for specific monopole moduli spaces
Feehan:1997vp
;
Feehan:1997rt
;
Feehan:2001jc
;
feehan2001pu
. See also
Gottsche:2019meh
for a recent survey on recent conjectures on the various correspondences.

Comparison between Segre numbers and physical correlation functions

In order to demonstrate the correspondence between Segre numbers and physical partition functions, let us first give the mathematical presentation. For simplicity, we fix the gauge group to be SU(2), and will not consider surface observables. Then
\rho=2
,
s=N_{f}
and
L=0
in
Gottsche:2021ihz
;
Gottsche:2020ass
). Conjecture 2.8 in
Gottsche:2020ass
reads in terms of the universal functions
V,W,\dots
,

\begin{split}&\sum_{k}z^{\frac{1}{2}\dim(\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k})}\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k}}c(\alpha_{\mathcal{M}})\,\mu_{D}(p)^{\ell}\\
&\qquad=2^{2-\chi_{\rm h}+\lambda}V^{c_{2}(\alpha)}\,W^{c_{1}(\alpha)^{2}}\,X^{\chi_{\rm h}}\,Y^{c_{1}(\alpha)\cdot K_{X}}\,Z^{K_{X}^{2}}\,T^{\ell}\\
&\qquad\quad\times\sum_{c}\varepsilon^{a\cdot{\bm{\mu}}}\,{\rm SW}(K_{X}-2a)\,Y_{1}^{c_{1}(\alpha)\cdot a}\,Z_{1}^{a^{2}},\end{split}

(177)

where
\alpha_{\mathcal{M}}
is related to
\alpha
using the universal bundle, and we used that the SW basic class
c
is related to
a
by
c=K_{X}-2a
.
The functions
V,W,X,T
are explicitly determined in
Gottsche:2021ihz
;
Gottsche:2020ass
for arbitrary rank. This leads typically to an infinite series in the parameter
z
, which suggests that
\alpha_{\mathcal{M}}
is a sheaf rather than a vector bundle.

Clearly, the structure of (
177
) is very similar to that of (
128
), and we can relate the functions
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\dots
to combinations of the functions
V,W,\dots
.

We discuss in the following the agreement of some of the functions in both the physical and mathematical approach.

\bm{N_{f}=1}

Let us begin with the case of only one mass. The expansion in
z
is expressed by introducing an auxiliary variable
t
through
z=t(1+t/2)^{1/2}
. This relation can be inverted, which we denote by
t(z)
.
19
19

19

The square of the relation
z=t(1+t/2)^{1/2}
is cubic in
t
, such that we find the solution

t(z)=\frac{1}{3}\left(\sqrt[3]{27z^{2}+3\sqrt{3}\sqrt{27z^{4}-16z^{2}}-8}+\frac{4}{\sqrt[3]{27z^{2}+3\sqrt{3}\sqrt{27z^{4}-16z^{2}}-8}}-2\right),

(178)

which can be expanded either at
z=0
or
z=\infty
.
For instance, we have
t(z)=z+\mathcal{O}(z^{2})
as
z\to 0
, and
t(z)=2^{\frac{1}{3}}z^{\frac{2}{3}}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-\frac{2}{3}})
as
z\to\infty
. In calculations, we must expand
t(z)
in
z
first in order to obtain the correct phases.

The universal functions then read as function of
t
as
Gottsche:2020ass
,

\begin{split}W(t)&=(1+\tfrac{1}{2}t)^{-1},\\
X(t)&=(1+\tfrac{1}{2}t)^{-3/4}\,(1+\tfrac{3}{4}t)^{-1/2},\\
T(t)&=2t(1+\tfrac{3}{8}t).\end{split}

(179)

By comparing (
128
) and (
177
), we deduce that
X
is proportional to
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{12}/\mathcal{B}_{1}^{8}
. Using (
139
), we then find the relation

X\left(t(z)\right)^{2}=\frac{48\,m^{5/2}}{\Lambda_{1}^{9/2}}\frac{g_{3}(u_{1}^{*})}{P^{\text{M}}_{1}(u_{1}^{*})^{2}}.

(180)

with the identification

z=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{m}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}=\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}{2m^{2}}.

(181)

Comparison of (
128
) and (
177
) also identifies
T
and
u_{1}^{*}
. Indeed with the relation (
181
), we arrive at

T(t(z))=-m^{-2}\,u_{1}^{*}.

(182)

This equivalence is due to the identity

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}(-m^{2}T(t(z)))

\displaystyle=0,

(183)

where
\Delta_{1}
is the discriminant of
N_{f}=1
. These identities are consequences of the SW geometry alone.
For instance, we can prove the first identity using the definition (
179
) and reducing the sextic polynomial
\Delta_{1}(-m^{2}T(t(z)))
in
t(z)
using
t^{3}+2t^{2}-2z^{2}=0
.

For
N_{f}=1
, there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry (
43
) that relates all three singularities
u_{j}^{*}
under
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
rotations of
\Lambda_{1}
. This would suggest that if one singularity
u_{1}^{*}
is related to universal functions generating the Segre invariants (as in (
183
)), then it must be true also for the other singularities. This is however not the case, as can be seen by expanding
t(z)
as a series in
z
, which is a (regular) Taylor series at
z=0
(see footnote
19
). If we rotate
\Lambda_{1}\mapsto\zeta_{3}\Lambda_{1}
, the variable
z
(
181
) is mapped to
-z
. Thus under the change of variables
t\leftrightarrow z
relating the SW invariants to Segre invariants, the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry collapses to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry, relating the contributions
u_{1}^{*}
,
u_{2}^{*}
constituting the instanton component. We find,

u_{2}^{*}(\Lambda_{1})=u_{1}^{*}(\zeta_{3}\Lambda_{1})=-m^{2}T(t(-z)),

(184)

while
u_{3}^{*}
can not be expressed through
T
.

If we expand at
m=0
rather, we have the full
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry
u_{j}^{*}(\zeta_{3}\Lambda_{1})=u^{*}_{j+1}(\Lambda_{1})
with
j\mod 3
. Expanding
t(z)
(
178
) for large
z
gives a Laurent series in
z^{\frac{2}{3}}
at
z=\infty
. Rotating
\Lambda_{1}\mapsto\zeta_{3}\Lambda_{1}
gives
z\mapsto e^{\pi i}z
and thus three different solutions
t(z)
, and therefore

\displaystyle u_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle=-m^{2}T(t(z)),

(185)

\displaystyle u_{2}^{*}

\displaystyle=-m^{2}T(t(e^{\pi i}z)),

\displaystyle u_{3}^{*}

\displaystyle=-m^{2}T(t(e^{-\pi i}z)).

For large masses, the singularity
u_{3}^{*}
corresponds to the hypermultiplet that decouples, and thus is associated with the monopole component. For small masses on the other hand, the three singularities
u_{1,2,3}^{*}
are indistinguishable. This can also be seen from the fact that
u_{2}^{*}
and
u_{3}^{*}
merge as we increase the mass from
m=0
to
m_{\text{AD}}=\frac{3}{4}\Lambda_{1}
. Thus the labels of the singularities
u_{j}^{*}
are not meaningful throughout the whole parameter space of the masses. As a consequence, it is not possible to attribute either
u_{1,2,3}^{*}
to the monopole component, which enables the identification (
185
).

To include a background flux for
N_{f}=1
, we identify
c_{1}(\alpha)={\bm{k}}_{1}
and
c_{2}(\alpha)=0
. Comparison of (
128
) and (
177
) relates
W
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. Indeed, using the large mass expansion (
33
) in terms of modular forms, we can compare the first terms near
u_{1}^{*}
, that is, in the limit
\tau_{1}=-1/\tau\to i\infty
. This matches indeed with the relation

W=\frac{m}{\Lambda_{1}}\,\mathcal{C}_{1}.

(186)

Returning to the specific
K3
geometry, we have
\chi_{h}=2
, while
K_{X}=0
. The odd powers of
z
of (
177
) agree with the large mass expansions in Eq. (
164
) up to an overall power in
z
. In fact, we can express the contribution from the
u_{1}^{*}
singularity for
K3
in terms of
X
,
W
and
T
,

Z_{0,1}[u_{1}^{*\ell}]=\frac{1}{2}(-1)^{\ell}m^{2\ell}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{m}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}+{\bm{k}}_{1}^{2}}X\left(t(z)\right)^{2}\,W(t(z))^{{\bm{k}}_{1}^{2}}\,T\left(t(z)\right)^{\ell}.

(187)

\bm{N_{f}=2}

Using the equal mass
m_{1}=m_{2}=m
theory for
N_{f}=2
, we can also check the matching with Segre invariants. For simplicity, we consider the theory only in absence of background fluxes.

The auxiliary variable
t
is equal to
z
in this case. With
s=N_{f}=2
, one finds for
X
from
Gottsche:2020ass
,

\begin{split}X(z)&=(1+z)^{-3/2}.\end{split}

(188)

Then with the identification

z=\frac{\Lambda_{2}}{2m}=\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}{2m^{2}},

(189)

one find the same expansion as for the contribution of the monopole cusp
u_{1}^{*}
. On the other hand, we find a slightly different value when point observables are inserted,
T(z)=2z
, whereas
u_{1}^{*}=-\Lambda_{2}m-\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{8}
. Of course, both insertions capture the same information.

12.5
Generalised simple type

To conclude this Section on SW partition functions for SU(2) SQCD, we study a generalisation of the simple type condition familiar from the pure SU(2) theory.

Let us define the generating functional of Seiberg-Witten invariants (
b_{2}^{+}>1
)

Z_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{N_{f}}[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}]\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\ell!}\left(\tfrac{2p}{\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\right)^{\ell}Z_{\bm{\mu}}^{N_{f}}\left[u^{\ell}\right],

(190)

where
Z_{\bm{\mu}}
is the sum over the contributions of all the
N_{f}+2
strong coupling cusps. In the following, we drop labels such as ‘SW’ and
{\bm{\mu}}
to avoid cluttering the notation, and denote the functions by
Z^{N_{f}}
.

General structure results about such generating functionals are known since the work of Kronheimer and Mrowka
kronheimer1994recurrence
;
Kronheimer1995
;
Fintushel1995
(see also
Donaldson1996
;
Morgan1995
). For instance, a four-manifold is of
generalised simple type
if there exists an integer
n\geq 0
such that the generating function satisfies

\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p^{2}}-4\right)^{n}Z^{0}(p)=0,

(191)

where we focus on the dependence of
Z^{N_{f}}(p)\coloneqq Z^{N_{f}}[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}]
on
p
only.
The
order
is the minimum of all
n
such that (
191
) holds. It has been shown that if
b_{2}^{+}(X)>1
, then the order of generalised simple type manifolds does not depend on the choice of ’t Hooft flux
{\bm{\mu}}
, that is, it only depends on the four-manifold. In fact, all four-manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}>1
are of finite type.
A manifold is said to be of Donaldson simple type if it is of simple type with
n=1
. While all four-manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}>1
are of generalised simple type, it is not known if there are simply-connected manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}>1
which are
not
of Donaldson simple type. Donaldson invariants for manifolds which are not of simple type have been studied in
Kronheimer1997
;
munoz1998basic
;
munoz2002donaldson
.

For instance, for
X
a
K3
surface the result is

\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p^{2}}-4\right)Z^{0}(p)=0.

(192)

In the pure SU(2) case, acting with the operator
\partial_{p}^{2}-4
on correlation functions is analogous to an insertion of the discriminant
\Delta
into the
u
-plane integral
Moore:1997pc
. Since at the monopole and dyon cusps,
\Delta_{D}(\tau)=q+\mathcal{O}(q^{2})
, this increases the overall
q
-exponent of the integrand by one. For arbitrary signature, inserting
\Delta^{n}
with large enough
n
will annihilate the partition function.

In
Labastida:1995gp
;
Kanno:1998qj
;
Labastida:1998hf
it has been observed that the SW contribution in massless SQCD satisfies a similar, but higher order differential equation. In the massless
N_{f}=1
theory, the generating functional satisfies
Kanno:1998qj

\left(\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial p^{3}}+\frac{27}{32}\right)Z^{1}(p)=0,

(193)

which can be directly confirmed from the sum over (
141
). This differential equation has a three-dimensional solution space which is spanned by the functions
e^{2p\mathtt{u}_{j}^{*}}
, where
\Delta_{1}(\mathtt{u}_{j})=0
are the three roots of the massless
N_{f}=1
discriminant, and
\mathtt{u}\coloneqq u/\Lambda_{1}^{2}
. The three coordinates can in this case be easily determined, and are just proportional to
\mathtt{u}_{j}^{*}
. This gives
Kanno:1998qj

Z^{1}(p)=-\frac{2^{6}}{3^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\mathtt{u}_{j}^{*}e^{2p\,\mathtt{u}_{j}^{*}}.

(194)

In the massive case, to the best of our knowledge such a result is not known in the literature. By collecting the results for massive
N_{f}=1
on
K3
(such as in Table
12
) into a generating function, we obtain the relation

\left(\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial p^{3}}-2\mu^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p^{2}}-\frac{9}{2}\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial p}+8\mu^{3}+\frac{27}{32}\right)Z^{1}(p)=0,

(195)

where
\mu=m/\Lambda_{1}
is the dimensionless mass.
Solutions to this equation take a similar form to (
194
), with coefficients now more complicated functions of the dimensionless singular points
\mathtt{u}_{j}^{*}
.

\bm{I_{1}}
singularities

We can in fact easily find the simple type condition for SQCD with
N_{f}
generic masses.
If we write the contribution from each cusp as (we again drop some dependence in the notation)

Z^{N_{f}}[u^{\ell}]=\sum_{j=1}^{2+N_{f}}Z_{j}^{N_{f}}(u_{j}^{*})(u_{j}^{*})^{\ell},

(196)

we resum it in (
190
) to find

Z^{N_{f}}(p)=\sum_{j=1}^{2+N_{f}}Z_{j}^{N_{f}}(\tfrac{u_{j}^{*}}{\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}})e^{2pu_{j}^{*}/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}.

(197)

Let us consider the function
f_{j}(p)=e^{p\,\mathtt{u}_{j}^{*}}
, where the
\mathtt{u}_{j}^{*}
are roots of some polynomial,
\Delta_{N_{f}}(\mathtt{u}_{j}^{*})=0
(we consider
\Delta_{N_{f}}
as a polynomial in
\mathtt{u}_{j}=u/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}
). Then these
2+N_{f}
functions
f_{j}
form the basis of the
2+N_{f}
-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-vector space of solutions to the
2+N_{f}
-order linear ODE
\Delta_{N_{f}}(\partial_{p})f(p)=0
. This is clear from the fact that

\Delta_{N_{f}}(\partial_{p})f_{j}(p)=\Delta(\mathtt{u}_{j}^{*})f_{j}(p)=0.

(198)

Indeed,
\Delta_{N_{f}}
is the characteristic polynomial of this differential equation.
In order to apply this to (
197
), we need to multiply
p
again by 2, due to conventions. It follows that

\boxed{\Delta_{N_{f}}(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p})Z^{N_{f}}(p)=0.}

(199)

This relation includes (
192
), (
193
) and (
195
) as examples, and it is expected to hold for any configuration only involving
I_{1}
singularities.
Similar to the case
N_{f}=0
, we can understand (
199
) as inserting the discriminant
\Delta_{N_{f}}
into the
u
-plane integral. Since
\Delta_{N_{f}}^{D}(\tau)=q+\dots
for any cusp, this increases the exponent of the integrand
q
-series by one, and will thus eliminate the contribution from the strong coupling cusps to the partition function if applied sufficiently many times.

\bm{I_{2}}
singularities

From (
199
) it is possible to extract the behaviour of the generating function
Z^{N_{f}}
in the case where some of the singularities collide. Consider for example the case where all
I_{1}
singularities collide in an
I_{k}
singularity (this is not possible for the SW curves (
), we are merely illustrating the structure). When zeros of the characteristic polynomials merge, the basis of the space of solutions to the ODE seemingly collapses. However, the dimension of the solution space is independent of the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial, as it only depends on its degree. Let for instance
\Delta(u)=(u-u_{0})^{k}
and we consider the equation
\Delta(\partial_{p})f(p)=0
. As an elementary calculation shows
20
20

20

Let
f_{0}(p)=P(p)e^{pu_{0}}
, then
(\partial_{p}-u_{0})f_{0}(p)=P^{\prime}(p)e^{pu_{0}}
and thus
0=\Delta(\partial_{p})f_{0}(p)=P^{(k)}(p)e^{pu_{0}}
. Therefore,
P
is a polynomial of degree
k-1
, and thus any solution to
0=\Delta(\partial_{p})f(p)
is a linear combination of the functions
f_{0}(p)=e^{pu_{0}}\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}a_{n}p^{n}
.
, a basis of solutions is given by the functions
f(p)=P_{k-1}(p)e^{pu_{0}}
, with
P_{k-1}
a polynomial of degree
k-1
.
This structure agrees with the results (4.6) and (4.10) in
Kanno:1998qj
. For the higher
I_{k}
singularities, there are contributions from subleading terms, and as mentioned above, only applies to
\Delta_{N_{f}}^{n}
for some power
n
. This follows necessarily also from the structure of the ODE, since the generating function (
197
) is never of the form
f(p)=P_{k-1}(p)e^{pu_{0}}
unless
k=1
.

For
I_{n}
with
n>1
, in particular massless
N_{f}=2
and
3
where
n=2
and
n=4
occur, the degree of the differential operator is known to in general exceed the degree of the discriminant. For
X=K3
, it has been shown that in the massless case
D_{N_{f}}(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p})Z^{N_{f}}(p)=0
, where
D_{2}
is a polynomial factor of
\Delta_{2}^{2}
, and
D_{3}
is a factor of
\Delta_{3}^{7}

Kanno:1998qj
.

We can confirm this by an explicit calculation. Let
m_{1}=m_{2}=m
in
N_{f}=2
, then there are two
I_{1}
singularities
u_{\pm}
and an
I_{2}
singularity
u_{*}
, in the same notation as
Aspman:2021vhs
. The physical discriminant accordingly reads
\Delta_{2}(u)=(u-u_{*})^{2}(u-u_{+})(u-u_{-})
.
Using the results from Section
12.2
(in particular, resumming the values in Table
13
), we can show that in this case the multiplicity of the
I_{2}
singularity increases to
3
in the differential equation,

(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p}-u_{*})^{3}(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p}-u_{+})(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p}-u_{-})Z^{2}(p)=0.

(200)

In the massless limit
m\to 0
, the singularities
u_{\pm}
merge to another
I_{2}
cusp
u_{0}
. Calculating the
I_{2}
correlation functions for both
u_{*}
and
u_{0}
, we find that

(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p}-u_{*})^{3}(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p}-u_{0})^{3}Z^{2}(p)=0.

(201)

This matches precisely with
(
Kanno:1998qj
, (4.8))
.
Thus whenever two
I_{1}
singularities in the
N_{f}=2
theory collide, in the ODE the linear factors are enhanced to cubic factors.

We can get another similar result for the massive configuration
{\bm{m}}=(m,0,0)
in
N_{f}=3
, which has discriminant
\Delta_{3}(u)=(u-u_{+})^{2}(u-u_{-})^{2}(u-u_{*})
. The point correlators are given explicitly in Table
14
. Resumming all point observable correlators, we find that the generating functions satisfies

(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p}-u_{+})^{3}(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p}-u_{-})^{3}(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p}-u_{*})Z^{3}(p)=0.

(202)

\bm{I_{n}}
singularities

Higher
I_{n}
contributions have also been studied in
Kanno:1998qj
. For the
I_{4}
cusp of massless
N_{f}=3
, the multiplicity of the corresponding factor in the generalised simple type condition is
7
. This motivates the following conjecture: Let
{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{f}}
be a mass configuration giving rise to a spectrum
{\bm{n}}({\bm{m}})=(n_{1},n_{2},\dots)
of massless hypermultiplets at each singularity, i.e. the physical discriminant is
\Delta_{N_{f}}(u)=\prod_{j}(u-u_{j}^{*})^{n_{j}}
. Then the generating function of SW invariants satisfies

\prod_{j}(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p}-u_{j}^{*})^{2n_{j}-1}Z^{N_{f}}(p)=0.

(203)

This in particular implies that
\Delta_{N_{f}}(\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{p})^{7}Z^{N_{f}}(p)=0
for all
N_{f}=0,\dots,4
and all mass configurations, since
n_{j}\leq 4
for
\mathcal{N}=2
SU(2) SQCD
Aspman:2021evt
;
Aspman:2021vhs
.

The reason for the enhanced exponents is the fact that for
n_{j}\geq 1
, the residues receive contributions from subleading terms in the expansion. For
n_{j}=2
in
N_{f}=2
in particular, next-to-next-to-leading order terms of all quantities involved are required for the calculation. Analogous to the above comment, we can thus always express the generating function as

Z^{N_{f}}(p)=\sum_{j}P_{2n_{j}-2}(p)e^{2pu_{j}^{*}/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}},

(204)

where
u_{j}^{*}
are the singularities with multiplicities
n_{j}
, and
P_{2n_{j}-2}(p)
are polynomials of degree
2n_{j}-2
in
p
with coefficients depending on the masses
{\bm{m}}
.

13
Contribution from AD points

As discussed in part I, the superconformal Argyres-Douglas theories present themselves in the fundamental domain as elliptic points and can contribute to
u
-plane integrals. Partition functions of Argyres-Douglas theories have been studied in various contexts, such as in the
\Omega
-background
Nishinaka:2012kn
;
Kimura:2020krd
;
Fucito:2023txg
;
Fucito:2023plp
and in topological theories

Marino:1998uy
;
Marino:1998uy_short
;
Gukov:2017
;
Moore:2017cmm
;
Dedushenko:2018bpp
;
moore_talk2018
;
Marino:1998bm
.

As demonstrated in e.g.

Moore:1997pc
;
Moore:2017cmm
;
Manschot:2021qqe
, the contribution from ‘interior’ points of the
u
-plane, i.e. AD points or the UV point in
N_{f}=4
or
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
, exhibits
continuous
metric dependence rather than discrete wall-crossing.
21
21

21

See for instance
(
Moore:2017cmm
, Equation (7.2))
for an example of an AD theory, and
(
Moore:1997pc
, Section 11.3)
for the
N_{f}=4
and
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory
Unlike the evaluation of the contribution from the cusps, the contribution from the AD points for a specific choice of period point
J
of the metric will then not be constant in the whole chamber of
J
. Rather, under small deformations of
J
it is expected to vary continuously. Note that this can only occur for
b_{2}>1
, since for
b_{2}=1
and
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in particular, the period point is unique.

In this section, we focus on the asymptotic behaviour of the
u
-plane integrand and derive conditions for the integral to be sufficiently singular near the AD point to pick up a contribution. We find that for a large class of manifolds, the contribution from any AD point in any theory vanishes.

While the following discussion can be applied to a large extent to general SW curves, we want to stress that in this part II we calculate only the contributions from the Coulomb branch of SU(2) SQCD, including the contribution from the AD points to SQCD partition functions. As discussed before in Section
8.1
, this can differ from the Argyres-Douglas partition functions themselves, for which a similar analysis can be done using the curves in
Argyres:1995xn
.

13.1
Expansion at AD point

For generic masses, the SW surface for
N_{f}
flavours is a rational elliptic surface with an
I_{4-N_{f}}^{*}
fibre at infinity corresponding to weak coupling, and
2+N_{f}
isolated
I_{1}
singularities at strong coupling.
22
22

22

See Table
25
in Appendix
G
for an explanation of the Kodaira classification of singular fibres
For specific configurations of the masses however, the singularities can collide. For instance, when
m_{N_{f}}
is large and the other masses are generic, one
I_{1}
fibre merges with the fibre
I_{4-N_{f}}^{*}
to form a new fibre
I_{4-(N_{f}-1)}^{*}
at infinity
Aspman:2021vhs
. When
m_{i}=\pm m_{j}
for
i\neq j
, two
I_{1}
singularities collide and become an
I_{2}
fibre
Aspman:2022sfj
(see also (
)). Finally, there is a locus
D^{\text{AD}}_{N_{f}}=0
in mass space where fibres of the type
I_{n}
collide and become an additive fibre
\mathscr{A}\in\{II,III,IV,IV^{*},III^{*},II^{*}\}
. For the SW surfaces, the only possibilities are
\mathscr{A}\in\{II,III,IV\}
.

On important distinction has to be done between the SQCD curves (
) and the curves for the AD theories, discussed in
Argyres:1995xn
for instance. As recently reviewed in
Closset:2021lhd
, the fibre at infinity essentially determines the field theory by giving it a ‘UV definition’: For SQCD with
N_{f}
hypermultiplets it is
I_{4-N_{f}}^{*}
, while for the AD curves it is
II^{*}
,
III^{*}
and
IV^{*}
for the
II
,
III
and
IV
AD theories (see for instance
Caorsi:2019vex
). On the other hand, the singular types
II^{*}
,
III^{*}
and
IV^{*}
themselves are associated with the Minahan–Nemeschansky (MN) SCFTs
Minahan:1996fg
;
Minahan:1996cj
. These theories do not appear in SU(2) SQCD, however, the below analysis carries over to a large extent to such singularities.
In this section, we focus on the SQCD curves, with masses tuned such that an AD point appears. We plan to discuss the topological theory for the AD curves in a future work
AFMM:future
.

When the SW surface contains such an additive fibre
II
,
III
,
IV
, the functional invariant
\mathcal{J}
becomes either 0 (for
II
and
IV
) or
12^{3}
(for
III
). As we identify it with the modular
j
-invariant, these correspond to points
\tau_{0}
in the fundamental domain with
j(\tau_{0})
either
0
or
12^{3}
, which means that
\tau_{0}=\gamma\alpha
or
\tau_{0}=\gamma i
for some
\gamma\in\text{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, where
\alpha=e^{2\pi i/3}
is a cube root of unity. Those are the elliptic points of
\text{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. In general, the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}({\bm{m}})
for some mass configuration
{\bm{m}}
is not modular, however in some cases
\mathcal{F}({\bm{m}})
is the fundamental domain for a subgroup of
\text{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
and then
\tau_{0}
is an elliptic point for that subgroup
Magureanu:2022qym
;
Closset:2021lhd
;
Aspman:2021vhs
.

Let us study the behaviour of the
u
-plane integrand
23
23

23

We omit the measure
d\tau\wedge d\bar{\tau}
the discussion here

\mathcal{I}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\nu(\tau)\Psi(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\bm{z}},\bar{\bm{z}})e^{2pu(\tau)/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}+{\bm{x}}^{2}G_{N_{f}}(\tau)}

(205)

of (
) near
\tau_{0}
, where we drop some of the dependence for brevity.
We also turn off the background fluxes for now. Recall from (
) that the elliptic variable then reads
{\bm{z}}=\frac{{\bm{x}}}{2\pi\Lambda_{N_{f}}}\frac{du}{da}
.
Let us assume that we can express
\Psi(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\bm{z}},\bar{\bm{z}})=\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{G}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\bm{z}},\bar{\bm{z}})
,
then the function

\widehat{h}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\nu(\tau)\widehat{G}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\bm{z}},\bar{\bm{z}})\,e^{2pu(\tau)/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}+{\bm{x}}^{2}G_{N_{f}}(\tau)}

(206)

is an anti-derivative of the integrand, as it satisfies
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{h}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\mathcal{I}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
. Thus
-\mathrm{d}(\widehat{h}\,d\tau)=d\tau\wedge d\bar{\tau}\,\mathcal{I}
, such that we can apply Stokes’ theorem and find that the integral of
d\tau\wedge d\bar{\tau}\,\mathcal{I}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
over
\mathcal{F}({\bm{m}})
is given by
-\int_{\partial\mathcal{F}({\bm{m}})}d\tau\,\widehat{h}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
.

As discussed in Section
, near an elliptic point
\tau_{0}
the integrand
\widehat{h}
can be singular, and there can be a contribution from a small contour integral with radius
\varepsilon
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
In order to find the contribution,
we expand
\widehat{h}
around an elliptic point
\tau_{0}
as

\widehat{h}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\sum_{m\gg-\infty,n\geq 0}d_{0}(m,n)\,(\tau-\tau_{0})^{m}\,(\bar{\tau}-\bar{\tau}_{0})^{n}.

(207)

Recall from part I that the contribution from the elliptic point
\tau_{0}
is then

[\mathcal{I}]_{\tau_{0}}=\frac{n_{0}}{k_{0}}\,d_{0}(-1,0),

(208)

where
n_{0}
and
k_{0}
are integers explained in Section
.

It is crucial that
\widehat{h}
is analytic in
\bar{\tau}
near
\bar{\tau}_{0}
, that is, the non-holomorphic expansion (
207
) only contains non-negative powers of
\bar{\tau}-\bar{\tau}_{0}
. As shown in
Korpas:2019cwg
, anti-derivatives of
\Psi
generally have poles in the elliptic variable. However, the poles can be avoided for a certain choice of anti-derivative.

Deriving the non-holomorphic expansion (
207
) of the anti-derivative of
\widehat{h}
of the
u
-plane integrand for a generic four-manifold with period point
J
, ’t Hooft flux
{\bm{\mu}}
and arbitrary AD configuration is a challenging task. Due to the product structure (
206
), we can study the expansion of the anti-derivative
\widehat{G}
separately from the measure
\nu
. We discuss those expansions in the following subsections in as much generality as possible. Our primary interest is to determine in which cases the expansion (
207
) has a nonzero coefficient
d_{0}(-1,0)
, which is why we mainly focus on the leading exponent rather than the precise (non-zero) leading coefficients.

13.2
Measure factor

In this subsection, we discuss the leading behaviour of the
u
-plane measure
\nu
in the absence of background fluxes. Recall from (
) that in this case the measure factor is proportional to

\nu\propto\frac{da}{d\tau}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{\frac{\chi}{2}}\Delta^{\frac{\sigma}{8}}.

(209)

As noticed in
(
Aspman:2021vhs
, Section 3.1)
, AD points
\tau_{0}
are characterised by the property that
\frac{du}{da}(\tau_{0})=0
. In appendix
G
, we prove this rigorously for any elliptic surface containing a singular fibre of type
II
,
III
and
IV
.
24
24

24

Many of the results easily carry over to singularities of type
IV^{*}
,
III^{*}
and
II^{*}
as well.
In order to work without square roots (
), let us define

\omega\coloneqq\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{2},

(210)

which is proportional to
\frac{g_{3}}{g_{2}}\frac{E_{4}}{E_{6}}
. Then
\omega
has an expansion

\omega(\tau)=d_{\mathcal{T}}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{\text{ord}\,\omega}+\dots

(211)

at
\tau\to\tau_{0}
. The value of
\text{ord}\,\omega
depends on the precise configuration of singular fibres in the elliptic surface, however, for the SQCD curves it is 1 in most cases. The value of
d_{\mathcal{T}}
can be determined exactly as well (see appendix
G
). However, here we are primarily interested in the leading behaviour, for which it is enough that
d_{\mathcal{T}}\neq 0
.

In order to determine the expansion of
\nu
at
\tau_{0}
, we define
u_{0}=u(\tau_{0})
, such that we have an expansion

u(\tau)=u_{0}+c_{\mathcal{T}}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}},

(212)

where we have defined
\mathtt{u}=u-u_{0}
. This is because unless
u_{0}=0
,
u
does not vanish at
\tau_{0}
and so does not have a positive order of vanishing.
25
25

25

In the following, we slightly abuse notation, and define
\text{ord}\,f
as a rational number such that
f(\tau)\sim(\tau-\tau_{0})^{\text{ord}\,f}+\dots
for
\tau\to\tau_{0}
. This number can in particular also be negative. Furthermore, we distinguish this order of vanishing as a function of
\tau
from the order of vanishing of say the Weierstraß invariants
g_{2}
and
g_{3}
as a function of
u
, which we denote by
o_{2}
,
o_{3}
etc., particularly in Appendix
G
.

Again, the order of vanishing
\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}
as well as the nonzero coefficient
c_{\mathcal{T}}
can be determined exactly given a singular elliptic surface. The value
\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}
turns out to partially characterise the
type
of AD point, i.e. type
II
,
III
or
IV
.

In the following, we focus only on the leading behaviour, such that it is enough to calculate the orders of vanishing of all holomorphic functions involved. From (
212
) we can determine the order of vanishing of
\frac{du}{d\tau}
, it is

\text{ord}\,\frac{du}{d\tau}=\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}-1.

(213)

On the other hand, from (
210
) it is clear that

\text{ord}\,\frac{da}{du}=-\frac{1}{2}\text{ord}\,\omega.

(214)

Thus from
\frac{da}{d\tau}=\frac{da}{du}\frac{du}{d\tau}
it is clear that

\text{ord}\,\frac{da}{d\tau}=-\frac{1}{2}\text{ord}\,\omega+\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}-1.

(215)

The order of vanishing of the physical discriminant depends on the type of AD point as well. We can use the identity
\eta^{24}\propto\Delta/\omega^{6}

(
Aspman:2021vhs
, Equation (3.9))
, and using the fact that
\eta(\tau_{0})\neq 0
, we have

\text{ord}\,\Delta=6\,\text{ord}\,\omega.

(216)

We have thus calculated the order of vanishing of the
u
-plane measure
\nu
(
209
) for any given elliptic point
\tau_{0}
, it is

\text{ord}\,\nu=\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}-1+\frac{1}{4}\left(3\sigma+\chi-2\right)\text{ord}\,\omega.

(217)

We can further use that
\chi+\sigma=4
for manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}=1
, since otherwise the
u
-plane integral vanishes. Thus

\boxed{\text{ord}\,\nu=\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}-1+\frac{\sigma+1}{2}\text{ord}\,\omega.}

(218)

It is important to notice that
\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}
and
\text{ord}\,\omega
are not given uniquely for a type (
II
,
III
and
IV
) of AD point. Rather, they can differ depending on the configuration containing a given Kodaira singularity. The ‘degree of freedom’ is the undetermined order of vanishing of the Weierstraß invariant
g_{2}
or
g_{3}
on the base
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Since for the SQCD curves
g_{2}
and
g_{3}
are polynomials of degree
2
and
3
however, this order of vanishing is greatly restricted. Thus in practise, for the curves under consideration, both
\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}
and
\text{ord}\,\omega
can take at most two values for a given type
II
,
III
or
IV
. We summarise these values in the following table
15
.

26

26

We do not know if the second possibility for type
III
is realised, but are also not able to rule it out.

We stress again that this result (

II^{(*)}
,
III^{(*)}
or
IV^{(*)}
(including the Minahan–Nemeschansky theories), and will be useful for the study of other

The possible orders of vanishing of the SW curves at AD points

of the Weierstraß invariants

and the discriminant

as a function of
