.

For the manifolds under consideration, the signature is bounded as
\sigma\leq 1
. Since
\text{ord}\,\omega>0
, this implies that the measure can become arbitrarily singular for manifolds with large
-\sigma
. Similarly, one can expect selection rules on the signature for the
u
-plane integrand to be sufficiently singular to have a nonzero residue.

Finally, if we include background fluxes in the theory, the path integral acquires couplings
\prod_{i,j=1}^{N_{f}}C_{ij}^{B({\bm{k}}_{i},{\bm{k}}_{j})}
which enter the measure factor in (
). For
N_{f}=2
with equal masses we have expressions of
C_{ij}
in terms of modular forms, see for instance (
322
). Since we do not know the coupling
v
analytically however, it is difficult to study the behaviour of those couplings at a given elliptic point
\tau_{0}
.
We hope to come back to this point in future work.

13.3
Photon path integral

Having discussed the singular behaviour of the measure factor at any given elliptic AD point, we discuss in this subsection the series expansion of the anti-derivative
\widehat{G}
of the Siegel-Narain theta function. This function has been determined in
Korpas:2019cwg
for a canonical choice of period point
J\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})
, i.e. either
J=(1,\bm{0})
for odd intersection forms on
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
, or
J=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,1,\bm{0})
for even intersection forms. Including surface observables (or elliptic arguments in general),
\widehat{G}
takes the form of a non-holomorphic completion of an Appell–Lerch sum.

Odd intersection form

Since it is rather involved to study the general expansion of
\widehat{G}
for all ’t Hooft fluxes
{\bm{\mu}}
and values or
\sigma
of
b_{2}
simultaneously, let us focus on manifolds
X
with odd intersection form and a fixed flux.
Consider the Siegel-Narain theta function for
{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{+},{\bm{\mu}}_{-})\equiv(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\dots,\frac{1}{2})\mod\mathbb{Z}^{b_{2}}
with
K=(3,1,\dots,1)
, such that
K^{2}=9-n
with
n=b_{2}^{-}
and
{\bm{\mu}}\equiv K/2\mod L
, as is the case for the del Pezzo surfaces
dP_{n}
for instance. From (
97
) and (
98
) we have

\Psi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J}(\tau,{\bm{\rho}})=if(\tau,\rho_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{b_{2}}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,\rho_{k}).

(219)

Then
\Psi_{{\bm{\mu}}}^{J}(\tau,{\bm{\rho}})=\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{G}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})
with

\widehat{G}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})=i\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})\prod_{k=2}^{b_{2}}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,\rho_{k}),

(220)

where
\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})
is defined in (
285
).
Since
\rho\to 0
for
\tau\to\tau_{0}
, we consider the limit of (
220
) for
\rho\to 0
.
As explained in detail in Appendix
D.4
, the function
\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})
has a well-defined Taylor series in
(\rho,\bar{\rho})
, and for
\rho,\bar{\rho}\to 0
we have
\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})\to\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
. On the level of the Siegel-Narain theta function
\Psi_{\bm{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\rho)
, the limit
\rho\to 0
exists since
\Psi
is a well-defined function on
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}^{b_{2}}

ZwegersThesis
;
zagier2009
;
Korpas:2017qdo
: For
\rho=0
,
\Psi(\tau,0)
is a non-holomorphic vector-valued modular form for
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
of weight
(\frac{n}{2},0)
.

We can in fact study the Taylor series of the whole anti-derivative (
220
) in
\rho
, while we focus on the leading contribution as
\rho\to 0
. For the Taylor series of the elliptic Jacobi theta series (
247
),

\vartheta_{1}(\tau,\rho)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\partial_{\rho}^{n}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,0)\rho^{n},

(221)

we determine

\partial_{\rho}^{n}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,0)=i(2\pi i)^{n}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(-1)^{r-\frac{1}{2}}r^{n}q^{r^{2}/2}.

(222)

In the limit
\rho\to 0
, we are primarily interested in the first nonzero term,

\vartheta_{1}(\tau,\rho)=-2\pi\eta(\tau)^{3}\rho+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{3}).

(223)

The Taylor series of
\widehat{G}
at
\rho=0
then reads

\displaystyle\widehat{G}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})=(-2\pi)^{b_{2}^{-}}\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau})\eta(\tau)^{3b_{2}^{-}}\rho_{2}\dots\rho_{b_{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{k}^{2}).

(224)

Chowla–Selberg formula

Let us determine the holomorphic expansion of this function at
\tau=\tau_{0}
. The function
\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
is a non-holomorphic modular form. If its value at the elliptic point
\tau_{0}
is nonzero, it has a regular Taylor expansion in
\tau-\tau_{0}
starting with a constant term. If it vanishes at
\tau_{0}
on the other hand, it has a positive order of vanishing, shifting the holomorphic series of the remainder of the integrand. In order to find the leading term in the expansion, we thus have to evaluate it at
\tau=\tau_{0}
.

In many cases, explicit values of holomorphic modular forms at the elliptic fixed points
i
and
\alpha
of
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
are specific combinations of powers of
\pi
and the Euler gamma function
\Gamma
evaluated at rational numbers. This is a consequence of the
Chowla–Selberg formula
, which describes the value of modular forms at complex multiplication points in terms of products of gamma functions.
27
27

27

See Appendix
D.1
for a survey on the Chowla–Selberg formula
The Chowla–Selberg formula expresses the value of any modular form
f
of weight
k
at a
complex multiplication
(CM) point
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{H}
as

f(\mathfrak{z})\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\,\Omega_{K}^{k},

(225)

where
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
is the field of all algebraic numbers
28
28

28

An algebraic number is a number which is a root of a non-zero polynomial in one variable with integer coefficients
, and
\Omega_{K}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
is a complex number that depends only on the imaginary quadratic field
K
containing
\mathfrak{z}
. Any
\tau_{0}
in the
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
-orbit of the
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
elliptic points
i
or
\alpha=e^{2\pi i/3}
is a CM point, and the period
\Omega
is computed from the Chowla–Selberg formula (Theorem
1
) as

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{4})^{2}}{4\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}},

(226)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\frac{3^{\frac{1}{4}}\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{3})^{3}}{4\pi^{2}},

where
\mathbb{Q}(i)
and
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
are the quadratic fields generated by the CM points
i
and
\alpha
.
Since the values (
226
) are known, the value
f(\mathfrak{z})
of some elliptic point
\mathfrak{z}
of a modular form
f
of weight
k
can now more easily be determined, since
f(\mathfrak{z})\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{z})}^{-k}
is an algebraic number. These can often be found using an integer relation algorithm such as the LLL-algorithm (see for instance
Cohen1993
).

The Chowla–Selberg formula is generally stated to apply to holomorphic modular forms with algebraic Fourier coefficients for finite index subgroups of
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.
Generalisations to non-holomorphic modular forms
bruinier2013
and mock modular forms
GUERZHOY2010
;
CHOI2018428
have been explored in the literature, however, the full range of validity of this formula is possiblly not determined yet.

A somewhat trivial example of the Chowla–Selberg formula concerns the anti-derivative
\widehat{H}
. Its values at the elliptic points are

\displaystyle\widehat{H}(i,\bar{i})

\displaystyle=0,

(227)

\displaystyle\widehat{H}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})

\displaystyle=0.

These can be found from the modular transformations of
\widehat{H}
(see (
263
)): As it is a non-holomorphic modular form for
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
of weight
(\frac{1}{2},0)
, one evaluates the modular
S
and
ST
transformation of
\widehat{H}
at their fixed points
i
and
\alpha
, and the multipliers and weight factors differing from one results in
\widehat{H}(i,\bar{i})
and
\widehat{H}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})
vanishing.
The coefficients of higher orders in the Taylor expansion can also be found, see Appendix
D.4
, and for instance (
274
) and Table
16
. In particular,
\widehat{H}
has a non-holomorphic Taylor series at
(\tau,\bar{\tau})=(\tau_{0},\bar{\tau}_{0})
with non-zero coefficient of
(\tau-\tau_{0})^{1}(\bar{\tau}-\bar{\tau}_{0})^{0}
. Thus the holomorphic order of vanishing is
\text{ord}\,\widehat{H}=1
.

The property that anti-derivatives of the photon path integral vanishes at elliptic points is certainly not true in general. Indeed, anti-derivatives are only defined up to integration constants, which can change properties of distinct anti-derivatives drastically.

Integration constant

There are two obvious way in which an integration ‘constant’ can be added. First, we can add to
\widehat{G}
a weakly holomorphic modular function
g
of weight
\frac{1}{2}
. Indeed, for this new anti-derivative
\widehat{h}=f(\tau)(\widehat{G}(\tau,\bar{\tau})+g(\tau))
, it remains true that
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{h}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\mathcal{I}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
. Regarding the functions
H
and
F
defined in Section
10.2
, and their completions
\widehat{H}
and
\widehat{F}
defined in Appendix
D.3
, their difference is the weakly holomorphic modular form
(\vartheta_{2}^{4}+\vartheta_{3}^{4})/\eta^{3}
, as stated precisely in (
86
) and (
265
).
By numerically evaluating the Eichler integral and using the Chowla--Selberg formula, we find the following values
29
29

29

We omit the anti-holomorphic dependence in the notation.
,

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(i)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(i+1)

\displaystyle=0,

(228)

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(e^{\frac{2}{3}\pi i})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\frac{5}{24}\pi i}}{2^{\frac{2}{3}}\sqrt{3}}\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(e^{\frac{1}{3}\pi i})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{5}{24}\pi i}}{2^{\frac{2}{3}}\sqrt{3}}\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{i}{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{3}{8}\pi i}}{2^{\frac{3}{4}}}\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}e^{\frac{1}{6}\pi i})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{13}{24}\pi i}}{2^{\frac{2}{3}}\sqrt[4]{3}}\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

As is apparent, all coefficients of the Chowla–Selberg periods are algebraic numbers. For example, the coefficient of
\widehat{F}(e^{\frac{2}{3}\pi i})
has minimal polynomial
2^{16}3^{12}x^{24}+1
.
Since
\widehat{F}
is a non-holomorphic modular form of weight
\left(\tfrac{1}{2},0\right)
for
\Gamma^{0}(2)
(which is of index 3 in
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
), these six values determine
\widehat{F}(\tau_{0})
for any elliptic point
\tau_{0}
of
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. In contrast to the modular form
\widehat{H}
, the holomorphic order of vanishing of
\widehat{F}
at any
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
elliptic point is zero.

Alternatively, we can also add a weakly holomorphic function of weight 2 to
\widehat{h}
directly.
The difference between the two places where the holomorphic function is inserted is of course equivalent.
The basis for the space of weakly holomorphic modular forms for
\text{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
is given by the derivatives of powers of
j
,
\partial_{\tau}j^{l}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
. These are given by

\partial_{\tau}j^{l}=-2\pi i\,l\,j^{l}\frac{E_{6}}{E_{4}},

(229)

with are holomorphic at
i
and
\alpha
. In fact, they vanish at both
i
and
\alpha
(we have
\text{ord}\,\partial_{\tau}j(\tau)^{l}=3l-1
at
\tau=\alpha
and
\text{ord}\,\partial_{\tau}j(\tau)^{l}=1
at
\tau=i
).
Thus they do not alter the residue.

13.4
AD contribution

We can now combine the results from the above considerations regarding the leading behaviour of the anti-derivative
\widehat{h}
(
224
).
Recall from (
) that
\text{ord}\,\rho=\frac{1}{2}\text{ord}\,\omega
. The Dedekind eta-function
\eta
is nowhere vanishing in
\mathbb{H}
. Thus from (
224
) we have

\displaystyle\text{ord}\,\widehat{G}

\displaystyle=\text{ord}\,\widehat{H}+b_{2}^{-}\text{ord}\,\rho

(230)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1-\sigma}{2}\text{ord}\,\omega.

From the anti-derivative (
206
) we are missing the exponentiated observables,
2pu(\tau)/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}
and
{\bm{x}}^{2}G_{N_{f}}(\tau)
. Of course, for
\tau
near
\tau_{0}
the first exponential is regular, since
2pu(\tau)/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}\to 2pu(\tau_{0})/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}
. For the surface observable, we need to study the contact term (
), which contains the three functions
E_{2}
,
\omega
and
u
. In Lemma
2
in Appendix
D
we prove that
E_{2}(\tau_{0})\neq 0
for any elliptic point
\tau_{0}
. Thus

\text{ord}\,G_{N_{f}}=\text{ord}\,\omega.

(231)

Since the contact term is exponentiated,
e^{{\bm{x}}^{2}G_{N_{f}}(\tau)}
however becomes a constant as
\tau\to\tau_{0}
.
30
30

30

This should be modified when the exponential is expanded and we study a particular coefficient in the series in
{\bm{x}}
. Here, we are interested mainly in the generating function.
This shows

\text{ord}\,e^{2pu(\tau)/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}+{\bm{x}}^{2}G_{N_{f}}(\tau)}=0.

(232)

Combining now (
218
) and (
230
) in (
206
) we have
\text{ord}\,\widehat{h}=\text{ord}\,\nu+\text{ord}\,\widehat{G}
, which gives

\displaystyle\boxed{\text{ord}\,\widehat{h}=\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}+\text{ord}\,\omega.}

(233)

Strikingly, the dependence on the signature of the manifold drops out. Moreover, this value is bounded from below,

\text{ord}\,\widehat{h}\geq 1

(234)

as is clear from Table
15
.
31
31

31

If we allow for other elliptic surfaces with type
II
,
III
and
IV
singular fibres, the value of
\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}+\text{ord}\,\omega
is only bounded from below by 0, since it converges to 0 for either
o_{2}\to\infty
or
o_{3}\to\infty
, whichever is undetermined by the Kodaira classification. For
rational
elliptic surfaces, the Weierstraß invariants
g_{2}
and
g_{3}
are polynomials of degree at most
4
and
6
, and thus
\text{ord}\,\mathtt{u}+\text{ord}\,\omega
is bounded from below by a positive rational number.
In fact, for
any
elliptic surface containing a singular fibre of type
II
,
III
or
IV
, the order of vanishing (
233
) is strictly positive.

We have shown that in the absence of background fluxes, for a fixed ’t Hooft flux on arbitrary four-manifolds with odd intersection form, the anti-derivative of the
u
-plane integrand has an expansion (
207
) with

d_{0}(-1,0)=0.

(235)

That is, according to (
208
) the
u
-plane integrals for these manifolds does not acquire a contribution from any possible AD point in
\mathcal{N}=2
SQCD,

[\mathcal{I}]_{\tau_{0}}=0.

(236)

This result is not entirely unexpected, since this class of manifolds also includes the del Pezzo surfaces
dP_{n}
, for which there is no SW contribution and thus also no contribution from the strong coupling
I_{r}
cusps of the
u
-plane. Of course, AD points are collisions of
I_{r}
and
I_{r^{\prime}}
cusps, and it is conceivable that if there is no contribution from those singularities if they are separated, their collision does not produce a contribution either. See also Section
10.3
for the related discussion on the collision of mutually local
I_{r}
and
I_{r}^{\prime}
singularities to an
I_{r+r^{\prime}}
singularity.

14
Conclusion and Outlook

In this part II, we formulated and evaluated topological partition functions for massive SQCD on compact four-manifolds.

As addressed in the introduction, topological correlators take the form of a continuous
u
-plane integral and a finite sum over the Coulomb branch singularities. In part I, we argued that the
u
-plane integral can itself be expressed as a sum over the CB singularities (see (
)). The SW curve fibered over the Coulomb branch constitutes an elliptic surface
\mathcal{S}
, whose singular fibres correspond to the CB singularities. The possible singular fibres
F_{i}
of an elliptic surface fall into Kodaira’s classification. To any surface
\mathcal{S}
, we can associate its configuration of singular fibres
\{F_{1},\dots,F_{k}\}
. We may therefore express topological correlation functions
Z_{\bm{\mu}}^{X}
for fixed fluxes
{\bm{\mu}}
on a given manifold
X
as a sum

Z_{\bm{\mu}}^{X}[\mathcal{S}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}},i}^{J}
,
where
\Phi_{{\bm{\mu}},i}^{J}
is the contribution from the singular fibre
F_{i}
. This proposal is valid for massive SU(2) SQCD, as well as the topological
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory
Manschot:2021qqe
.

An important consistency check of this formulation and of correlation functions in general is the invariance under reparametrisations of the theory.
For a given 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theory, there can be multiple SW curves describing the same local low-energy physics
Seiberg:1994aj
;
Moore:1997pc
;
Shapere:2008zf
;
Gaiotto:2010be
;
Aharony:2013hda
;
Manschot:2021qqe
.
32
32

32

This was first noticed in the pure 4d SU(2) theory, where the SW curves associated with
\Gamma(2)

Seiberg:1994rs
and
\Gamma^{0}(4)

Seiberg:1994aj
monodromy groups were understood to be related by a 2-isogeny and a change of normalisation of the periods.
Recently, the choice of SW curve has been identified with the choice of global structure of a rank one 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theory, i.e. the spectrum of line operators
Argyres:2022kon
. The global forms of a given local theory are related by gauging 1-form symmetries. On the level of the SW curve, the relation is provided by compositions of isogenies
Closset:2023pmc
. An isogeny is given by a quotient
\mathcal{S}/\alpha
of the elliptic surface
\mathcal{S}
by an automorphism
\alpha
induced by the 1-form symmetry group, which is a particular subgroup of the Mordell-Weil group
\text{MW}(\mathcal{S})

Closset:2021lhd
. The Mordell-Weil group sits in a larger group
\text{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
of automorphisms of the surface
\mathcal{S}
itself, which encodes the 1-form as well as the 0-form symmetries of the theory
Caorsi:2018ahl
;
Caorsi:2019vex
;
Closset:2023pmc
;
ACFMM23
. Formulating
\mathcal{N}=2
theories on (especially non-spin) compact four-manifolds provides an ideal testing ground for the effects of such higher-form symmetries and their anomalies
Aharony:2013hda
;
Gaiotto:2014kfa
;
Cordova:2018acb
;
Ang:2019txy
;
Brennan:2022tyl
;
Brennan:2023kpo
;
Brennan:2023vsa
. It would thus be interesting and important to explore the action of
\text{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
on topological correlators.

Formulating superconformal field theories on four-manifolds has been proven to be a fruitful tool for understanding inherent features about the theories themselves, as well as the topological invariants of the underlying spacetime. An example of the former is the derivation of the conformal and flavour central charges of 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFTs from the topological twist, by comparing the
U(1)_{R}
anomaly of the Coulomb branch measure with the conformal anomaly of the trace of the energy-momentum tensor generated by a background gravitational field

Shapere:2008zf
. Given these central charges of an SCFT, topological correlators satisfy a selection rule due to the
U(1)_{R}
anomaly
Aharony_2008
;
Xie:2013jc
;
Shapere:2008zf
;
Marino:1998bm
;
Moore:2017cmm
;
Gukov:2017
;
Witten:1995gf
. For rank 1
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFTs, this selection rule identifies the anomalous
R
-charge of the vacuum with the
R
-charge of the operator whose correlation function is evaluated.

Moreover, the existence of superconformal theories on a four-manifold
X
can give new structural
insights on the invariants of
X
. For the simplest Argyres-Douglas SCFT
H_{0}
, it was found that the regularity of topological partition functions in the critical limit imposes
sum rules
on the classical Seiberg-Witten invariants
Marino:1998uy
.
All other Argyres-Douglas theories, including the
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
theories appearing as critical points in SU(2) SQCD, are associated with noncompact Higgs branches. For those higher AD theories, postulating analogous sum rules is therefore more intricate. Nevertheless, identifying the hypermultiplet masses as equivariant deformation parameters, the Higgs branch singularities can be made precise
LoNeSha
;
Dedushenko:2017tdw
.
We plan to discuss the interplay between Higgs branch singularities, sum rules and selection rules in more detail in a future work
AFMM:future
.

While coupling SQCD to background fluxes for the flavour group makes the theory well-defined on non-spin manifolds
X
with arbitrary ’t Hooft flux, it renders the evaluation of topological correlators more involved. One possible reason for this is that the structure of poles and zeros of the background couplings can interfere with the pole structure of the mock modular forms used in the evaluation. Another obstruction comes from the branch points of the integrand, which only in the absence of background fluxes are guaranteed to not affect the result. In Appendix
F
, we analyse the various issues and discuss possible resolutions. It would be desirable to have a more systematic treatment of this aspect.

The methods we employ to evaluate the topological partition functions of
\mathcal{N}=2
SQCD are largely independent on the specific form of the underlying SW curves. The SW curves for other 4d rank 1
\mathcal{N}=2
theories are (generally non-modular) elliptic surfaces, where the ‘remaining’ Kodaira singularities absent in SQCD are the types
II^{*}
,
III^{*}
and
IV^{*}
, which are associated with the Minahan–Nemeschansky (MN) SCFTs
Minahan:1996fg
;
Minahan:1996cj
. It would therefore be interesting to formulate topological partition functions of other 4d rank 1
\mathcal{N}=2
theories, including all rank 1
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFTs, which are classified
Argyres:2015ffa
;
Argyres:2015gha
;
Argyres:2016xmc
;
Argyres:2016xua
.

Another interesting avenue for future research is the 5-dimensional uplift of 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theories, for instance the 5-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
SU(2) gauge theory compactified on a circle

Nekrasov_1998
;
Ganor:1996pc
. More generally, a family of 4d
\mathcal{N}=2
theories is represented by the Kaluza-Klein theories obtained by a circle compactification of the 5d
\mathcal{N}=1
SU(2) theory with fundamental flavours, which have
E_{n}
flavour symmetry
Seiberg:1996bd
;
Morrison:1996xf
. See

Chang:2017cdx
;
Closset:2022vjj
;
kim2023
;
Closset:2021lhd
for related recent works on 5d topological partition functions.

Appendix D
Modular forms II

In this Appendix, we discuss aspects of modular forms relevant for this Part II. It complements Appendix
of Part I.
Section
D.1
gives a review of the Chowla–Selberg formula, which is used in Section
13
in the expansion of the photon path integral around the AD points.
In Section
D.2
, we discuss additional properties of modular forms, such as derivatives and special values. These are used in Section
13
for the expansion of modular forms around elliptic points, as well as in Section
10
in the study of anti-derivatives of the
u
-plane integrand. Finally, Sections
D.3
and
D.4
list properties of mock modular forms and mock Jacobi forms relevant to the evaluation of
u
-plane integrals.

D.1
Chowla–Selberg formula

Explicit values of modular forms at the elliptic fixed points
i
and
\alpha=e^{2\pi i/3}
can often be expressed as specific combinations of powers of
\pi
and the Euler gamma function
\Gamma
. This is a consequence of the Chowla–Selberg formula, which describes the value of modular forms at complex multiplication points in terms of products of gamma functions. In order to understand the final formula, we need to introduce some basic notions. We follow
(
Bruinier08
,
\lx@sectionsign 1
Section 6)
. Generalisations to non-holomorphic modular forms
bruinier2013
and mock modular forms
GUERZHOY2010
;
CHOI2018428
have been explored. In physics it has been used in the context of the BCOV conjecture
Eriksson:2019jhg
.
Further results can be found in
Chowla1949
;
Shimura2010
;
DHoker:2022dxx
;
shimura1977derivatives
;
MORENO1983226
;
lerch1897
;
Waldschmidt2006
;
LI2022108264
;
BRUINIER201738
;
voight2014
;
Cohen1993
.

Elliptic curves over
\mathbb{C}
can be viewed as quotients
E=\mathbb{C}/\Lambda
, with
\Lambda
a lattice in
\mathbb{C}
. If
E^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}/\Lambda^{\prime}
is another curve and
\lambda
a complex number with
\lambda\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda^{\prime}
, then multiplication by
\lambda
induces an algebraic map from
E
to
E^{\prime}
. In particular, if
\lambda\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda
, then we get a map from
E
to itself.
Elliptic curves
E=\mathbb{C}/\Lambda
which satisfy
\lambda\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda
for some non-real value of
\lambda
are said to admit
complex multiplication
.

We can think of the upper half-plane
\mathbb{H}
as a moduli space for elliptic curves. Then the points in
\mathbb{H}
that correspond to elliptic curves with complex multiplication (from now on called CM points) are the numbers
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{H}
which satisfy a quadratic equation over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Examples of CM points are the
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
-images
\gamma\tau_{0}
of the
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
elliptic points
\tau_{0}
. We can easily check that for any
\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
, the elliptic points
\gamma i
and
\gamma\alpha
satisfy

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(c^{2}+d^{2})(\gamma i)^{2}-2(ac+bd)\gamma i+a^{2}+b^{2},

(237)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(c^{2}-cd+d^{2})(\gamma\alpha)^{2}+(b(c-2d)+a(d-2c))\gamma\alpha+a^{2}-ab+b^{2}.

The equations are strictly quadratic, since
c^{2}+d^{2}=|ci+d|^{2}
and
c^{2}-cd+d^{2}=|c\alpha+d|^{2}
.

Definition 1

(Discriminant of CM point)
.

The discriminant of a CM point is the smallest discriminant of the quadratic polynomial over
\mathbb{Z}
of which it is a root.

For the elliptic points in (
237
), we can easily compute the discriminants
-4
for
\mathfrak{z}=\gamma i
and
-3
for
\mathfrak{z}=\gamma\alpha
. Generally, discriminants of CM points are congruent to
0
or
1
modulo
4
. For example,
i=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{-4}
and
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}(-1+\sqrt{-3})
.

There is a one-to-one relation between primitive positive definite binary quadratic forms of discriminant
D
and the set
\mathfrak{Z}_{D}
of CM points with discriminant
D
. For fixed
D
, there are only finitely many classes of such quadratic forms with discriminant
D
. Their number is the
class number

h(D)
of the discriminant
D
.
33
33

33

A generalisation of the class numbers are the Hurwitz class numbers, which are class numbers weighted by the inverse of the order of the automorphism group in
\Gamma_{1}
.
Due to the bijection with
\mathfrak{Z}_{D}
, the class number
h(D)=|\Gamma_{1}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}_{D}|
is given by the cardinality of the set of
\Gamma_{1}
-equivalent elements in
\mathfrak{Z}_{D}
.

CM points
\mathfrak{z}
have many special properties. For instance, the value of
j(\mathfrak{z})
or that of any other modular function with algebraic coefficients evaluated at
\mathfrak{z}
is an
algebraic number
34
34

34

An algebraic number is a number which is a root of a non-zero polynomial in one variable with integer coefficients. The set of all algebraic numbers forms an algebraic field, commonly denoted by
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.
. Furthermore, these
singular moduli

j(\mathfrak{z})
give explicit generators of the class fields of imaginary quadratic fields.

Many results depend on the following

Proposition 1
.

Let
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{H}
be a CM point. Then
j(\mathfrak{z})
is an algebraic number.

This is quite trivially true for the
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
elliptic points:
j(\gamma i)=j(i)=12^{3}
and
j(\gamma\alpha)=j(\alpha)=0
, which are both roots of degree 1 polynomials over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We can use the fact that if
\mathfrak{z}
is a CM point, it is fixed
M\mathfrak{z}=\mathfrak{z}
by some integer-valued matrix
M
with positive determinant. Then
j(\tau)
and
j(M\tau)
are both modular functions on the subgroup
\Gamma_{1}\cap M^{-1}\Gamma_{1}M
of finite index in
\Gamma_{1}
, so they are algebraically dependent and thus there is a polynomial
P(X,Y)
in two variables such that
P(j(M\tau),j(\tau))=0
. Since the Fourier expansion of
j
has integer coefficients, the coefficients of
P
can be chosen to lie in
\mathbb{Q}
. Since
M\mathfrak{z}=\mathfrak{z}
,
j(\mathfrak{z})
is a root of the non-zero polynomial
P(X,X)\in\mathbb{Q}[X]
.
∎

The idea easily generalises to subgroups of
\Gamma_{1}
:

Proposition 2
.

Let
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{H}
be a CM point and
f
a modular function for a finite index subgroup of
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
with algebraic Fourier coefficients. Then
f(\mathfrak{z})\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Interestingly, this statement is equivalent to the following

Proposition 3
.

Let
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{H}
be a CM point and
f
a modular form of weight
k
for a finite index subgroup of
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
with algebraic Fourier coefficients. Then there is a complex number
\Omega_{\mathfrak{z}}
(depending on
\mathfrak{z}
only), such that
f(\mathfrak{z})\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\cdot\Omega_{\mathfrak{z}}^{k}
.

Proposition
3
implies Proposition
2
by specialising to
k=0
. Proposition
2
implies Proposition
3
on the other hand since if
f\in M_{k}
and
g\in M_{l}
then
f^{l}/g^{k}
is a modular function and is thus algebraic at
\mathfrak{z}
, such that
f(\mathfrak{z})^{\frac{1}{k}}
and
g(\mathfrak{z})^{\frac{1}{l}}
are algebraically proportional.

The number
\Omega_{\mathfrak{z}}
is defined only up to an algebraic number, and does not change up to an algebraic number if
\mathfrak{z}
is mapped to
M\mathfrak{z}
with some positive determinant integer valued matrix
M
. Thus Proposition
3
lifts to arbitrary imaginary quadratic fields
K
,

Proposition 4
.

Let
K
be an imaginary quadratic field. Then there exists a number
\Omega_{K}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that

f(\mathfrak{z})\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\,\Omega_{K}^{k}

(238)

for all
\mathfrak{z}\in K\cap\mathbb{H}
, all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and all modular forms f of weight
k
with algebraic Fourier coefficients.

To find
\Omega_{K}
, we can compute
f(\mathfrak{z})
for any modular form. For instance, we can choose the non-holomorphic modular function
\Phi(\tau)=y|\eta(\tau)|^{4}
. As described above, for general
D
we can look at CM points with discriminant
D
, of which modulo
\Gamma_{1}
there are
h(D)
, the class number of
K
. Since there is no distinguished element of
\mathfrak{Z}_{D}
, we take the product over
\Gamma_{1}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}_{D}
and take the
h^{\prime}(D)
-th root, where
h^{\prime}(D)=h(D)/\frac{1}{2}w(D)
is either
\frac{1}{3}
,
\frac{1}{2}
or
h(K)
depending on the value
D=-3
,
D=-4
or
D<-4
(these numbers take into account the multiplicities of the elliptic fixed points
i
and
\alpha
).
Then we have the following

Theorem 1

(Chowla–Selberg formula)
.

Let
K
be an imaginary quadratic field with discriminant
D
. Then

\prod_{\mathfrak{z}\in\Gamma_{1}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}_{D}}\left(4\pi\sqrt{|D|}\Phi(\mathfrak{z})\right)^{2/w(D)}=\prod_{j=1}^{|D|-1}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{j}{|D|}\right)^{\chi_{D}(j)},

(239)

where
\chi_{D}(\cdot)\coloneqq\left(\frac{D}{\cdot}\right)
(the Legendre symbol) is the quadratic character associated to
K
, and
\Gamma
is the Euler gamma function.

This formula was essentially found by Lerch
lerch1897
and rediscovered by Chowla and Selberg
Chowla1949
in the study of Epstein’s
\zeta
-function.

Since
\eta^{2}
is a weight
1
modular form for
\Gamma_{1}
, we can find the number
\Omega_{K}
.

Corollary 1
.

The number
\Omega_{K}
in Proposition
4
can be chosen to be

\Omega_{K}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi|D|}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{|D|-1}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{j}{|D|}\right)^{\chi_{D}(j)}\right)^{1/2h^{\prime}(D)}.

(240)

For the two CM points
i\in\mathfrak{Z}_{-4}
and
\alpha\in\mathfrak{Z}_{-3}
generating the quadratic imaginary fields
\mathbb{Q}(i)
and
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
, we can now easily compute their corresponding Chowla--Selberg periods
35
35

35

They are periods in the sense of
Kontsevich2001
.
,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{4})^{2}}{4\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}},

(241)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\frac{3^{\frac{1}{4}}\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{3})^{3}}{4\pi^{2}}.

The fact that the periods for the quadratic fields
\mathbb{Q}(i)
and
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
can be written by powers of a single
\Gamma
function is a consequence of Euler’s reflection formula. For larger discriminant CM points, the periods are generally products of
\Gamma
-functions at multiple rational values.

Since the values (
241
) are known, the value
f(\mathfrak{z})
of some elliptic point
\mathfrak{z}
of a modular form
f
of weight
k
can now more easily be determined, since
f(\mathfrak{z})\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{z})}^{-k}
is an algebraic number, which often can be determined using an integer relation algorithm such as the LLL-algorithm
(
Cohen1993
, Section 2.6.1)
. For instance, we have

E_{4}(i)=12\,\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}^{4},\qquad E_{6}(\alpha)=12^{\frac{3}{2}}\,\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}^{6},

(242)

where the algebraic coefficients are solutions to the linear and quadratic equations
x=12
and
x^{2}=12^{3}
.

D.2
Derivatives and theta functions

Derivatives of modular functions are described by Ramanujan’s differential operator. It increases the holomorphic weight by 2 and it can be explicitly constructed using the theory of Hecke operators
ono2004
.
For the derivatives of the Jacobi theta functions (
), one finds

\displaystyle D\vartheta_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{6}\vartheta_{2}^{4}\left(E_{2}+\vartheta_{3}^{4}+\vartheta_{4}^{4}\right),

(243)

\displaystyle D\vartheta_{3}^{4}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{6}\vartheta_{3}^{4}\left(E_{2}+\vartheta_{2}^{4}-\vartheta_{4}^{4}\right),

\displaystyle D\vartheta_{4}^{4}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{6}\vartheta_{4}^{4}\left(E_{2}-\vartheta_{2}^{4}-\vartheta_{3}^{4}\right),

where
D\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{d}{d\tau}=q\frac{d}{dq}
and
E_{2}
is the quasi-modular Eisenstein series (
) of weight 2. The derivatives of
\vartheta_{j}^{2}
are given by
D\vartheta_{j}^{2}=\frac{D\vartheta_{j}^{4}}{2\vartheta_{j}^{2}}
and therefore also in the ring of quasi-modular forms.

All quasi-modular forms for
\Gamma_{1}=SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
can be expressed as polynomials in
E_{2}
,
E_{4}
and
E_{6}
.
Derivatives of modular forms are quasi-modular in a precise way:

Lemma 1
.

Let
f\in M_{k}(\Gamma_{1})
be a modular form of weight
k
. Then the
Serre derivative

\vartheta_{k}f\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\pi i}f^{\prime}-\frac{k}{12}E_{2}f
is a modular form
\vartheta_{k}f\in M_{k+2}(\Gamma_{1})
.

As a consequence, the quasi-modular forms for
\Gamma_{1}
form a ring
\widetilde{M_{*}}(\Gamma_{1})=\mathbb{C}[E_{2},E_{4},E_{6}]
. We have the following

Proposition 5
.

The ring
\widetilde{M_{*}}(\Gamma_{1})
is closed under differentiation. Specifically, we have

\displaystyle E_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{12}(E_{2}^{2}-E_{4}),

(244)

\displaystyle E_{4}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{3}(E_{2}E_{4}-E_{6}),

\displaystyle E_{6}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{2}(E_{2}E_{6}-E_{4}^{2}).

In the main text, we use the property that
E_{2}(\tau_{0})
is never zero for any
\tau_{0}
in the
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
orbit of an
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
-elliptic point (see also
BASRAOUI
):

Lemma 2
.

Let
\alpha=e^{2\pi i/3}
. The second Eisenstein series
E_{2}(\gamma i)\neq 0
and
E_{2}(\gamma\alpha)\neq 0
for all
\gamma\in\text{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Consider the transformation property

E_{2}(\gamma\tau)=(c\tau+d)^{2}E_{2}(\tau)-\frac{6i}{\pi}c(c\tau+d)

(245)

for
\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.
From
E_{2}(i)=\frac{3}{\pi}
and
E_{2}(\alpha)=\frac{2\sqrt{3}}{\pi}
we can easily compute

\displaystyle E_{2}(\gamma i)

\displaystyle=E_{2}(i)|ci+d|^{2},

(246)

\displaystyle E_{2}(\gamma\alpha)

\displaystyle=E_{2}(\alpha)|c\alpha+d|^{2}.

None of the factors on the rhs are zero, since
c,d\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Elliptic Jacobi theta functions

We use also the elliptic Jacobi theta functions
\vartheta_{i}(\tau,v)
,
\vartheta_{j}:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
,
j=1,\dots,4
, which we define as

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v)=i\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(-1)^{r-\frac{1}{2}}q^{r^{2}/2}e^{2\pi irv},\\
&\vartheta_{2}(\tau,v)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}q^{r^{2}/2}e^{2\pi irv},\\
&\vartheta_{3}(\tau,v)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}q^{n^{2}/2}e^{2\pi inv},\\
&\vartheta_{4}(\tau,v)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}q^{n^{2}/2}e^{2\pi inv}.\end{split}

(247)

The Jacobi theta functions defined in (
) of Part I are
\vartheta_{j}(\tau)=\vartheta_{j}(\tau,0)
for
j=2,3,4
.
The
S
-transformation reads

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(-1/\tau,v/\tau)

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{-i\tau}e^{\pi iz/\tau^{2}}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v),

(248)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(-1/\tau,v/\tau)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-i\tau}e^{\pi iz/\tau^{2}}\vartheta_{4}(\tau,v),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(-1/\tau,v/\tau)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-i\tau}e^{\pi iz/\tau^{2}}\vartheta_{3}(\tau,v),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(-1/\tau,v/\tau)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-i\tau}e^{\pi iz/\tau^{2}}\vartheta_{2}(\tau,v).

The periodicity in
v
is

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v+1)

\displaystyle=-\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v),

(249)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(\tau,v+1)

\displaystyle=-\vartheta_{2}(\tau,v),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(\tau,v+1)

\displaystyle=+\vartheta_{3}(\tau,v),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(\tau,v+1)

\displaystyle=+\vartheta_{4}(\tau,v),

while the map
v\mapsto v+\tau
sends

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v+\tau)

\displaystyle=-e^{-\pi i(\tau+2v)}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v),

(250)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(\tau,v+\tau)

\displaystyle=+e^{-\pi i(\tau+2v)}\vartheta_{2}(\tau,v),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(\tau,v+\tau)

\displaystyle=+e^{-\pi i(\tau+2v)}\vartheta_{3}(\tau,v),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(\tau,v+\tau)

\displaystyle=-e^{-\pi i(\tau+2v)}\vartheta_{4}(\tau,v).

Their zeros are

\begin{split}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,m+n\tau)&=0,\\
\vartheta_{2}(\tau,m+\tfrac{1}{2}+n\tau)&=0,\\
\vartheta_{3}(\tau,m+\tfrac{1}{2}+(n+\tfrac{1}{2})\tau)&=0,\\
\vartheta_{4}(\tau,m+(n+\tfrac{1}{2})\tau)&=0,\\
\end{split}

(251)

with
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
. The elliptic Jacobi theta functions satisfy various generalisations of the abstruse identity (
). We use the following identity,

\vartheta_{2}(\tau,0)^{2}\vartheta_{2}(\tau,v)^{2}+\vartheta_{4}(\tau,0)^{2}\vartheta_{4}(\tau,v)^{2}=\vartheta_{3}(\tau,0)^{2}\vartheta_{3}(\tau,v)^{2}.

(252)

D.3
Mock modular forms

In this Appendix, we introduce various mock modular forms and their relations that are useful for the evaluation of
u
-plane integrals. For more comprehensive treatments, we refer the reader to the available literature. See for instance
Larry
;
ZwegersThesis
;
Dabholkar:2012nd
;
zagier2009
.
In the context of elliptic AD points, we are also interested in evaluating their modular completions at the elliptic fixed points in the fundamental domain.

The mock modular forms
F
and
H

Following
ZwegersThesis
, we define the Appell–Lerch sum
M(\tau,u,v)
as
36
36

36

See also
Korpas:2019cwg
for more details.

M(\tau,u,v)=\frac{e^{\pi iu}}{\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}q^{n(n+1)/2}e^{2\pi inv}}{1-e^{2\pi iu}q^{n}}.

(253)

The important property of the Appell–Lerch function is that if we add the function

R(\tau,\bar{\tau},u,\bar{u})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathrm{sgn}(n)-\text{Erf}\left((n+a)\sqrt{2\pi y}\right)\right)(-1)^{n-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-2\pi iun}q^{-n^{2}/2},

(254)

the completion

\begin{split}\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})&=-ie^{\pi i\nu}q^{-\nu^{2}/2}w^{-\nu}\left(M(\tau,\rho+\mu\tau,\tfrac{1}{2}\tau)\right.\\
&\quad\left.+\tfrac{i}{2}R(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho+\nu\tau,\bar{\rho}+\nu\bar{\tau})\right),\\
\end{split}

(255)

where
\nu=\mu-\frac{1}{2}
,
w=e^{2\pi i\rho}
,
transforms as a Jacobi form.

We study in the main text the mock modular form

F(\tau)=-\frac{1}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{8}}}{1-q^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}

(256)

and its non-holomorphic completion

\widehat{F}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=F(\tau)-\frac{i}{2}\int_{-\bar{\tau}}^{i\infty}\frac{\eta(w)^{3}}{\sqrt{-i(w+\tau)}}\mathrm{d}w,

(257)

which correspond to (
255
) with
\mu=\frac{1}{2}
.
The completion
\widehat{F}
transforms as a non-holomorphic modular form of weight
\left(\tfrac{1}{2},0\right)
for
\Gamma^{0}(2)
,

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(\tau+2,\bar{\tau}+2)

\displaystyle=-i\widehat{F}(\tau,\bar{\tau}),

(258)

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(\tfrac{\tau}{\tau+1},\tfrac{\bar{\tau}}{\bar{\tau}+1})

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}}\sqrt{\tau+1}\,\widehat{F}(\tau,\bar{\tau}).

Since
\widehat{F}
does not transform under the full
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, it is useful to consider similar (mock) modular forms which do.
The holomorphic function
F
is related to the
q
-series
H^{(2)}\eqqcolon H
of Mathieu moonshine
Eguchi:2010ej
in a simple way. We have
Dabholkar:2012nd
;
Korpas:2019cwg
37
37

37

Typo in
Dabholkar:2012nd

\displaystyle H(\tau)

\displaystyle=2\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}-\frac{24}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{8}}}{1+q^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}

(259)

\displaystyle=2q^{-\frac{1}{8}}\left(-1+45q+231q^{2}+770q^{3}+2277q^{4}+\dots\right).

The
q
-series is the OEIS sequence A169717, and the Fourier coefficients are sums of dimensions of irreducible representations of the sporadic group
M_{24}
.
This function has been studied in detail in
EGUCHI1988125
;
Eguchi:1988vra
;
Eguchi:2004yi
;
Cheng:2010pq
;
Eguchi:2010fg
and appears in the elliptic genus of the
K3
sigma model with
\mathcal{N}=(4,4)
supersymmetry.
It relates to
F
as
Korpas:2019cwg

F(\tau)=\frac{1}{24}\left(H(\tau)+2\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}\right),

(260)

which gives an alternative representation for
F
,

F(\tau)=\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}}{4\eta(\tau)^{3}}-\frac{1}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{8}}}{1+q^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}.

(261)

The completion

\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=H(\tau)-12i\int_{-\bar{\tau}}^{i\infty}\frac{\eta(w)^{3}}{\sqrt{-i(w+\tau)}}\mathrm{d}w

(262)

transforms as a non-holomorphic modular form for
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
of weight
(\tfrac{1}{2},0)
,

\displaystyle\widehat{H}(\tau+1,\bar{\tau}+1)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\pi i}{4}}\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau}),

(263)

\displaystyle\widehat{H}(-1/\tau,-1/\bar{\tau})

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{-i\tau}\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau}).

Using modular transformations which fix the
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
elliptic points
\tau=i
and
\tau=\alpha
,
we can readily evaluate

\displaystyle\widehat{H}(i,\bar{i})

\displaystyle=0,

(264)

\displaystyle\widehat{H}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})

\displaystyle=0.

The completions
\widehat{H}
and
\widehat{F}
are of course not unrelated. By comparing (
257
) with (
262
) and using (
260
), we find

\widehat{F}(\tau)=\frac{1}{24}\left(\widehat{H}(\tau)+2\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}\right).

(265)

This is precisely the modular completion of (
260
). Since the correction

r(\tau)=2\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}

(266)

is already modular, its modular completion vanishes. It is in fact a modular form for
\Gamma^{0}(2)
and transforms as

\displaystyle r(\tau+2)

\displaystyle=-i\,r(\tau),

(267)

\displaystyle r(\tfrac{\tau}{\tau+1})

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}}\sqrt{\tau+1}\,r(\tau).

Another mock modular form with shadow
\eta^{3}
is the function
Q^{+}
introduced by Malmendier and Ono
Malmendier:2012zz
;
Malmendier:2008db
,

\displaystyle Q^{+}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12}H(\tau)+\frac{7}{6}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}

(268)

\displaystyle=\frac{3\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+2\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{2\eta(\tau)^{3}}-\frac{2}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{8}}}{1+q^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=q^{-\frac{1}{8}}(1+28q^{\frac{1}{2}}+39q+196q^{\frac{3}{2}}+161q^{2}+756q^{\frac{5}{2}}+\dots).

Its non-holomorphic completion

\widehat{Q^{+}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=Q^{+}(\tau)-i\int_{-\bar{\tau}}^{i\infty}\frac{\eta(w)^{3}}{\sqrt{-i(w+\tau)}}\mathrm{d}w

(269)

evaluates to

\displaystyle\widehat{Q^{+}}(i,\bar{i})

\displaystyle=7\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(270)

\displaystyle\widehat{Q^{+}}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\frac{7\sqrt[3]{2}}{\sqrt{3}}\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\widehat{Q^{+}}(1+i,\overline{1+i})

\displaystyle=0,

where
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}
and
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}
are the Chowla–Selberg periods (
241
), which we introduced in Subsection
D.1
.
It is related to
F
by

Q^{+}(\tau)=2F(\tau)+\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}.

(271)

Laurent series of
H

The Chowla–Selberg formula (Proposition
4
) seems to be valid for the three completions
\widehat{F}
,
\widehat{H}
and
\widehat{Q^{+}}
of mock modular forms of weight
\frac{1}{2}
. See equations (
227
), (
228
) and (
270
).
We can also use it to compute non-holomorphic Laurent/Taylor series around special points. Regarding the
u
-plane integrand, we are interested in expansions

f(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\sum_{m\gg-\infty,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!n!}d_{\tau_{0}}^{f}(m,n)\,(\tau-\tau_{0})^{m}\,(\bar{\tau}-\bar{\tau}_{0})^{n}

(272)

around some point
(\tau_{0},\bar{\tau}_{0})
.
It is clear that if
f
does not have poles in
\mathbb{H}
, then the series is only over
m\geq 0
. Furthermore, the Taylor coefficients are given by

d_{\tau_{0}}^{f}(m,n)=\frac{\partial^{m+n}f(\tau,\bar{\tau})}{\partial\tau^{m}\partial\bar{\tau}^{n}}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\,\,\tau=\tau_{0}\\
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}_{0}\end{subarray}}.

(273)

For
\widehat{H}
, we can compute some of these coefficients, which we give in Table
16
.

0

1

2

0

0

-6\sqrt{2}i

-6\sqrt{2}

1

\sim 6.22669i

\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}

-\frac{9i}{2\sqrt{2}}

2

\sim-9.34004

\frac{9i}{4\sqrt{2}}

\frac{9}{2\sqrt{2}}

Table 16
:
The coefficients
d_{i}^{\widehat{H}}(m,n)\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}^{-\frac{3}{2}}
of the completion
\widehat{H}
. The coefficients for
m\geq 1
and
n=0
do not seem to be algebraic.

For some coefficients, we find closed formulas,

\displaystyle d_{i}^{\widehat{H}}(m,1)

\displaystyle=-3i^{m+1}2^{\frac{3}{2}-2m}(2m-1)!!\,\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}^{\frac{3}{2}},

(274)

\displaystyle d_{i}^{\widehat{H}}(m,2)

\displaystyle=-3(-i)^{-m}2^{\frac{1}{2}-2m}(2m-1)!!(m+2)\,\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}^{\frac{3}{2}},

where
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}
is the Chowla–Selberg period (
226
), and
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
This is possible due to the fact that
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{H}
is proportional to
\overline{\eta(\tau)}^{3}/\sqrt{y}
, whose derivatives are straightforward to compute.

Similarly, for the expansion around
(\tau,\bar{\tau})=(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})
we find

\displaystyle d_{\alpha}^{\widehat{H}}(m,1)

\displaystyle=2^{2-m}3^{\frac{3}{4}-\frac{m}{2}}e^{\frac{1}{8}i\pi(4m-3)}(2m-1)!!\,\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}^{\frac{3}{2}},

(275)

\displaystyle d_{\alpha}^{\widehat{H}}(m,2)

\displaystyle=2^{2-m}3^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{m}{2}}e^{\frac{1}{8}i\pi(4m+9)}(2m-1)!!(m+2)\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)}^{\frac{3}{2}}.

The coefficients for
n=1
and
n=2
for both
i
and
\alpha
are simply related by

\displaystyle d_{i}^{\widehat{H}}(m,2)

\displaystyle=\,\,-\frac{i}{2}\,\,(m+2)d_{i}^{\widehat{H}}(m,1),

(276)

\displaystyle d_{\alpha}^{\widehat{H}}(m,2)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}(m+2)d_{\alpha}^{\widehat{H}}(m,1).

From Table
16
it is clear that the smallest value of
m
in the expansion (
272
) of
\widehat{H}
at
\tau=i
is
m=1
. At
\tau=\alpha
, the same is true, as is clear from the nonzero constant
d_{\alpha}^{\widehat{H}}(1,1)
in (

). However, we have

\displaystyle d_{\alpha}^{\widehat{H}}(0,0)

\displaystyle=0

(277)

\displaystyle d_{\alpha}^{\widehat{H}}(1,0)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle d_{\alpha}^{\widehat{H}}(2,0)

\displaystyle\sim 22.8072\,e^{\frac{\pi i}{8}}.

D.4
Mock Jacobi forms

We can generalise the function
H=H^{(2)}
to include an elliptic argument. For this, we need to find a suitable elliptic generalisation of the holomorphic modular form (
266
) which is the difference of
\widehat{F}
and
\widehat{H}
. Recall the elliptic generalisation

F(\tau,\rho)=-\frac{w^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}q^{n^{2}/2-\frac{1}{8}}}{1-wq^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}.

(278)

We define the dual modular form

r_{D}(\tau)\coloneqq-(-i\tau)^{-\frac{1}{2}}r(-1/\tau)=-\frac{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}.

(279)

Then using the Jacobi identity (
), we have

r(\tau)-r_{D}(\tau)=6\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{3}}.

(280)

The difference between
\widehat{F}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
and
\widehat{F}_{D}(\tau,\bar{\tau})
is a weakly holomorphic modular form of weight
\frac{1}{2}
, since
\widehat{F}
and
\widehat{F}_{D}
have the same non-holomorphic part. This can be seen from (
265
) and the fact that
\widehat{H}
is
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
-invariant. This gives

\widehat{F}(\tau,\bar{\tau})-\widehat{F}_{D}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{4\eta(\tau)^{3}}=\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{3}}{2\vartheta_{2}(\tau)\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}.

(281)

Including the elliptic variables, we find

\widehat{F}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})-\widehat{F}_{D}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})=\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{2}\vartheta_{3}(\tau,\rho)}{2\vartheta_{2}(\tau,\rho)\vartheta_{4}(\tau,\rho)}.

(282)

This again follows from the fact that both
\widehat{F}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})
and
F_{D}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})
have the same non-holomorphic part, and
F(\tau,\rho)-F_{D}(\tau,\rho)
must transform as a Jacobi form of weight
\frac{1}{2}
and for
\rho=0
give back (
281
). The holomorphic functions
F(\tau,\rho)
and
F_{D}(\tau,\rho)
are given in (
81
) and (
82
).
The rhs of (
282
) is a specialisation of the Appell–Lerch sum
M(\tau,u,v)
(
253
), which is another way to find it.

We are thus aiming for a function
r(\tau,\rho)
such that

r(\tau,\rho)-r_{D}(\tau,\rho)=12\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{2}\vartheta_{3}(\tau,\rho)}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau,\rho)\vartheta_{4}(\tau,\rho)}

(283)

and such that
r(\tau,0)
agrees with (
266
). This function is given by elliptically extending the arguments of some of the Jacobi theta functions of
r(\tau)
,

r(\tau,\rho)=4\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{2}\vartheta_{2}(\tau,\rho)^{2}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{2}\vartheta_{3}(\tau,\rho)^{2}}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau,\rho)\vartheta_{3}(\tau,\rho)\vartheta_{4}(\tau,\rho)}.

(284)

A short calculation using the elliptic
S
-transformation (
248
) and the elliptic abstruse identity (
252
) confirms this. Then we know that

\widehat{H}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})=24\widehat{F}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})-r(\tau,\rho),

(285)

where the holomorphic part reads

\displaystyle H(\tau,\rho)=

\displaystyle 24\,F(\tau,\rho)-4\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{2}\vartheta_{2}(\tau,\rho)^{2}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{2}\vartheta_{3}(\tau,\rho)^{2}}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau,\rho)\vartheta_{3}(\tau,\rho)\vartheta_{4}(\tau,\rho)}

(286)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4\,w^{1/2}}{(1+w)}q^{-1/8}+\frac{4(4+11w+15w^{2}+11w^{3}+4w^{4})}{w^{3/2}(1+w)}q^{7/8}+\mathcal{O}(q^{15/8}),

with
w=e^{2\pi i\rho}
. As far as checked, the
w
-dependent higher order terms in the
q
-expansion can be expressed as positive linear combinations of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
characters. In particular, the polynomials are palindromic. Their coefficients appear to asymptote to those of the series

\displaystyle(1+w)\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1+w^{k}}{1-w^{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+w)(-1;w)_{\infty}}{2(w;w)_{\infty}}

(287)

\displaystyle=1+3w+6w^{2}+12w^{3}+22w^{4}+38w^{5}+64w^{6}+\mathcal{O}(w^{7}).

It would be interesting to explore if the coefficients of this refined generating function also have an interpretation within Mathieu Moonshine. The analysis in
MARGOLIN1993370
;
Taormina:2013mda
;
Taormina:2013jza
could be a possible avenue for this direction.

We have the following quasi-periodicity properties of the three elliptic functions
F
,
H
and
r
,

\displaystyle F(\tau,\rho+1)

\displaystyle=-F(\tau,\rho),

(288)

\displaystyle H(\tau,\rho+1)

\displaystyle=-H(\tau,\rho),

\displaystyle r(\tau,\rho+1)

\displaystyle=-r(\tau,\rho),

\displaystyle F(\tau,\rho+\tau)

\displaystyle=-e^{\pi i\tau}e^{2\pi i\rho}F(\tau,\rho)-e^{\frac{3}{4}\pi i\tau}e^{\pi i\rho},

\displaystyle H(\tau,\rho+\tau)

\displaystyle=-e^{\pi i\tau}e^{2\pi i\rho}H(\tau,\rho)-24\,e^{\frac{3}{4}\pi i\tau}e^{\pi i\rho},

\displaystyle r(\tau,\rho+\tau)

\displaystyle=-e^{\pi i\tau}e^{2\pi i\rho}r(\tau,\rho).

The mock Jacobi forms
F
and
H
are symmetric in
\rho
, which gives a palindromic property of their Fourier coefficients,

\displaystyle F(\tau,-\rho)

\displaystyle=F(\tau,\rho),

(289)

\displaystyle H(\tau,-\rho)

\displaystyle=H(\tau,\rho).

Poles of
F
and
H

Since the functions
F
and
H
are quasi-periodic on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\tau\mathbb{Z}
, it is sufficient to study its poles in the fundamental domain of the torus
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}\oplus\tau\mathbb{Z}
.

The function
H(\tau,\rho)
has (possible) poles at

\rho=\frac{m}{2}+\frac{n}{2}\tau,\quad m,n\in\mathbb{Z},\quad(m,n)\neq(0,0),

(290)

which come from the zeros (
251
) of the Jacobi theta functions in the denominator of
r(\tau,\rho)
(here, we can already exclude the cases where both
m
and
n
are even). Meanwhile,
F(\tau,\rho)
has poles at
\rho=(n+\tfrac{1}{2})\tau
, which follows from the definition (
278
). Restricting to the fundamental domain, the complete pole structure of both
F
and
H
are then
\rho\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},\frac{\tau}{2},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\}
.
Let us study which of these elliptic values are poles of
r
,
F
and
H
.

The zeros of the Jacobi theta functions
\vartheta_{i}
are listed in (
251
). By construction, the functions
r
,
F
and
H
have a smooth limit
\rho\to 0
and reduce to holomorphic functions with the same symbol. Thus by (
288
), there are no poles on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\tau\mathbb{Z}
.

The potential poles of
r
,
F
and
H
are thus on the half-lattice points.
Consider first
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
.
We find

F(\tau,\rho)=-2\pi m(\tau)(\rho-\tfrac{1}{2})+\mathcal{O}((\rho-\tfrac{1}{2})^{3}),

(291)

with a nonzero holomorphic function
m
.

Out of the four Jacobi theta functions, only
\vartheta_{2}
has a zero at
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
. We easily calculate the Taylor expansions,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=-2\pi\eta(\tau)^{3}(\rho-\tfrac{1}{2})+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\tfrac{1}{2})^{3}\right),

(292)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{4}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{3}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}\right).

From (
284
) we then find

r(\tau,\rho)=-\frac{4}{\pi\vartheta_{2}(\tau)}(\rho-\tfrac{1}{2})^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\tfrac{1}{2})^{1}\right).

(293)

Combining with (
286
) and (
291
) we thus find that
H(\tau,\rho)
has a simple pole at
\rho=\tfrac{1}{2}
with residue

\underset{\rho=\frac{1}{2}}{\text{Res}}\,H(\tau,\rho)=\frac{4}{\pi\vartheta_{2}(\tau)}.

(294)

At
\rho=\tau
, both
F(\tau,\tau)
and
H(\tau,\tau)
are regular. At
\rho=\frac{\tau}{2}
,
F
,
r
and
H
have a pole. We find

\displaystyle F(\tau,\rho)=\frac{q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{2\pi i\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}(\rho-\tfrac{\tau}{2})^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\tfrac{\tau}{2})^{0}\right)

(295)

Regarding
r(\tau,\rho)
, we calculate

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=q^{-\frac{1}{8}}\vartheta_{3}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\tfrac{\tau}{2})^{1}\right),

(296)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=q^{-\frac{1}{8}}\vartheta_{2}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\tfrac{\tau}{2})^{1}\right),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=2\pi iq^{-\frac{1}{8}}\eta(\tau)^{3}(\rho-\tfrac{\tau}{2})+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\tfrac{\tau}{2})^{2}\right).

This gives

r(\tau,\rho)=-\frac{8iq^{\frac{1}{8}}}{\pi\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}(\rho-\tfrac{\tau}{2})^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(1).

(297)

Combining (
295
) and (
297
), we find

\underset{\rho=\frac{\tau}{2}}{\text{Res}}\,H(\tau,\rho)=\frac{4q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{\pi i\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}.

(298)

Finally, consider
\rho_{0}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}
. We find

F(\tau,\rho_{0})=\frac{q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{2i}.

(299)

The Jacobi theta functions have leading terms

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=-iq^{-\frac{1}{8}}\vartheta_{4}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\rho_{0})^{1}\right),

(300)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=2\pi iq^{-\frac{1}{8}}\eta(\tau)^{3}(\rho-\rho_{0})+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\rho_{0})^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=q^{-\frac{1}{8}}\vartheta_{2}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\rho_{0})^{1}\right).

This gives

r(\tau,\rho)=-\frac{4q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{\pi\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}(\rho-\rho_{0})^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(1),

(301)

and therefore

\underset{\rho=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}}{\text{Res}}\,H(\tau,\rho)=\frac{4q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{\pi\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}.

(302)

In Table
17
we summarise the residues of
r
,
F
and
H
at all possible poles of these functions.
In particular, the poles of
H
are

\displaystyle\underset{\rho\in\mathbb{C}}{\text{poles}}\,H(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle=\{\tfrac{m}{2}+\tfrac{n}{2}\tau\}\,\backslash\,\mathbb{Z}\oplus\tau\mathbb{Z}

(303)

\displaystyle=\{\tfrac{m}{2}+\tfrac{n}{2}\tau\,|\,(\tfrac{m}{2},\tfrac{n}{2})\not\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}.

\rho_{0}

\underset{\rho=\rho_{0}}{\text{Res}}\,F(\tau,\rho)

\underset{\rho=\rho_{0}}{\text{Res}}\,H(\tau,\rho)

\underset{\rho=\rho_{0}}{\text{Res}}\,r(\tau,\rho)

\displaystyle 0

0

0

0

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

0

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\pi\vartheta_{2}(\tau)}

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{\pi\vartheta_{2}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\frac{\tau}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{2\pi i\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\frac{4q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{\pi i\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\frac{8q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{\pi i\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}

0

\displaystyle\frac{4q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{\pi\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}

\displaystyle-\frac{4q^{\frac{1}{8}}}{\pi\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}

Table 17
:
The pole structure of the functions
F
(
278
),
H
(
286
) and
r
(
284
) including residues at the half-lattice points.

Given the precise pole structure of
H
, we can study functions related to
H
which have no poles for real
\rho
. To this end, we can add to
H(\tau,\rho)
a meromorphic Jacobi form which cancels those poles of
H(\tau,\rho)
.
38
38

38

See
Dabholkar:2012nd
for a systematic study of poles of Jacobi forms.
In this way, we arrive at

\widetilde{H}(\tau,\rho)=H(\tau,\rho)-\frac{8\eta(\tau)^{3}}{\theta_{1}(\tau,\rho)^{2}}\sum_{j=2,3,4}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}(\tau,2\rho)}{\theta_{j}(\tau,\rho)}-\frac{\theta_{j}(\tau,\rho)^{3}}{\theta_{j}(\tau)^{3}}\right),

(304)

which does not have a pole at either
\rho=0
or
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
. Indeed,
\widetilde{H}(\tau,0)=H(\tau)
and the Taylor series at
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
reads

\widetilde{H}(\tau,\rho)=4\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{3}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{2}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{2}}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{2}\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left((\rho-\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}\right).

(305)

While
\widetilde{H}
does not have poles at
\rho=0
or
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
, it has poles at
\rho=\frac{\tau}{2}
and
\rho=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}
.
\widetilde{H}
is periodic under
\rho\to\rho+2
. The first few terms of the
q
-expansion of
\widetilde{H}
are

\begin{split}&\widetilde{H}(\tau,\rho)=q^{-1/8}(-w^{1/2}-w^{-1/2})\\
&\quad+(-16w^{2}+19w^{3/2}-64w+26w^{1/2}+160+\text{palindromic terms})\,q^{7/8}+\dots.\end{split}

(306)

Similarly to the refinement
H(\tau,\rho)
of
H(\tau)
, it is an interesting question whether this refinement
\widetilde{H}(\tau,\rho)
has an interpretation in the context of Mathieu moonshine.

Appendix E
Mass expansions

In this Appendix, we list various expansions of Coulomb branch functions required for the computation of topological correlation functions. In Subsection
E.1
, we comment on the calculation of the couplings
v_{j}
and
w_{jk}
to the background fluxes. In Appendix
E.2
, we give explicit series of the order parameter and the period
\frac{du}{da}
in a large mass expansion, which are relevant particularly in Section
11.1
.
Due to the modular structure, the equal mass
N_{f}=2
theory is the simplest massive case where many results can be obtained exactly. We study this configuration in detail in subsection
E.3
.

E.1
Couplings to background fluxes

The background couplings
v_{j}
and
w_{jk}
, defined in (
), can be calculated either directly from the prepotential
Ohta_1997
;
Ohta:1996hq
, or using the hypergeometric representation of the periods. For the latter case, from
Masuda:1996xj
;
Brandhuber:1996ng
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial a}{\partial u}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\cdot 3^{1/4}g_{2}^{1/4}}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{1}{12},\frac{5}{12},1;\frac{12^{3}}{\mathcal{J}}\right],

(307)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial a_{D}}{\partial u}=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2\cdot 3^{1/4}g_{2}^{1/4}}\left(3\log(12)\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{1}{12},\frac{5}{12},1;\frac{12^{3}}{\mathcal{J}}\right]-F^{*}\left[\frac{1}{12},\frac{5}{12},1;\frac{12^{3}}{\mathcal{J}}\right]\right),

where

F^{*}\left[\alpha,\beta,1;z\right]={}_{2}F_{1}\left[\alpha,\beta,1;z\right]\log z+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\alpha)_{n}(\beta)_{n}}{(n!)^{2}}z^{n}\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\alpha+r}+\frac{1}{\beta+r}-\frac{2}{r+1}.

(308)

Here,
\mathcal{J}=12^{3}g_{2}^{3}/(g_{2}^{3}-27g_{3}^{2})
is the
\mathcal{J}
-invariant of the curve.

The periods can be in principle expanded around any point on the Coulomb branch, and by integration and comparison with the prepotential (
) they can be found by differentiation with respect to the masses
m_{j}
. In practise, for generic masses this is straightforward only for the weak coupling limit (
).
In the following, we give explicit expressions at weak coupling for
N_{f}=1
and
N_{f}=2
.

\bm{N_{f}=1}

For
N_{f}=1
, we find in this way the couplings
v
and
C
,

\displaystyle v=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4m}{\pi\Lambda_{1}}q^{\frac{1}{6}}+\frac{8\left(\Lambda_{1}^{3}-4m^{3}\right)}{\pi\Lambda_{1}^{3}}q^{\frac{3}{6}}+\frac{64\left(374m^{5}-135\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{2}\right)}{45\pi\Lambda_{1}^{5}}q^{\frac{5}{6}}-\frac{64\left(98800m^{7}-49140\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{4}+3969\Lambda_{1}^{6}m\right)}{567\left(\pi\Lambda_{1}^{7}\right)}q^{\frac{7}{6}}

(309)

\displaystyle+\frac{64\left(-17\Lambda_{1}^{9}+12320m^{9}-7840\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{6}+1188\Lambda_{1}^{6}m^{3}\right)}{3\pi\Lambda_{1}^{9}}q^{\frac{9}{6}}

\displaystyle-\frac{4096\left(m^{2}\left(-120285\Lambda_{1}^{9}+13017172m^{9}-10103049\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{6}+2241756\Lambda_{1}^{6}m^{3}\right)\right)}{8019\left(\pi\Lambda_{1}^{11}\right)}q^{\frac{11}{6}}

\displaystyle+\frac{8m\left(824527431\Lambda_{1}^{12}+1880795176960m^{12}-1723243991040\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{9}+503472153600\Lambda_{1}^{6}m^{6}-48655676160\Lambda_{1}^{9}m^{3}\right)}{85293\pi\Lambda_{1}^{13}}q^{\frac{13}{6}}

\displaystyle+\frac{16\left(12393\Lambda_{1}^{15}-1513521152m^{15}+1599037440\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{12}-579532800\Lambda_{1}^{6}m^{9}+81786880\Lambda_{1}^{9}m^{6}-3533220\Lambda_{1}^{12}m^{3}\right)}{5\pi\Lambda_{1}^{15}}q^{\frac{15}{6}}+\mathcal{O}(q^{\frac{17}{6}}),

\displaystyle C=

\displaystyle-4q^{\frac{1}{6}}+\frac{128m^{2}}{3\Lambda_{1}^{2}}q^{\frac{3}{6}}+\frac{128\left(9\Lambda_{1}^{3}m-56m^{4}\right)}{9\Lambda_{1}^{4}}q^{\frac{5}{6}}+\frac{16\left(405\Lambda_{1}^{6}+90112m^{6}-27648\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{3}\right)}{81\Lambda_{1}^{6}}q^{\frac{7}{6}}

\displaystyle-\frac{4096\left(320m^{8}-144\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{5}+11\Lambda_{1}^{6}m^{2}\right)}{3\Lambda_{1}^{8}}q^{\frac{9}{6}}+\frac{512m\left(-26973\Lambda_{1}^{9}+16187392m^{9}-9584640\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{6}+1370520\Lambda_{1}^{6}m^{3}\right)}{729\Lambda_{1}^{10}}q^{\frac{11}{6}}

\displaystyle-\frac{16\left(12892365\Lambda_{1}^{12}+125513498624m^{12}-92649553920\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{9}+19568812032\Lambda_{1}^{6}m^{6}-707678208\text{$\Lambda$1}^{9}m^{3}\right)}{6561\Lambda_{1}^{12}}q^{\frac{13}{6}}

\displaystyle+\frac{256m^{2}\left(204435\Lambda_{1}^{12}+2078277632m^{12}-1849688064\Lambda_{1}^{3}m^{9}+518246400\text{$\Lambda$1}^{6}m^{6}-35288064\Lambda_{1}^{9}m^{3}\right)}{63\Lambda_{1}^{14}}q^{\frac{15}{6}}+\mathcal{O}(q^{\frac{17}{6}}),

which we list to order
\mathcal{O}(q^{\frac{5}{2}})
.
We have determined them up to order
\mathcal{O}(q^{4})
.

Relating the
q
-expansion of
v
to that of the period
\frac{da}{du}
, we can compare the normalisation in the weak coupling limit,

v-\frac{1}{2}\sim\frac{\sqrt{8}}{\pi i}m\frac{da}{du},

(310)

as
\tau\to i\infty
.

One can similarly make a small mass expansion. For
C
, we find

\begin{split}C&=-4\,q^{1/6}(1-20\,q+2250\,q^{2}+\mathcal{O}(q^{3})\\
&\quad+128\,q^{5/6}(1-148\,q+27826\,q^{2}+\mathcal{O}(q^{3}))\frac{m}{\Lambda_{1}}+\mathcal{O}(m^{2}).\end{split}

(311)

For the small mass expansion of
e^{2\pi iv_{1}}
, we have

\begin{split}e^{2\pi iv_{1}}&=-1-16i\,q^{1/2}+128\,q+1408i\,q^{3/2}+\mathcal{O}(q^{2})\\
&\quad-8i\,q^{1/6}(1+46i\,q^{1/2}-240\,q-3200i\,q^{3/2}+\mathcal{O}(q^{2}))\frac{m}{\Lambda_{1}}+\mathcal{O}(m^{2}).\end{split}

(312)

We note that the small mass expansion commutes with the small
q
expansion in (
309
).

As observed in
Gottsche:2006
, the coupling
v
can also be expressed in terms of
\frac{du}{da}
and a power series in
u
. Since under the large
u
monodromy,
v
transforms to
-v+1
, we can rather consider
v-1
, which transforms to
-(v-1)

Aspman:2022sfj
39
39

39

We can confirm this directly from the
q
-series. It is also important to notice that at strong coupling,
v
rather than
v-1
transforms modularly
Aspman:2022sfj
.
. This is also true for
\frac{du}{da}\to-\frac{du}{da}
, which has modular weight
-1
as well. This suggests that the quotient of
v-1
and
\frac{du}{da}
is invariant under any monodromy, and thus a function of
u
. A positive power series in
u
can be excluded, as it would generate an arbitrarily large principal part. For
N_{f}=1
we can make the ansatz

v-\frac{1}{2}=-\frac{1}{16\sqrt{2}\pi i\Lambda_{1}}\frac{du}{da}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}{u}\right)^{n},

(313)

where the coefficients
a_{n}
depend on the mass
\mu=m/\Lambda_{1}
. The first few coefficients are
a_{0}=0
,
a_{1}=8\mu
,
a_{2}=\frac{8\mu^{3}}{3}-1
,

a_{3}=\frac{8\mu^{5}}{5}+2\mu^{2}
,
a_{4}=\frac{8\mu^{7}}{7}+\mu^{4}-\frac{3\mu}{4}
etc. In the massless case, we have the series (
\Lambda_{1}=1
)

-u^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{u^{n}}=1-\frac{1}{24u^{3}}+\frac{7}{2560u^{6}}-\frac{3}{14336u^{9}}+\mathcal{O}(u^{-12}).

(314)

It would be interesting to express this series as a hypergeometric function, such as the periods
\frac{da}{du}
as given in (
307
).
See also
Poghosyan:2023zvy
;
Masuda:1996xj
;
suzuki2
for related formulae.

\bm{N_{f}=2}

If we keep the masses in
N_{f}=2
distinct, we find

v^{1}=-\frac{n_{1}}{2}-\frac{8im_{1}}{\pi\Lambda_{2}}\left(q^{1/4}+\left(\frac{16}{3\Lambda_{2}^{2}}(7m_{1}^{2}+3m_{2}^{2})-\frac{8m_{2}}{m_{1}}\right)q^{3/4}+\mathcal{O}(q^{5/4})\right).

(315)

In the equal mass case, the couplings
v_{1}=v_{2}=v
, and in particular the couplings
C_{jk}
can be found exactly. See the series (
321
) and (
322
) in Section
E.3
.

E.2

In this Appendix, we list expansions of Coulomb branch functions in the large mass limit. We consider
N_{f}
hypermultiplets with equal masses
m_{i}=m
,
i=1,\dots,N_{f}
.
When
m
is large, the scales
\Lambda_{N_{f}}
and
\Lambda_{0}
are related as

\Lambda_{0}^{4}=m^{N_{f}}\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{4-N_{f}}.

(316)

The order parameters
u_{N_{f}}
decouple precisely
u_{N_{f}}\to u_{0}

(317)

and iteratively find

by satisfying the relation
\mathcal{J}_{N_{f}}(u_{N_{f}})=\mathcal{J}_{0}(u_{0})
order by order in
m^{-2}
. The fact that there are only even powers on
m
follows by direct inspection. We list the coefficient functions

\displaystyle-\frac{u\left(23\Lambda_{0}^{4}+6u^{2}\right)}{2048}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{128}u\left(u^{2}-2\Lambda_{0}^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{128}u\left(u^{2}-2\Lambda_{0}^{4}\right)

\displaystyle 4

\displaystyle\frac{-123\Lambda_{0}^{8}+140u^{4}-876\Lambda_{0}^{4}u^{2}}{131072}

\displaystyle\frac{-12\Lambda_{0}^{8}-5u^{4}+12\Lambda_{0}^{4}u^{2}}{2048}

\displaystyle\frac{-12\Lambda_{0}^{8}-5u^{4}+12\Lambda_{0}^{4}u^{2}}{2048}

\displaystyle 5

\displaystyle\frac{3u\left(-599\Lambda_{0}^{8}+364u^{4}-908\Lambda_{0}^{4}u^{2}\right)}{1048576}

\displaystyle\frac{-7u^{5}+20\Lambda_{0}^{4}u^{3}-20\Lambda_{0}^{8}u}{8192}

\displaystyle\frac{-7u^{5}+20\Lambda_{0}^{4}u^{3}-20\Lambda_{0}^{8}u}{8192}

Table 21
:
Coefficient functions
d_{n}^{N_{f}}
of the large
m
expansion (
318
) of
\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{N_{f}}
for
N_{f}=1,2,3
.

E.3
Equal mass
N_{f}=2

For the case of
N_{f}=2
with equal masses, we can give explicit closed-form expressions for many Coulomb branch functions. For instance, we have
Aspman:2021vhs
(see footnote
14
)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{8}\frac{\vartheta_{4}^{8}+\vartheta_{2}^{4}\vartheta_{3}^{4}+(\vartheta_{2}^{4}+\vartheta_{3}^{4})\sqrt{f_{2}}}{\vartheta_{2}^{4}\vartheta_{3}^{4}},

(319)

\displaystyle\frac{du}{da}

\displaystyle=-i\Lambda_{2}\frac{\sqrt{\vartheta_{2}^{4}+\vartheta_{3}^{4}+\sqrt{f_{2}}}}{\vartheta_{2}^{2}\vartheta_{3}^{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{du}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=\pi i\Lambda_{2}^{2}\vartheta_{4}^{8}\frac{2(4\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}+1)\vartheta_{2}^{4}\vartheta_{3}^{4}+\vartheta_{4}^{8}+(\vartheta_{2}^{4}+\vartheta_{3}^{4})\sqrt{f_{2}}}{8\vartheta_{2}^{4}\vartheta_{3}^{4}\sqrt{f_{2}}},

\displaystyle\frac{da}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi\Lambda_{2}}{8}\frac{\vartheta_{4}^{8}}{\vartheta_{2}^{2}\vartheta_{3}^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{f_{2}}(\vartheta_{2}^{4}+\vartheta_{3}^{4})+\vartheta_{4}^{8}+2\vartheta_{2}^{4}\vartheta_{3}^{4}(1+4\tfrac{m^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}})}{\sqrt{f_{2}(\sqrt{f_{2}}+\vartheta_{2}^{4}+\vartheta_{3}^{4})}},

where we defined
f_{2}=16\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}\vartheta_{2}^{4}\vartheta_{3}^{4}+\vartheta_{4}^{8}
and we suppress the dependence on
\tau
. The physical discriminant is

\Delta=(u-u_{1}^{*})(u-u_{2}^{*})(u-u_{3}^{*})^{2},

(320)

where
(u_{1}^{*},u_{2}^{*})=(u_{-},u_{+})
with

u_{\pm}=\tfrac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{8}\pm m\Lambda_{2}
, and
u_{3}^{*}=u_{*}=m^{2}+\tfrac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{8}
. Using the solution for
u
above we can easily express also the discriminant in terms of theta functions.

The fundamental domain was found in
Aspman:2021vhs
and is shown in Fig.
. Due to the square roots there will also be branch points present, the paths of these as we vary the mass are also indicated in the Figures.

In the equal mass case, the couplings
v^{j}
to the background
\text{Spin}^{c}
-structures are equal,
v^{1}=v^{2}\eqqcolon v
. Using the hypergeometric expressions for the periods and fixing the magnetic winding number
n=-1
, it is given by

\footnotesize v=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{8}{\pi}\frac{m}{\Lambda_{2}}\left(q^{1/4}+\left(8-\frac{160}{3}\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}\right)q^{3/4}+\left(42-1536\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}+\frac{32256}{5}\frac{m^{4}}{\Lambda_{2}^{4}}\right)q^{5/4}+\mathcal{O}(q^{7/4})\right).

(321)

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle C_{11}=

\displaystyle C_{22}=e^{-\pi iw^{11}}=i\sqrt{2}\frac{\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v)^{2}}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau,v)\vartheta_{4}(\tau,0)}=4i\sqrt{2}\left(q^{1/4}-64\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}q^{3/4}+\mathcal{O}(q^{5/4})\right),

(322)

\displaystyle C_{12}=

\displaystyle e^{-\pi iw^{12}}=\frac{\vartheta_{4}(\tau,v)}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau,0)}=1+4q^{1/2}+\left(8-256\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}\right)q+\mathcal{O}(q^{3/2}).

We can see that, even though
v\to\tfrac{1}{2}
when
m\to 0
, the couplings
C_{ij}
remain dependent on
\tau
. In the massless limit, we find

\displaystyle C_{11}(\tau)\to i\sqrt{2}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{2}}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)\vartheta_{4}(\tau)},

(323)

\displaystyle C_{12}(\tau)\to\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)},

where we denote
\vartheta_{j}(\tau)=\vartheta_{j}(\tau,0)
as before.

In the decoupling limit to the pure theory,
m\to\infty
,
\Lambda_{2}\to 0
, we expect
v\to 0
and
C_{jk}\to 1
. We see that this agrees with the behaviour of
C_{12}
(since then
\vartheta_{4}(\tau,0)=\vartheta_{4}(\tau,0)
), but not for
C_{11}
, where we instead find zero.

Another important observation is that we have zeros and poles in
C_{ij}
for special values of
v
. Namely, we have a double zero for
C_{11}
when
v=l_{1}+l_{2}\tau
and a pole when
v=l_{1}+l_{2}\tau+\tfrac{\tau}{2}
, where
l_{j}
are any integers. The value
v=l_{1}+l_{2}\tau+\tfrac{\tau}{2}
also corresponds to a simple zero of
C_{12}
. We thus want to exclude these values from the domain of
v
. The pole structure of
C_{ij}
might be important for finding the correct modular completion as discussed in Appendix
F
. A similar story is occurs for the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory
Manschot:2021qqe
, where a stronger claim can be made due to a certain relationship on theta functions involving
v
.

Behaviour near
I_{2}
singularity

We finish this Appendix with some details of the behaviour near the
I_{2}
singularities. We can also determine the behaviour of the couplings near other singularities using (
319
). For
da/du
near the singularity
u_{3}^{*}
, we obtain for example

\begin{split}&\left(\frac{da}{du}\right)_{3}(\tau_{3})=\\
&\qquad\frac{i}{\Lambda_{2}}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{3})^{2}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{3})^{2}}{(\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{3})^{4}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{3})^{4}+(\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{3})^{8}-16(m^{2}/\Lambda_{3}^{2})\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{3})^{4}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{3})^{4})^{1/2})^{1/2}}.\end{split}

(324)

This gives for the leading term in the
q
-series,

\left(\frac{da}{du}\right)_{3}(\tau_{3})=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\Lambda_{2}^{2}-8m^{2}}}+\mathcal{O}(q_{3}^{1/2}).

(325)

Similarly, we determine for the leading terms of
u_{3}
,

u_{3}(\tau_{3})=u_{3}^{*}+\frac{(2\Lambda_{2}^{2}-8m^{2})^{2}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}\,q_{3}^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}(q_{3}).

(326)

Using integration of Matone’s relation (
), we find for the leading term of
a_{3}
,

a_{3}(\tau_{3})=-\frac{i}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}(2\Lambda_{2}^{2}-8m^{2})^{3/2}\,q_{3}^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}(q_{3}).

(327)

Appendix F
Comments on non-vanishing background fluxes for
\mathbb{P}^{2}

In Section
11.3
, we have studied point correlators on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with non-vanishing background fluxes. Including background fluxes, for large masses we can use the mock Jacobi form
F_{\mu}(\tau,\rho)
, defined in (
81
), for any value of the background fluxes
k_{j}
. For small masses however, this function does not have the right behaviour under
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
monodromies. For
\mu=\frac{1}{2}
, we can rather use the mock Jacobi form
H(\tau,\rho)
(
286
),
and determine the
u
-plane integral in a small mass expansion,

\displaystyle\Phi_{1/2}\big[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\big]=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{N_{f}}}{2\,\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{12-2N_{f}}}{\rm Coeff}_{q^{0}}{\rm Ser}_{{\bm{m}}}\Bigg[\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{12}\frac{\eta(\tau)^{27}}{P_{N_{f}}^{\text{M}}}\prod_{i,j=1}^{N_{f}}C_{ij}^{B(k_{i},k_{j})}

(328)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{24}H(\tau,v_{1}k_{1}+\dots+v_{N_{f}}k_{N_{f}})e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\Bigg].

Alternatively, we can also calculate the series for finite masses, without expanding first around
{\bm{m}}=0
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{1/2}\big[e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\big]=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{N_{f}}}{2\,\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{12-2N_{f}}}{\rm Coeff}_{q^{0}}\Bigg[\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{12}\frac{\eta(\tau)^{27}}{P_{N_{f}}^{\text{M}}}\prod_{i,j=1}^{N_{f}}C_{ij}^{B(k_{i},k_{j})}

(329)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{24}H(\tau,v_{1}k_{1}+\dots+v_{N_{f}}k_{N_{f}})e^{2pu/\Lambda_{N_{f}}^{2}}\Bigg].

For
N_{f}=1
, we use the expansions for
C=C_{11}
and
v=v_{1}
given in (
311
) and (
312
). These expansions commute with the expansion for small
q
in (
309
).
We list the first few point correlators for small mass
m

), in Table

, while the ones for fixed mass
m
, as in Eq. (

), are listed in Table

. We find that the small mass and fixed mass calculation agree for
k_{1}
even, and is different for
k_{1}
odd. We comment on this issue below.
